\́mathrm{i}

Abstrakt:
C\́mathrm{i}lem této práce je hledán\́mathrm{i} integrabiln\́mathrm{i}ch a superintegrabiln\́mathrm{i}ch systémů cylindrického typu s magnetickým polem. Po zformulován\́mathrm{i} kvantově mechanických určuj\́mathrm{i}c\́mathrm{i}ch rovnic pro integrály pohybu druhého řádu v cylindrických souřadnic\́mathrm{i}ch jsou nalezeny všechny kvadraticky integrabiln\́mathrm{i} systémy cylindrického typu. Mezi nimi jsou hledány systémy, které připouštěj\́mathrm{i} dodatečné integrály pohybu. Nejprve jsou př\́mathrm{i}mým řešen\́mathrm{i}m určuj\́mathrm{i}c\́mathrm{i}ch rovnic nalezeny všechny systémy s dodatečným integrálem prvn\́mathrm{i}ho řádu v klasické i kvantové mechanice. Ukazuje se, že všechny tyto systémy již byly známé a žádné dalš\́mathrm{i} neexistuj\́mathrm{i}. Nalezeny jsou také všechny klasické systémy s dodatečným integrálem typu
L^{2}+\ldots
, respektive
L_{x}p_{y}-L_{y}p_{x}+\ldots
, z nichž většina zat\́mathrm{i}m nebyla publikována. Všechny nalezené superintegrabiln\́mathrm{i} systémy připouštěj\́mathrm{i} integrál prvn\́mathrm{i}ho řádu
L_{z}
a jejich Hamiltonovy-Jacobiho, respektive Schrödingerovy, rovnice jsou vyřešeny separac\́mathrm{i} proměnných v cylindrických souřadnic\́mathrm{i}ch, u systémů prvn\́mathrm{i}ho řádu i v kartézských.

Kl
\́mathrm{i}
čová slova:

integrabilita, superintegrabilita, kvantová korekce, magnetické pole, cylindrické souřadnice

Title:

Integrable and superintegrable systems of cylindrical type in magnetic fields

Author:

Bc. Ondřej Kubů

Abstract:
The goal of this thesis is the search for integrable and superintegrable systems with magnetic field. We formulate the quantum mechanical determining equations for second order integrals of motion in the cylindrical coordinates and we find all quadratically integrable systems of the cylindrical type. Among them we search for systems admitting additional integrals of motion. We find all systems with an additional first order integral both in classical and quantum mechanics. It turns out that all these systems have already been known and no other exist. We also find all systems with an additional integral of type
L^{2}+\ldots
, respectively
L_{y}p_{y}-L_{x}p_{y}+\ldots
, of which the majority is new to the literature. All found superintegrable systems admit the first order integral
L_{z}
and we solve their Hamilton-Jacobi and Schrödinger equations by separation of variables in the cylindrical coordinates, for the first order systems in the Cartesian coordinates as well.

Key words:

integrability, superintegrability, quantum correction, magnetic field, cylindrical coordinates

Introduction

This thesis is a contribution to the study of integrable and superintegrable systems with electric and magnetic fields. These are Hamiltonian systems distinguished by the existence of as many independent integrals of motion in involution as degrees of freedom (integrability) or even some additional not necessarily in involution with the rest (superintegrability) which give them extraordinary properties: in classical mechanics the equations of motion can be solved by quadratures (integrable) or even algebraically in closed form (maximally superintegrable), in quantum mechanics the energy levels are degenerate and it has been conjectured that all maximally superintegrable systems are exactly solvable
[
26
]
. These properties make them invaluable as physical models which allow us to develop insight into the principles governing physical laws. They are also the basis for constructing more complicated models, often by the method of perturbations. The prime example in this regard is the periodic table
[
23
]
, which is obtained as a perturbation to the Coulomb model.

The most well-known superintegrable systems are the Kepler-Coulomb system and the harmonic oscillator. As stated by Bertrand’s theorem
[
3
]
, see also
[
13
]
, these are the only spherically symmetrical maximally superintegrable systems without magnetic field. For analysis of these systems in quantum mechanics see e.g.
[
14
]
.

Most of the work in the field of superintegrability was done for the systems with so-called natural Hamiltonian

H=\frac{1}{2}\left(\vec{p}\right)^{2}+W(\vec{x}),

(1)

mainly with the assumption that the integrals of motion are polynomial in momenta. The best studied cases are those on the Euclidean spaces
\mathbb{E}_{2}
and
\mathbb{E}_{3}
, for which all second order superintegrable systems were found
[
28
,
12
,
10
]
. The subsequent developments include studies in higher dimensional Euclidean spaces, more general spaces, e.g. Riemannian or pseudo-Riemannian, and of course higher order integrals, see the review article
[
23
]
and references therein.

Another natural generalization, which we consider in this thesis, is the Hamiltonian for the electromagnetic field, that is with the vector potential
\vec{A}(\vec{x})
in addition to the scalar potential
W(\vec{x})
, namely

H=\frac{1}{2}\left(\vec{p}+\vec{A}(\vec{x})\right)^{2}+W(\vec{x}).

(2)

(We consider an electron in electromagnetic field, so we choose the units of measurement so that
e=-1
and
m=1
.)
The earlier work, which focused mainly on the
\mathbb{E}_{2}
case, see e.g.
[
5
,
22
,
8
]
, was followed by the
\mathbb{E}_{3}
case, see
[
30
,
20
,
17
,
18
,
4
]
and references therein.

The case with the Hamiltonian (
1
) is related to the separation of Hamilton-Jacobi equation in classical mechanics and Schrödinger equation in quantum mechanics. More specifically, in
[
28
]
it was shown that in
\mathbb{E}_{3}
there are 11 pairs of commuting integrals corresponding to 11 coordinate systems in which Hamilton-Jacobi or Schrödinger equations separate determined in
[
9
]
. Even though the correspondence between second order integrals and separation no longer holds when magnetic field is present
[
2
,
20
]
, we still talk about these 11 classes because the highest order terms have the same structure, namely lie in the enveloping algebra of the Euclidean Lie algebra.

The focus of our thesis is one of the 11 coordinate systems, namely the cylindrical with the defining transformations
x=r\cos(\phi),\ y=r\sin(\phi),\ z=Z
. If we have vanishing magnetic field and scalar potential of the form

W(\vec{x})=W_{1}(r)+\frac{1}{r^{2}}W_{2}(\phi)+W_{3}(Z),

(3)

the Hamilton-Jacobi equation separates in these coordinates. In the article
[
11
]
the authors obtained all classical integrable cases with magnetic field in this class. In this thesis we will continue this research program by studying the quantum integrable cases and determine which of the integrable cases admit additional first order independent integrals of motion, hence being superintegrable. We use Maple™
[
16
]
for the calculations and occasionally verify some results in Mathematica
®

[
29
]
.

The structure of the thesis is as follows: After reviewing definitions of integrability and superintegrability in Section
1.1
, the first chapter is focused on integrable systems in quantum mechanics. In Section
1.2
we review classical determining equations for integrals of second order and in Section
1.3
we extend them to quantum mechanics by calculating the quantum correction in the cylindrical coordinates with the focus on the first order and cylindrical integrals. In Section
1.4
we find all quadratic cylindrical integrable systems in quantum mechanics and analyse those differing from the classical case considered in
[
11
]
.

In Chapter
2
we search for additional integrals of motion to make the integrable systems superintegrable. In Section
2.1
we find all cylindrical systems with at least one additional first order integral and in Section
2.2
we consider second order integrals. Due to the computational complexity of the problem we were not able to solve the second order case in general. After analysing one system we found by chance in Subsection
2.2.1
, we restrict to a physically motivated
ansatz
and find all cylindrical systems admitting integrals of the form
L^{2}+\ldots
in Subsection
2.2.2
and
L_{x}p_{y}-L_{y}p_{x}+\ldots
in Subsection
2.2.3
. We conclude by a summary of results.

Chapter 1
Quantum integrable systems in cylindrical coordinates

In this chapter, we consider quantum integrable systems in cylindrical coordinates. In particular, we derive the determining equations in cylindrical coordinates (Section
1.3
), compare them with the classical ones from
[
11
]
, which we include in Section
1.2
for reference, and solve them for the so-called cylindrical case, i.e. integrals with the highest order term
L_{z}^{2}
or
P_{z}^{2}
(Section
1.4
).

But first we review the key notions for our thesis: integrability and superintegrability.

1.1
Integrability and superintegrability

We will use the standard definitions in the field, see e.g.
[
20
]
, which are, however, slightly different from those in the review article
[
23
]
.

We start in classical mechanics: A finite-dimensional classical Hamiltonian system in a
2n
-dimensional phase space is called integrable (or Liouville integrable) if it allows
n
integrals of motion
\{X_{0}=H,...,X_{n-1}\}
(including the Hamiltonian
H
), which are

1.

well-defined analytic functions on the phase space (possibly with exceptions of lower dimensional manifolds, such as the
z
-axis or
xy
-plane),

2.

in involution, that is pairwise Poisson commute,
\{X_{i},X_{j}\}_{\text{P.B.}}=0
, where the Poisson bracket is defined in any canonical coordinates
(q_{i},p_{i})
as

\{X_{1},X_{2}\}_{\text{P.B.}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{\partial X_{1}}{\partial q_{i}}\frac{\partial X_{2}}{\partial p_{i}}-\frac{\partial X_{2}}{\partial q_{i}}\frac{\partial X_{1}}{\partial p_{i}}\right),

(1.1)

3.

and are functionally independent, i.e. their Jacobian matrix
\left(\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial q_{j}},\frac{\partial X_{i}}{\partial p_{k}}\right)
has the maximal possible rank (here
n
) on the region where the integrals are well defined and locally analytic.

A finite dimensional Hamiltonian system is superintegrable if it is integrable with integrals
\{X_{0}=H,X_{1},...,X_{n-1}\}
and admits additional integrals of motion
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k},

1\leq k\leq n-1
so that the set
\{X_{0}=H,X_{1},\ldots,X_{n-1},Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{k}\}
is functionally independent. (Integrals
Y_{i}
need not Poisson commute with any other integral except the Hamiltonian.) We call the system minimally superintegrable if
k=1
and maximally superintegrable if
k=2n-1
. (In our case we will have
n=3
and maximal superintegrability means
k=2
, so there is no other option.)

In quantum mechanics the notions in previous definitions must be slightly modified:

1.

The integrals of motion must be well-defined operators in the enveloping algebra of the Heisenberg Lie algebra, i.e. polynomials in phase space coordinate operators
\hat{Q}_{i}
and
\hat{P}_{i}
, or convergent series in them. (Here we must work in the Cartesian coordinates because there are some problems with quantizing the momenta in other coordinate systems.)

2.

The Poisson bracket is replaced by the commutator of operators. (We proceed formally, ignoring complications arising from unbounded operators.)

3.

Functional independence is replaced by the so-called algebraic (or polynomial) independence which means that no non-trivial fully symmetrized (Jordan) polynomial of the integrals vanishes.

1.2
Classical determining equations

We are working with magnetic fields so let us review a few basic notions: The magnetic field
B
, which is defined in the Cartesian coordinates as

\vec{B}=\nabla\times\vec{A},\quad\text{i.e.}\quad B_{j}=\varepsilon_{jkl}\frac{\partial A_{l}}{\partial x_{k}},

(1.2)

where the Levi-Civita symbol
\varepsilon_{jkl}
is totally antisymmetric with
\varepsilon_{123}=1
,
is gauge invariant, i.e. does not change under transformation of the potentials

\vec{A}^{\prime}(\vec{x})=\vec{A}(\vec{x})+\nabla\chi(\vec{x}),\quad V^{\prime}(\vec{x})=V(\vec{x})

(1.3)

with an arbitrary choice of the scalar function
\chi(\vec{x})
. (We consider the time independent case only.)
For the effect of gauge transformation in quantum mechanics see
eq.

1.38
.

Because the systems we consider are gauge invariant, it is useful to write their integrals of motion in terms of the so-called covariant momenta (using units
e=-1
,
m=1
)

p_{j}^{A}=p_{j}+A_{j}.

(1.4)

Using these momenta, the commutation relations in the Cartesian coordinates read

\{p_{j}^{A},p_{k}^{A}\}_{\text{P.B.}}=\varepsilon_{jkl}B_{l},\quad\{p_{j}^{A},q_{k}\}_{\text{P.B.}}=-\delta_{jk},

(1.5)

where
\delta_{jk}
is the Kronecker delta.

To work with momenta and magnetic fields in the cylindrical coordinates
(r,\phi,Z)
defined by the transformation

x=r\cos(\phi),\quad y=r\sin(\phi),\quad z=Z,

(1.6)

it is convenient to use the formalism of differential forms introduced in
[
21
]
:
Given the structure of the canonical 1-form

\lambda=p_{x}\mathrm{d}x+p_{y}\mathrm{d}y+p_{z}\mathrm{d}z=p_{r}\mathrm{d}r+p_{\phi}\mathrm{d}\phi+p_{Z}\mathrm{d}Z,

(1.7)

we obtain the following transformation for the linear momenta

\displaystyle p_{x}=\cos(\phi)p_{r}-\frac{\sin(\phi)}{r}p_{\phi},\quad p_{y}=\sin(\phi)p_{r}+\frac{\cos(\phi)}{r}p_{\phi},\quad p_{z}=p_{Z},

(1.8)

and the components of the vector potential
A
transform the same way. (We directly associate the Cartesian vector components to the 1-form components.) This enables us to use the gauge invariant momenta from
eq.

1.4
in the cylindrical coordinates as well.

On the other hand, components of the magnetic field 2-form
B=\mathrm{d}A
are

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} B&=B^{x}(\vec{x})\,\mathrm{d}y\wedge\mathrm{d}z+B^{y}(\vec{x})\,\mathrm{d}z\wedge\mathrm{d}x+B^{z}(\vec{x})\,\mathrm{d}x\wedge\mathrm{d}y\\
&=B^{r}(r,\phi,Z)\,\mathrm{d}\phi\wedge\mathrm{d}Z+B^{\phi}(r,\phi,Z)\,\mathrm{d}Z\wedge\mathrm{d}r+B^{Z}(r,\phi,Z)\,\mathrm{d}r\wedge\mathrm{d}\phi.\end{aligned}

(1.9)

This leads to the following transformation

\displaystyle B^{x}(\vec{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{\cos(\phi)}{r}B^{r}(r,\phi,Z)-\sin(\phi)B^{\phi}(r,\phi,Z),

\displaystyle B^{y}(\vec{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sin(\phi)}{r}B^{r}(r,\phi,Z)+\cos(\phi)B^{\phi}(r,\phi,Z),

(1.10)

\displaystyle B^{z}(\vec{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}B^{Z}(r,\phi,Z),

so that the components of the magnetic field are computed in the same way as in the Cartesian coordinates, namely

B^{r}=\frac{\partial A_{Z}}{\partial\phi}-\frac{\partial A_{\phi}}{\partial Z},\quad B^{\phi}=\frac{\partial A_{r}}{\partial Z}-\frac{\partial A_{Z}}{\partial r},\quad B^{Z}=\frac{\partial A_{\phi}}{\partial r}-\frac{\partial A_{r}}{\partial\phi}.

(1.11)

Now we search for integrals of motion. In classical mechanics, we use the determining equations from
[
11
]
, which were obtained by setting the Poisson bracket
\{X,H\}_{\text{P.B.}}=0
in the cylindrical coordinates (for definition see
eq.

1.1
) with the Hamiltonian
H
written in terms of the covariant momenta from
eq.

1.4
,

H=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(p_{r}^{A}\right)^{2}+\frac{\left(p_{\phi}^{A}\right)^{2}}{r^{2}}+\left(p_{Z}^{A}\right)^{2}\right)+W(r,\phi,Z),

(1.12)

and with a general integral of motion of the second order

\displaystyle X={}

\displaystyle h^{r}\left(r,\phi,Z\right)\left(p_{r}^{A}\right)^{2}+h^{\phi}\left(r,\phi,Z\right)\left(p_{\phi}^{A}\right)^{2}+h^{Z}\left(r,\phi,Z\right)\left(p_{Z}^{A}\right)^{2}+

\displaystyle+n^{r}\left(r,\phi,Z\right)p_{\phi}^{A}p_{Z}^{A}+n^{\phi}\left(r,\phi,Z\right)p_{r}^{A}p_{Z}^{A}+n^{Z}\left(r,\phi,Z\right)p_{\phi}^{A}p_{r}^{A}+

(1.13)

\displaystyle+s^{r}\left(r,\phi,Z\right)p_{r}^{A}+s^{\phi}\left(r,\phi,Z\right)p_{\phi}^{A}+s^{Z}\left(r,\phi,Z\right)p_{Z}^{A}+m\left(r,\phi,Z\right),

where the functions
h,

n,

s,

m
are to be determined. We separate the coefficients of obtained powers of momenta
p_{r},

p_{\phi},

p_{Z}
. The determining equations obtained from the third order terms are

\displaystyle\partial_{r}h^{r}

\displaystyle=0,\quad\partial_{\phi}h^{r}=-r^{2}\partial_{r}n^{Z},\quad\partial_{Z}h^{r}=-\partial_{r}n^{\phi},

\displaystyle\partial_{r}h^{\phi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\phi}n^{Z}-\frac{2}{r^{3}}h^{r},\quad\partial_{\phi}h^{\phi}=-\frac{1}{r}n^{Z},\quad\partial_{Z}h^{\phi}=-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\phi}n^{r}-\frac{1}{r^{3}}n^{\phi},

\displaystyle\partial_{r}h^{Z}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{Z}n^{\phi},\quad\partial_{\phi}h^{Z}=-r^{2}\partial_{Z}n^{r},\quad\partial_{Z}h^{Z}=0,

(1.14)

\displaystyle\partial_{\phi}n^{\phi}

\displaystyle=-r^{2}(\partial_{Z}n^{Z}+\partial_{r}n^{r}).

We simplify the second order term equations using the third order
section

1.2
and rewrite derivatives of
\vec{A}
in terms of
\vec{B}
using
eq.

1.11
to obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{r}s^{r}

\displaystyle=n^{\phi}B^{\phi}-n^{Z}B^{Z},

\displaystyle\partial_{\phi}s^{r}

\displaystyle=r^{2}(n^{r}B^{\phi}-2h^{\phi}B^{Z}-\partial_{r}s^{\phi})-n^{\phi}B^{r}+2h^{r}B^{Z},

\displaystyle\partial_{r}s^{Z}

\displaystyle=n^{Z}B^{r}-\partial_{Z}s^{r}-n^{r}B^{Z}+2h^{Z}B^{\phi}-2h^{r}B^{\phi},

\displaystyle\partial_{\phi}s^{\phi}

\displaystyle=-n^{r}B^{r}+n^{Z}B^{Z}-\frac{1}{r}s^{r},

(1.15)

\displaystyle\partial_{\phi}s^{Z}

\displaystyle=r^{2}(2h^{\phi}B^{r}-n^{Z}B^{\phi}-\partial_{Z}s^{\phi})-2h^{Z}B^{r}+n^{\phi}B^{Z},

\displaystyle\partial_{Z}s^{Z}

\displaystyle=n^{r}B^{r}-n^{\phi}B^{\phi}.

After the same simplification the first and zeroth order terms imply

\displaystyle\partial_{r}m

\displaystyle=s^{Z}B^{\phi}-s^{\phi}B^{Z}+n^{\phi}\partial_{Z}W+n^{Z}\partial_{\phi}W+2h^{r}\partial_{r}W,

\displaystyle\partial_{\phi}m

\displaystyle=s^{r}B^{Z}-s^{Z}B^{r}+r^{2}(n^{r}\partial_{Z}W+2h^{\phi}\partial_{\phi}W+n^{Z}\partial_{r}W),

(1.16)

\displaystyle\partial_{Z}m

\displaystyle=s^{\phi}B^{r}-s^{r}B^{\phi}+2h^{Z}\partial_{Z}W+n^{r}\partial_{\phi}W+n^{\phi}\partial_{r}W,

and

s^{r}\partial_{r}W+s^{\phi}\partial_{\phi}W+s^{Z}\partial_{Z}W=0,

(1.17)

respectively.

The third order equations (
1.2
) can be readily integrated, because they do not depend on the magnetic field
\vec{B}
nor the scalar potential
W
. The solution is therefore the same as in the vanishing magnetic field: The highest order terms lie in the enveloping algebra of the Euclidean Lie algebra
[
20
]
generated by
p_{x},

p_{y},

p_{z},

L_{x},

L_{y},

L_{z}
. For convenience we write it in terms of gauge covariant expressions and in the Cartesian coordinates:

X=\sum_{1\leq a\leq b\leq 6}\alpha_{ab}Y^{A}_{a}Y^{A}_{b}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}s_{j}(\vec{x})p_{j}^{A}+m(\vec{x}),

(1.18)

where
Y^{A}=(p_{x}^{A},p_{y}^{A},p_{z}^{A},L_{x}^{A},L_{y}^{A},L_{z}^{A})
. The coordinates of the gauge covariant angular momentum vector
\vec{L}^{A}=(L_{1}^{A},L_{2}^{A},L_{3}^{A})\equiv(L_{x}^{A},L_{y}^{A},L_{z}^{A})
are defined as

L_{j}^{A}=\sum_{k,l}\varepsilon_{jkl}x_{k}p_{l}^{A}

(1.19)

with the covariant momenta from
eq.

1.4
.

Transforming the second order terms to the cylindrical coordinates using
eq.

1.6
and
eq.

1.8
followed by collecting the momenta
p^{r}
,
p^{\phi}
,
p^{Z}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}h^{r}={}&\left[\left(\alpha_{55}-\alpha_{44}\right){Z}^{2}+\left(\alpha_{24}+\alpha_{15}\right)Z+\alpha_{11}-\alpha_{22}\right]\left(\cos(\phi)\right)^{2}-\\
&-\left[\alpha_{45}{Z}^{2}+\left(\alpha_{14}-\alpha_{25}\right)Z-\alpha_{12}\right]\sin(\phi)\cos(\phi)+\alpha_{44}{Z}^{2}-\alpha_{24}Z+\alpha_{22},\end{split}

(1.20)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{h^{\phi}}={}&\frac{\left[\left(\alpha_{44}-\alpha_{55}\right){Z}^{2}-\left(\alpha_{24}+\alpha_{15}\right)Z+\alpha_{22}-\alpha_{11}\right]\left(\cos(\phi)\right)^{2}}{r^{2}}+\\
&+\frac{\left[\left(\alpha_{45}{Z}^{2}+\left(\alpha_{14}-\alpha_{25}\right)Z-\alpha_{12}\right)\sin(\phi)-\left(\alpha_{46}Z-\alpha_{26}\right)r\right]\cos(\phi)}{r^{2}}+\\
&+\frac{-\left(\alpha_{56}Z+\alpha_{16}\right)r\sin(\phi)+\alpha_{55}{Z}^{2}+\alpha_{66}r^{2}+\alpha_{15}Z+\alpha_{11}}{r^{2}},\end{split}

(1.21)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{h^{Z}}={}&r^{2}\left[\alpha_{44}-\alpha_{45}\cos(\phi)\sin(\phi)-\left(\alpha_{44}-\alpha_{55}\right)\left(\cos(\phi)\right)^{2}\right]+\\
&\alpha_{34}r\sin(\phi)-\alpha_{35}r\cos(\phi)+\alpha_{33},\end{split}

(1.22)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{n^{r}}={}&\left(2\alpha_{45}Z-\alpha_{25}+\alpha_{14}\right)\left(\cos(\phi)\right)^{2}+\\
&+\left[2\left(\alpha_{55}-\alpha_{44}\right)Zr+\alpha_{24}+\alpha_{15}\right]\sin(\phi)\cos(\phi)+\\
&+\frac{\left(-\alpha_{56}r^{2}-\alpha_{34}Z+\alpha_{23}\right)\cos(\phi)+\left(\alpha_{46}r^{2}-\alpha_{35}Z-\alpha_{13}\right)\sin(\phi)}{r}-\\
&-\left(\alpha_{45}Z+\alpha_{14}-\alpha_{36}\right),\end{split}

(1.23)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{n^{\phi}}={}&\left[2\left(\alpha_{44}-\alpha_{55}\right)Z-\alpha_{24}-\alpha_{15}\right]r\left(\cos(\phi)\right)^{2}+\\
&+\left(2\alpha_{45}Z-\alpha_{25}+\alpha_{14}\right)r\sin(\phi)\cos(\phi)+\\
&+\left(\alpha_{35}Z+\alpha_{13}\right)\cos(\phi)-\left(\alpha_{34}Z-\alpha_{23}\right)r\sin(\phi)-\left(2\alpha_{44}Z-\alpha_{24}\right),\end{split}

(1.24)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{n^{Z}}={}&-\frac{2\left[\alpha_{45}{Z}^{2}+\left(\alpha_{14}-\alpha_{25}\right)Z-\alpha_{12}\right]\left(\cos(\phi)\right)^{2}}{r}-\\
&-\frac{2\left[\left(\alpha_{55}-\alpha_{44}\right){Z}^{2}+\left(\alpha_{24}+\alpha_{15}\right)Z-\alpha_{22}+\alpha_{11}\right]\sin(\phi)\cos(\phi)}{r}-\\
&-\left(\alpha_{46}Z-\alpha_{26}\right)\sin(\phi)-\left(\alpha_{56}Z+\alpha_{16}\right)\cos(\phi)+\\
&+\frac{\alpha_{45}{Z}^{2}+\left(\alpha_{14}-\alpha_{25}\right)Z-\alpha_{12}}{r}.\end{split}

(1.25)

If we suppose that the integrals of motion are of the first order,

X=s^{r}\left(r,\phi,Z\right)p_{r}^{A}+s^{\phi}\left(r,\phi,Z\right)p_{\phi}^{A}+s^{Z}\left(r,\phi,Z\right)p_{Z}^{A}+m\left(r,\phi,Z\right),

(1.26)

which corresponds to setting the functions
h,

n
in
section

1.2
to 0, the determining equations simplify as follows: There are no equations of the third order (they are satisfied identically).
The second order equations read

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{r}{s^{r}}=0,\quad\partial_{\phi}{s^{r}}=-r^{2}\partial_{r}{s^{\phi}},\quad\partial_{r}{s^{Z}}=-\partial_{Z}{s^{r}},\\
&\partial_{\phi}{s^{\phi}}=-{\frac{{s^{r}}}{r}},\quad\partial_{\phi}{s^{Z}}=-r^{2}\partial_{Z}{s^{\phi}},\quad\partial_{Z}{s^{Z}}=0.\end{aligned}

(1.27)

The first order equations read

\displaystyle\partial_{r}m

\displaystyle={s^{Z}}{B^{\phi}}-{s^{\phi}}{B^{Z}},

\displaystyle\partial_{\phi}m

\displaystyle={s^{r}}{B^{Z}}-{s^{Z}}{B^{r}},

(1.28)

\displaystyle\partial_{Z}m

\displaystyle={s^{\phi}}{B^{r}}-{s^{r}}{B^{\phi}},

and the zeroth order equation is

{s^{r}}\partial_{r}W+{s^{\phi}}\partial_{\phi}W+{s^{Z}}\partial_{Z}W=0.

(1.29)

The first thing we note is that the second order equations (
1.27
) do not depend on either the magnetic field or the scalar potential in the same way as the third order equations in the previous case. We can therefore solve them for all the cases now. The solution reads

\displaystyle s^{r}

\displaystyle=(k_{5}Z+k_{1})\cos(\phi)-(k_{4}Z-k_{2})\sin(\phi),

(1.30)

\displaystyle s^{\phi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(k_{4}Z-k_{2})\cos(\phi)+(k_{5}Z+k_{1})\sin(\phi)-k_{6}r}{r},

(1.31)

\displaystyle s^{Z}

\displaystyle=-k_{5}r\cos(\phi)+k_{4}r\sin(\phi)+k_{3},

(1.32)

which corresponds to the integral with the first order term in the Euclidean Lie algebra (in the Cartesian coordinates and gauge covariant form)

Y=k_{1}p_{x}^{A}+k_{2}p_{y}^{A}+k_{3}p_{z}^{A}+k_{4}L_{x}^{A}+k_{5}L_{y}^{A}+k_{6}L_{z}^{A}+m(x,y,z).

(1.33)

1.3
Determining equations in quantum mechanics

Let us start with some basic notions regarding the considered systems in quantum mechanics.

We begin in the Cartesian coordinates. We work in the Schrödinger representation, where the coordinates and linear momenta are

\hat{Q}_{j}=x_{j},\quad\hat{P}_{j}=-\mathrm{i}\hbar\partial_{j}\equiv-\mathrm{i}\hbar\nabla_{j},

(1.34)

respectively. (The operator
\hat{Q}
is a multiplication by the corresponding coordinate.)

The convention for operator ordering in the field of integrability is to symmetrize properly
[
23
]
, therefore the Hamiltonian for a system with magnetic field in the Cartesian coordinates reads

\displaystyle\hat{H}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\left(\hat{P}_{j}\hat{P}_{j}+\hat{P}_{j}\hat{A}_{j}(\vec{x})+\hat{A}_{j}(\vec{x})\hat{P}_{j}+\hat{A}_{j}(\vec{x})\hat{A}_{j}(\vec{x})\right)+\hat{V}(\vec{x})=

\displaystyle=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\Delta-\mathrm{i}\hbar\hat{\vec{A}}\cdot\vec{\nabla}-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\widehat{\textrm{div}\vec{A}}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{\vec{A}}\cdot\hat{\vec{A}}+\hat{V}(\vec{x}),

(1.35)

where
\hat{A}_{j}(\vec{x})
,
\widehat{\textrm{div}\vec{A}}
and
\hat{V}(\vec{x})
are the operators of multiplication by the
j
-th coordinate of the vector potential
\vec{A}(\vec{x})
, the divergence of
\vec{A}(\vec{x})
and the scalar potential
V(\vec{x})
, respectively.

We follow the same convention for the integrals of motion as well, namely we write the general second order integral as

\hat{X}=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\{h_{j}(\vec{x}),\hat{P}_{j}^{A}\hat{P}_{j}^{A}\}+\sum_{j,k,l=1}^{3}\frac{|\varepsilon_{jkl}|}{2}\{n_{j}(\vec{x}),\hat{P}_{k}^{A}\hat{P}_{l}^{A}\}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\{s_{j}(\vec{x}),\hat{P}_{j}^{A}\}+m(\vec{x}),

(1.36)

where
\{,\}
denotes the symmetrization

\{\hat{a},\hat{b}\}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\hat{a}\hat{b}+\hat{b}\hat{a}\right).

(1.37)

All choices of symmetrization are equivalent up to redefinition of the lower order terms
[
20
]
, so the choice is without loss of generality.

In quantum mechanics the gauge transformation (
1.3
) demonstrates itself as a unitary transformation
\hat{U}
of the underlying Hilbert space. Defining
\hat{U}
as

\hat{U}\psi(\vec{x})=\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\chi(\vec{x})\right)\cdot\psi(\vec{x}),

(1.38)

the states and observables describing the same physical system are

\psi\to\psi^{\prime}=\hat{U}\psi,\quad\hat{O}\to\hat{O}^{\prime}=\hat{U}\hat{O}\hat{U}^{\dagger}.

(1.39)

The potentials and momenta transform the following way

(\hat{P}_{j}+\hat{A}_{j})\to\hat{U}(\hat{P}_{j}+\hat{A}_{j})\hat{U}^{\dagger}=\hat{P}_{j}+\hat{A}^{\prime}_{j},\quad\hat{V}=\hat{U}\hat{V}\hat{U}^{\dagger}.

(1.40)

We can therefore use the gauge covariant momenta

\hat{P}_{j}^{A}=\hat{P}_{j}+\hat{A}_{j}

(1.41)

in quantum mechanics as well. Their commutation relations in the Cartesian coordinates read

[\hat{P}_{j}^{A},\hat{P}_{k}^{A}]=-\mathrm{i}\hbar\varepsilon_{jkl}B_{l},\quad[\hat{P}_{j}^{A},\hat{Q}_{k}]=-\mathrm{i}\hbar\delta_{jk}.

(1.42)

Now let us turn to the cylindrical coordinates
(r,\phi,Z)
, see
eq.

1.6
. Quantization in non-Cartesian coordinates poses a problem, because the Dirac quantization rule

\{A,B\}_{\text{P.B}}\to\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\hbar}[\hat{A},\hat{B}]

(1.43)

is not invariant under canonical transformations (not even coordinate transformations) in the sense that Schrödinger equations obtained from different canonical coordinates are inequivalent
[
25
, Exercise 7.4.10]
. Experiments show that the correct Schrödinger equation is obtained by quantization of the classical Hamiltonian in the Cartesian coordinates and subsequent transformation into the other coordinate systems. Succinctly, the rule is to quantize and transform (in that order).

Let us therefore transform the quantized Hamiltonian (
1.35
). The gradient operator in the cylindrical coordinates reads

\nabla f=\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial r},\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\phi},\frac{\partial f}{\partial Z}\right)

(1.44)

and the Laplace operator is

\Delta=\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(r\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\right)+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\phi^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial Z^{2}}.

(1.45)

The divergence is

\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{A}=\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial(rA_{r})}{\partial r}+\frac{\partial A_{\phi}}{\partial\phi}+\frac{\partial A_{Z}}{\partial Z},

(1.46)

(the right hand side in term of 1-form components)
so the final form of the Hamiltonian in the cylindrical coordinates reads (
A_{i}
and
V
functions of
r,

\phi,

Z
)

\displaystyle\hat{H}={}

\displaystyle-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(r\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\right)+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\phi^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial Z^{2}}\right)-\mathrm{i}\hbar\left(\hat{A}_{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\frac{\hat{A}_{\phi}}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}+\hat{A}_{Z}\frac{\partial}{\partial Z}\right)-

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathrm{i}\hbar}{2}\left(\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial(rA_{r})}{\partial r}+\frac{\partial A_{\phi}}{\partial\phi}+\frac{\partial A_{Z}}{\partial Z}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left((\hat{A}_{r})^{2}+\frac{(\hat{A}_{\phi})^{2}}{r^{2}}+(\hat{A}_{Z})^{2}\right)+\hat{V}.

(1.47)

The same procedure must be applied to the integrals of motion as well, we thus quantize in the Cartesian coordinates, symmetrize and transform into the cylindrical coordinates. For this we need the transformation rules for momenta form
eq.

1.8
, which we readily invert to get

p_{r}=\cos(\phi)p_{x}+\sin(\phi)p_{y},\quad p_{\phi}=-r\sin(\phi)p_{x}+r\cos(\phi)p_{y},\quad p_{Z}=p_{z}

(1.48)

and the momentum operators
\hat{P}_{i}
in the cylindrical coordinates read

\hat{P}_{x}=-\mathrm{i}\hbar\left(\cos(\phi)\partial_{r}-\frac{\sin(\phi)}{r}\partial_{\phi}\right),\ \hat{P}_{y}=-\mathrm{i}\hbar\left(\sin(\phi)\partial_{r}+\frac{\cos(\phi)}{r}\partial_{\phi}\right),\ \hat{P}_{z}=-\mathrm{i}\hbar\partial_{Z}.

(1.49)

The correct way to obtain the determining equations in the cylindrical coordinates is to transform the Cartesian equations from
[
20
]
. Let us list them for reference:

The third order equations read

\displaystyle\partial_{x}h_{x}=0,\quad\partial_{y}h_{x}=-\partial_{x}n_{x},\quad\partial_{z}h_{x}=-\partial_{x}n_{y},

\displaystyle\partial_{x}h_{y}=-\partial_{y}n_{z},\quad\partial_{y}h_{y}=0,\quad\partial_{z}h_{y}=-\partial_{y}n_{x},

(1.50)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}h_{z}=-\partial_{z}n_{z},\quad\partial_{y}h_{z}=-\partial_{z}n_{x},\quad\partial_{z}h_{z}=0,

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\vec{n}=0.

The second order equations are

\displaystyle\partial_{x}s_{x}=n_{y}B^{y}-n_{z}B^{z},

\displaystyle\partial_{y}s_{y}=n_{z}B^{z}-n_{x}B^{x},

\displaystyle\partial_{z}s_{z}=n_{x}B^{x}-n_{y}B^{y},

\displaystyle\partial_{y}s_{x}+\partial_{x}s_{y}=n_{x}B^{y}-n_{y}B^{y}+2(h_{x}-h_{y})B^{z},

(1.51)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}s_{x}+\partial_{x}s_{z}=n_{z}B^{x}-n_{x}B^{z}+2(h_{z}-h_{x})B^{y},

\displaystyle\partial_{y}s_{z}+\partial_{z}s_{y}=n_{y}B^{z}-n_{z}B^{y}+2(h_{y}-h_{z})B^{x}

with the following consequence

\nabla\cdot\vec{s}=0.

(1.52)

The first order

\displaystyle\partial_{x}m=2h_{x}\partial_{x}W+n_{z}\partial_{y}W+n_{y}\partial_{z}W+s_{z}B^{y}-s_{y}B^{z},

\displaystyle\partial_{y}m=2h_{y}\partial_{y}W+n_{z}\partial_{x}W+n_{x}\partial_{z}W+s_{x}B^{z}-s_{z}B^{x},

(1.53)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}m=2h_{z}\partial_{z}W+n_{y}\partial_{x}W+n_{x}\partial_{y}W+s_{y}B^{x}-s_{x}B^{y}.

The zeroth order equation containing the
\hbar^{2}
–proportional quantum correction reads

\begin{split}\vec{s}\cdot\nabla W+\frac{\hbar^{2}}{4}\left(\partial_{z}n_{x}\partial_{z}B^{x}-\partial_{y}n_{x}\partial_{y}B^{x}+\partial_{x}n_{y}\partial_{x}B^{y}-\partial_{z}n_{y}\partial_{z}B^{y}\right.+\\
+\left.\partial_{y}n_{z}\partial_{y}B^{z}-\partial_{x}n_{z}\partial_{x}B^{z}+\partial_{x}n_{x}\partial_{y}B^{y}-\partial_{y}n_{y}\partial_{x}B^{x}\right)=0.\end{split}

(1.54)

Although the last two terms suggest that the quantum correction is not invariant under Euclidean transformations, which are symmetries the system possesses clearly, it is not the case due to the identity
[
20
]

\partial_{x}n_{x}\partial_{y}B^{y}-\partial_{y}n_{y}\partial_{x}B^{x}=\partial_{y}n_{y}\partial_{z}B^{z}-\partial_{z}n_{z}\partial_{y}B^{y}=\partial_{z}n_{z}\partial_{x}B^{x}-\partial_{x}n_{x}\partial_{z}B^{z},

(1.55)

which follows from

\nabla\cdot\vec{B}=0,\quad\nabla\cdot\vec{n}=0.

(1.56)

The last missing piece needed to perform the transformation is

\begin{split}h_{x}&=h^{r}(\cos(\phi))^{2}+h^{\phi}r^{2}(\sin(\phi))^{2}-n^{Z}r\cos(\phi)\sin(\phi),\\
h_{y}&=h^{r}(\sin(\phi))^{2}+h^{\phi}r^{2}(\cos(\phi))^{2}+n^{Z}r\cos(\phi)\sin(\phi),\\
h_{z}&=h^{Z},\\
n_{x}&=n^{r}r\cos(\phi)+n^{\phi}\sin(\phi),\\
n_{y}&=-n^{r}r\sin(\phi)+n^{\phi}\cos(\phi),\\
n_{z}&=2h^{r}\sin(\phi)\cos(\phi)-2h^{\phi}r^{2}\sin(\phi)\cos(\phi)+n^{Z}r[(\cos(\phi))^{2}-(\sin(\phi))^{2}],\\
s_{x}&=s^{r}\cos(\phi)-s^{\phi}r\sin(\phi),\\
s_{x}&=s^{r}\sin(\phi)+s^{\phi}r\cos(\phi),\\
s_{z}&=s^{Z}.\end{split}

(1.57)

It is obtained (in classical mechanics) by transforming the linear momenta using
eq.

1.8
in the second order integral of motion
X
, see
section

1.2
, and the definition of the functions
n,

h,

s
as the coefficients of the corresponding momenta (
h_{x}
is the coefficient of
p_{x}^{2}
,
n_{x}
of
p_{y}p_{z}
,
s_{x}
of
p_{x}
etc. and analogously in the cylindrical coordinates).

Due to the transformation rules (
1.57
), the third, second and first order equations (
1.3
), (
1.3
) and (
1.3
), which do not have any quantum correction, get the same form as in classical mechanics, namely the form of equations (
1.2
), (
1.2
) and (
1.2
), respectively.
The transformed zeroth order equation with derivatives of the functions
h
eliminated using the third order equations (
1.2
) reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}&s^{r}\partial_{r}W+s^{\phi}\partial_{\phi}W+s^{Z}\partial_{Z}W+\\
&+\frac{\hbar^{2}}{4}\left[-{\frac{\partial_{Z}n^{Z}B^{r}}{r}}+{\frac{\left({r}\partial_{\phi}n^{r}+n^{\phi}\right)B^{\phi}}{r^{2}}}+{\frac{\left(r\partial_{r}n^{Z}+n^{Z}\right)B^{Z}}{r^{2}}}-\right.\\
&-{\frac{\left(r\partial_{\phi}n^{r}+n^{\phi}\right)\partial_{\phi}B^{r}}{r^{3}}}+\frac{\left(r\partial_{Z}n^{Z}+r\partial_{r}n^{r}+n^{r}\right)\partial_{r}B^{r}}{r}+\left(\partial_{Z}n^{r}\right)\partial_{Z}B^{r}-\\
&-\left(\partial_{Z}n^{\phi}\right)\partial_{Z}B^{\phi}+\frac{\left(r\partial_{r}n^{r}+n^{r}\right)\partial_{\phi}B^{\phi}}{r}+\left(\partial_{r}n^{\phi}\right)\partial_{r}B^{\phi}+\\
&+\left.{\frac{\left(r\partial_{\phi}n^{Z}+2h^{r}-2r^{2}h^{\phi}\right)\partial_{\phi}B^{Z}}{r^{3}}}-{\frac{\left(r\partial_{r}n^{Z}+n^{Z}\right)\partial_{r}B^{Z}}{r}}\right]=0.\end{split}

(1.58)

If we use the identity
eq.

1.55
to change the last 2 terms in the quantum correction in
eq.

1.54
, we find that only the terms with diagonal derivatives such as
\partial_{r}B^{r}
change. By taking
\tfrac{1}{3}
of each term in
eq.

1.55
instead of the last 2 terms in
eq.

1.54
and using the identity

\partial_{r}B^{r}+\partial_{\phi}B^{\phi}+\partial_{Z}B^{Z}=0,

(1.59)

which follows from exactness of the 2-form
B={\operatorname{d}}A
, we simplify the diagonal terms

\frac{\left(r\partial_{Z}n^{Z}+r\partial_{r}n^{r}+n^{r}\right)\partial_{r}B^{r}}{r}+\frac{\left(r\partial_{r}n^{r}+n^{r}\right)\partial_{\phi}B^{\phi}}{r}=(\partial_{Z}n^{Z})\partial_{r}B^{r}-\frac{(r\partial_{r}n^{r}+n^{r})\partial_{Z}B^{Z}}{r}.

(1.60)

We note that brackets of the type
\left(r\partial_{r}n^{Z}+n^{Z}\right)
vanish if
n^{Z}\sim\frac{1}{r}
. This means vanishing quantum correction for the integrals of motion with the highest order term
P_{i}P_{j},\ i,j\in\{x,y,z\}
, taking into account
eq.

1.57
. (The result can be clearly seen from
eq.

1.54
, so this merely checks the transformation.)

For comparison, we include the quantum correction with collected functions
n
and their derivatives. We simplified it using the identities (
1.59
) and (
1.60
).

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{\hbar^{2}}{4}\left[{\frac{\left(B^{Z}-r\partial_{r}B^{Z}\right)(n^{Z}+r\partial_{r}n_{Z})}{r^{2}}}+{\frac{\left(rB^{\phi}-\partial_{\phi}B^{r}\right)(n^{\phi}+r\partial_{\phi}n^{r})}{r^{3}}}-\right.\\
&-\frac{\left(\partial_{Z}B^{Z}\right)(n^{r}+r\partial_{r}n^{r})}{r}-\frac{\left(B^{r}-r\partial_{r}B^{r}\right)\partial_{Z}n^{Z}}{r}+{\frac{\left(\partial_{\phi}B^{Z}\right)\partial_{\phi}n^{Z}}{r^{2}}}-\\
&-(\partial_{Z}B^{\phi})\partial_{Z}n^{\phi}+\left(\partial_{r}B^{\phi}\right)\partial_{r}n^{\phi}+(\partial_{Z}B^{r})\partial_{Z}n^{r}\left.+\frac{2\partial_{\phi}B^{Z}\left(h^{r}-r^{2}h^{\phi}\right)}{r^{3}}\right].\end{split}

(1.61)

Let us have a look at the effects of the correction (
1.58
) on the cylindrical integrable systems. From
eq.

1.54
follows that the determining equations for the first order integrals are the same as in the classical case (
n^{i}=0
,
i\in\{x,y,z\}
). As we have already mentioned, the correction vanishes for integrals of motion with the highest order term
\hat{P}_{i}\hat{P}_{j},

i,j\in\{x,y,z\},
whose functions
n^{i}
are constant. This includes our integral
\hat{P}_{Z}^{2}+\ldots

On the other hand, the integral
\hat{P}_{\phi}^{2}+\ldots
with
h^{\phi}=1
corresponds to

h^{x}=y^{2},\quad h^{y}=x^{2},\quad h^{z}=0,\quad n^{z}=-{2xy},\quad n^{x}=0,\quad n^{y}=0

(1.62)

and the quantum correction reads

-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2r}\partial_{\phi}B^{Z}.

(1.63)

(It can be seen from
eq.

1.58
directly.)
The correction is non-zero for the general form of
B^{Z}
obtained from the highest order determining equations
[
11
]
,

B^{Z}=-\frac{1}{2r^{2}}(\psi(\phi)+\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi))+r\mu(Z)-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{\prime}(r),

(1.64)

see
eq.

1.88
below.

There could be another quantum correction arising from
[\hat{X}_{1},\hat{X}_{2}]=0
, which is the quantum analogue of involutivity condition. Let us show that is not the case:

We first assume that both integrals of motion are of the first order, that is

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{1}=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\frac{1}{2}\left(s_{j}(x,y,z)\hat{P}_{j}^{A}+\hat{P}_{j}^{A}s_{j}(x,y,z)\right)+m(x,y,z),

(1.65)

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{3}\frac{1}{2}\left(\tilde{s}_{k}(x,y,z)\hat{P}_{k}^{A}+\hat{P}_{k}^{A}\tilde{s}_{k}(x,y,z)\right)+\tilde{m}(x,y,z).

(1.66)

After some calculations using the commutation relations from eq. (
1.42
),
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}[X_{1},X_{2}]={}&\sum_{j,k}\mathrm{i}\hbar\left(\tilde{s}_{k}\partial_{k}s_{j}\hat{P}_{j}^{A}-s_{j}\partial_{j}\tilde{s}_{k}\hat{P}_{k}^{A}-\varepsilon_{jkl}s_{j}\tilde{s}_{k}B^{l}-s_{j}\partial_{j}\tilde{m}+\tilde{s}_{k}\partial_{k}m\right)+\\
&+\sum_{j,k}\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}(\tilde{s}_{k}\partial_{jk}s_{j}-s_{j}\partial_{jk}\tilde{s}_{k}).\end{split}

(1.67)

The first line corresponds to the Poisson bracket, the second line is an apparent quantum correction.
Collecting the coefficients of
\hat{P}_{j}^{A}
(renaming the dummy indices in the second term), we obtain the first order equations

\sum_{k=1}^{3}(\tilde{s}_{k}\partial_{k}s_{j}-s_{k}\partial_{k}\tilde{s}_{j})=0.

(1.68)

The quantum correction can be rewritten as follows

\sum_{j,k}\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\partial_{j}(\tilde{s}_{k}\partial_{k}s_{j}-s_{k}\partial_{k}\tilde{s}_{j}),

(1.69)

because the terms with the first order derivatives cancel if we rename the indices. The bracket contains the first order equations; therefore, the quantum correction vanishes.

Now let us turn to the cylindrical case, i.e. the integrals of motion of the form

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{1}=\left(\hat{L}_{z}^{A}\right)^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\frac{1}{2}\left(s_{j}(x,y,z)\hat{P}_{j}^{A}+\hat{P}_{j}^{A}s_{j}(x,y,z)\right)+m(x,y,z),

(1.70)

\displaystyle\hat{X}_{2}=\left(\hat{P}_{z}^{A}\right)^{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{3}\frac{1}{2}\left(\tilde{s}_{k}(x,y,z)\hat{P}_{k}^{A}+\hat{P}_{k}^{A}\tilde{s}_{k}(x,y,z)\right)+\tilde{m}(x,y,z).

(1.71)

We impose the relation
[\hat{X}_{1},\hat{X}_{2}]=0
and separate the equation according to the powers of
P
.
The second order terms

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{z}\hat{P}_{z}

\displaystyle:\partial_{z}s_{z}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{z}\hat{P}_{x}

\displaystyle:y(x\partial_{y}-y\partial_{x})\tilde{s}_{z}+2y(yB_{x}+yB_{y})+\partial_{z}s_{x}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{z}\hat{P}_{y}

\displaystyle:x(x\partial_{y}-y\partial_{x})\tilde{s}_{z}+2x(yB_{x}+yB_{y})-\partial_{z}s_{y}

\displaystyle=0,

(1.72)

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{x}\hat{P}_{x}

\displaystyle:y\tilde{s}_{y}+y(x\partial_{y}-y\partial_{x})\tilde{s}_{x}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{y}\hat{P}_{y}

\displaystyle:x\tilde{s}_{x}-y(x\partial_{y}-y\partial_{x})\tilde{s}_{y}

\displaystyle=0,

give the same equations as would be obtained in the classical case. (The equation for
\hat{P}_{x}\hat{P}_{y}
vanishes as a consequence of those for
\hat{P}_{x}\hat{P}_{x}
and
\hat{P}_{y}\hat{P}_{y}
.)

The first order equations obtain some rather complicated apparent corrections, which nevertheless vanish when we use the second order equations (
1.3
). The equations (without the apparent corrections) read

\displaystyle 2y[(x\partial_{y}-y\partial_{x})\tilde{m}+(xB_{x}+yB_{y})\tilde{s}_{z}-(x\tilde{s}_{x}+y\tilde{s}_{y})B_{z}]+\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\tilde{s}_{j}\partial_{j}s_{x}-\tilde{s}_{j}\partial_{j}s_{x})

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle 2x[(x\partial_{y}-y\partial_{x})\tilde{m}+(xB_{x}+yB_{y})\tilde{s}_{z}-(x\tilde{s}_{x}+y\tilde{s}_{y})B_{z}]+\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\tilde{s}_{j}\partial_{j}s_{y}-\tilde{s}_{j}\partial_{j}s_{y})

\displaystyle=0,

(1.73)

\displaystyle 2\partial_{z}m-2(s_{y}B_{x}-s_{x}B_{y})+\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\tilde{s}_{j}\partial_{j}s_{z}-s_{j}\partial_{j}\tilde{s}_{z})

\displaystyle=0.

The same happens in the zeroth order: All apparent corrections vanish when we use the higher order conditions (
1.3
) and (
1.3
), so the equation has the classical form

\tilde{s}_{x}(s_{y}B_{z}-s_{z}B_{y})+\tilde{s}_{y}(s_{z}B_{x}-s_{x}B_{z})+\tilde{s}_{z}(s_{y}B_{x}-s_{x}B_{y})+\sum_{j=1}^{3}(\tilde{s}_{j}\partial_{j}m-s_{j}\partial_{j}\tilde{m})=0.

(1.74)

To sum up: The apparent quantum corrections to the involutivity condition
[\hat{X}_{1},\hat{X}_{2}]=0
vanish once we impose the higher order conditions from the same commutator for both the first order and the cylindrical integrals. We note that we do not need to use the determining equations for the integrals to prove the assertion.

In the cylindrical case there is, therefore, only one equation with quantum correction, namely the zeroth order equation for
X_{1}
, which now reads

s_{1}^{r}\partial_{r}W+s_{1}^{\phi}\partial_{\phi}W+s_{1}^{Z}\partial_{Z}W-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2r}\partial_{\phi}B^{Z}=0.

(1.75)

In order to consider the quantum correction for integrals containing angular momenta in the second order term, we suggest using the Cartesian form of the correction from
eq.

1.54
or transforming it into more suitable coordinates because using the correction from
eq.

1.58
seems not tractable.

1.4
Quantum integrable systems

Here we solve the quantum determining equations for the case of cylindrical integrals in the cylindrical coordinates. As we have seen in the last section, the quantum correction has arisen in one equation only, namely the zeroth order equation for the integral
\hat{X}_{1}=(\hat{P}_{\phi}^{A})^{2}+\ldots
, see eq. (
1.75
). Because there are so little changes, we will closely follow the analysis from
[
11
]
and focus mainly on the cases which are affected by the correction.

We start with reducing the determining equations (
1.2
)–(
1.17
) to the case of cylindrical integrals, which in classical mechanics read

\begin{split}&X_{1}=(p_{\phi}^{A})^{2}+s_{1}^{r}(r,\phi,Z)p_{r}^{A}+s_{1}^{\phi}(r,\phi,Z)p_{\phi}^{A}+s_{1}^{Z}(r,\phi,Z)p_{Z}^{A}+m_{1}(r,\phi,Z),\\
&X_{2}=(p_{Z}^{A})^{2}+s_{2}^{r}(r,\phi,Z)p_{r}^{A}+s_{2}^{\phi}(r,\phi,Z)p_{\phi}^{A}+s_{2}^{Z}(r,\phi,Z)p_{Z}^{A}+m_{2}(r,\phi,Z).\end{split}

(1.76)

To obtain their quantum mechanical form, we would have to transform the integrals into the Cartesian coordinates, quantize with proper symmetrization and transform back into the cylindrical coordinates. Because we have transformed the determining equations from the Cartesian form, we do not need the explicit form of the integrals here.

The reduction is done by substituting the appropriate values for the functions
h,

n
, namely the only non-zero functions

h^{\phi}_{1}=1,\quad h_{2}^{Z}=1,

(1.77)

into the general determining equations (
1.2
)–(
1.17
) and taking into account the quantum correction (
1.63
) in the zeroth order equation. We obtain the following:

The third order equations (
1.2
) are trivially satisfied for both integrals. The second order equations for the integrals
\hat{X}_{1}
and
\hat{X}_{2}
read

\begin{split}\partial_{r}s_{1}^{r}&=0,\quad\partial_{\phi}s_{1}^{\phi}=-\frac{s_{1}^{r}}{r},\\
\partial_{\phi}s_{1}^{r}&=-r^{2}(\partial_{r}s_{1}^{\phi}+2B^{Z}),\quad\partial_{\phi}s_{1}^{Z}=-r^{2}(\partial_{Z}s_{1}^{\phi}-2B^{r}),\\
\partial_{r}s_{1}^{Z}&=-\partial_{Z}s_{1}^{r},\quad\partial_{Z}s_{1}^{Z}=0,\end{split}

(1.78)

and

\begin{split}\partial_{r}s_{2}^{r}&=0,\quad\partial_{\phi}s_{2}^{\phi}=-\frac{s_{2}^{r}}{r},\\
\partial_{\phi}s_{2}^{r}&=-r^{2}\partial_{r}s_{2}^{\phi},\quad\partial_{\phi}s_{2}^{Z}=-r^{2}\partial_{Z}s_{2}^{\phi}-2B^{r},\\
\partial_{r}s_{2}^{Z}&=-\partial_{Z}s_{2}^{r}+2B^{\phi},\quad\partial_{Z}s_{2}^{Z}=0,\end{split}

(1.79)

respectively.

The first order equations reduce to those for
\hat{X}_{1}

\displaystyle\partial_{r}m_{1}

\displaystyle=s_{1}^{Z}B^{\phi}-s_{1}^{\phi}B^{Z},

\displaystyle\partial_{\phi}m_{1}

\displaystyle=s_{1}^{r}B^{Z}-s_{1}^{Z}B^{r}+2r^{2}\partial_{\phi}W,

(1.80)

\displaystyle\partial_{Z}m_{1}

\displaystyle=s_{1}^{\phi}B^{r}-s_{1}^{r}B^{\phi},

and those for
\hat{X}_{2}

\displaystyle\partial_{r}m_{2}

\displaystyle=s_{2}^{Z}B^{\phi}-s_{2}^{\phi}B^{Z},

\displaystyle\partial_{\phi}m_{2}

\displaystyle=s_{2}^{r}B^{Z}-s_{2}^{Z}B^{r},

(1.81)

\displaystyle\partial_{Z}m_{2}

\displaystyle=s_{2}^{\phi}B^{r}-s_{2}^{r}B^{\phi}+2\partial_{Z}W.

One of the zeroth order equations is the only one to change with respect to the classical case:

\displaystyle s_{1}^{r}\partial_{r}W+s_{1}^{\phi}\partial_{\phi}W+s_{1}^{Z}\partial_{Z}W-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2r}\partial_{\phi}B^{Z}=0,

(1.82)

\displaystyle s_{2}^{r}\partial_{r}W+s_{2}^{\phi}\partial_{\phi}W+s_{2}^{Z}\partial_{Z}W=0.

(1.83)

In addition to the commutation with the Hamiltonian, i.e. the condition on integrals of motion, we impose the commutation of the integrals
[\hat{X}_{1},\hat{X}_{2}]=0
, which corresponds to the classical notion of involution. We obtain additional equations for each order in momenta, namely those of the second order

\displaystyle\partial_{\phi}s_{2}^{\phi}

\displaystyle=0,\quad\partial_{\phi}s_{2}^{r}=0,\quad\partial_{Z}s_{1}^{r}=0,\quad\partial_{\phi}s_{2}^{Z}=\partial_{Z}s_{1}^{\phi}-2B^{r},

(1.84)

of the first order

\displaystyle s_{2}^{Z}\partial_{Z}s_{1}^{r}+s_{2}^{\phi}\partial_{\phi}s_{1}^{r}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle-s_{1}^{\phi}(2B^{r}+\partial_{\phi}s_{2}^{Z})+s_{2}^{Z}\partial_{Z}s_{1}^{Z}-s_{1}^{Z}\partial_{Z}s_{2}^{Z}+

\displaystyle+s_{2}^{\phi}\partial_{\phi}2_{1}^{Z}+s_{1}^{r}(2B^{\phi}-\partial_{r}s_{2}^{Z})+2\partial_{Z}m_{1}

\displaystyle=0,

(1.85)

\displaystyle-s_{2}^{Z}(2B^{r}-\partial_{Z}s_{1}^{\phi})+s_{2}^{\phi}\partial_{\phi}s_{1}^{\phi}-

\displaystyle-s_{1}^{Z}\partial_{Z}s_{2}^{\phi}-s_{1}^{r}\partial_{r}s_{2}^{\phi}-2\partial_{\phi}m_{2}

\displaystyle=0,

and of the zeroth order

\displaystyle-s_{1}^{r}\partial_{r}m_{2}+s_{2}^{\phi}\partial_{\phi}m_{1}-s_{1}^{\phi}\partial_{\phi}m_{2}+s_{2}^{Z}\partial_{Z}m_{1}-s_{1}^{Z}\partial_{Z}m_{2}+

\displaystyle+B^{r}(s_{2}^{\phi}s_{1}^{Z}-s_{1}^{\phi}s_{2}^{Z})+B^{\phi}s_{1}^{r}s_{2}^{Z}-B^{Z}s_{1}^{r}s_{2}^{\phi}

\displaystyle=0.

(1.86)

None of them has any quantum correction, as was considered at the end of Section
1.3
(in the Cartesian coordinates).

The second order equations (
1.78
), (
1.79
) and (
1.84
) can be solved for the functions
s_{j}
and the magnetic field
B
in terms of 5 functions of one variable each, which we call the auxiliary functions:

\displaystyle\begin{split}s_{1}^{r}&=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\phi}\psi(\phi),\quad s_{1}^{\phi}=-\frac{\psi(\phi)}{r}-r^{2}\mu(Z)+\rho(r),\quad s_{1}^{Z}=\tau(\phi),\\
s_{2}^{r}&=0,\quad s_{2}^{\phi}=\mu(Z),\quad s_{2}^{Z}=-\frac{\tau(\phi)}{r^{2}}+\sigma(r),\end{split}

(1.87)

\displaystyle\begin{split}B^{r}&=-\frac{r^{2}}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}Z}\mu(Z)+\frac{1}{2r^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\phi}\tau(\phi),\quad B^{\phi}=\frac{\tau(\phi)}{r^{3}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\sigma(r),\\
B^{Z}&=\frac{-\psi(\phi)}{2r^{2}}+r\mu(Z)-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}r}\rho(r)-\frac{1}{2r^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}\phi^{2}}\psi(\phi).\end{split}

(1.88)

From now on we use primes for derivatives of functions of one variable, but we use dot for derivatives with respect to time.

We substitute the result into the remaining determining equations to replace the functions
s
and the magnetic field
B
by the auxiliary functions
\rho(r),

\sigma(r),

\tau(\phi),

\psi(\phi),

\mu(Z)
. The first order equations (
1.4
), (
1.4
) and (
1.4
) give us one direct condition on the auxiliary functions
\psi(\phi)
and
\mu(Z)
and several conditions on the derivatives of
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
:

\displaystyle\left(r\rho(r)-\psi(\phi)-r^{3}\mu(Z)\right)\left(\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+r^{2}\rho^{\prime}(r)\right)+

\displaystyle+\left(r^{3}\mu(Z)+r\rho(r)\right)\psi(\phi)-\psi(\phi)^{2}+r^{3}\tau(\phi)\sigma^{\prime}(r)+

\displaystyle+2r^{6}\mu(Z)^{2}-2r^{4}\rho(r)\mu(Z)+2\tau(\phi)^{2}-2r^{3}\partial_{r}m_{1}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\left(2r^{3}\mu(Z)-r^{2}\rho^{\prime}(r)-\psi(\phi)-\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)\right)+

\displaystyle+\tau(\phi)\left(r^{4}\mu^{\prime}(Z)-\tau^{\prime}(\phi)\right)+4r^{4}W_{\phi}-2r^{2}\partial_{\phi}m_{1}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\left(\tau^{\prime}(\phi)-r^{4}\mu^{\prime}(Z)\right)\left(r\rho(r)-\psi(\phi)-r^{3}\mu(Z)\right)-

\displaystyle-\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\left(r^{3}\sigma^{\prime}(r)+2\tau(\phi)\right)-2r^{3}\partial_{Z}m_{1}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle r^{3}\mu(Z)(\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+\psi(\phi))+r^{3}\sigma^{\prime}(r)\left(r^{2}\sigma(r)-\tau(\phi)\right)-2r^{6}\mu(Z)^{2}+

(1.89)

\displaystyle+r^{5}\mu(Z)\rho^{\prime}(r)+2r^{2}\sigma(r)\tau(\phi)-2\tau(\phi)^{2}-2r^{5}\partial_{r}m_{2}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\left(r^{4}\mu^{\prime}(Z)-\tau^{\prime}(\phi)\right)\left(r^{2}\sigma(r)-\tau(\phi)\right)-2r^{4}\partial_{\phi}m_{2}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle-r^{4}\mu(Z)\mu^{\prime}(Z)+\mu(Z)\tau^{\prime}(\phi)+4r^{2}W_{Z}-2r^{2}\partial_{Z}m_{2}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\mu(Z)\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\left(r^{2}\rho(r)-r\psi(\phi)-r^{4}\mu(Z)\right)\mu^{\prime}(Z)+\mu(Z)\tau^{\prime}(\phi)+2\partial_{Z}m_{1}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle 0,

\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}(\phi)\left(r^{2}\sigma(r)-\tau(\phi)\right)+r^{4}\tau(\phi)\mu^{\prime}(Z)+r^{3}\mu(Z)\psi^{\prime}(\phi)+2r^{4}\partial_{\phi}m_{2}

\displaystyle{}={}

\displaystyle 0.

We see that we have two equations for each of the derivatives
\partial_{Z}m_{1}
and
\partial_{\phi}m_{2}
, so we obtain constraints for the auxiliary functions (the two values of
\partial_{Z}m_{1}
and
\partial_{\phi}m_{2}
must coincide). Assuming
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
to be sufficiently smooth, we impose the Clairaut compatibility conditions
\partial_{ba}m_{i}=\partial_{ab}m_{i}
on the second derivatives and obtain equations for the mixed second derivatives of the scalar potential
W
, which we list shortly, after we analyse the zeroth order equations.

Only one of the zeroth order equations (
1.82
) and (
1.4
) obtains the quantum correction:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\left(r^{2}\sigma(r)-\tau(\phi)\right)W_{Z}+r^{2}\mu(Z)W_{\phi}&{}={}&0,&\\
&\left(r\rho(r)-\psi(\phi)-r^{3}\mu(Z)\right)W_{\phi}+r\left(\psi^{\prime}(\phi)W_{r}+\tau(\phi)W_{Z}\right)+&\\
&+\hbar^{2}\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)+\psi^{\prime}(\phi)}{4r^{2}}&{}={}&0,&\\
&2r^{4}\left(r^{3}\mu(Z)-r\rho(r)+\psi(\phi)\right)\partial_{\phi}m_{2}+2r^{3}\left(r^{2}\sigma(r)-\tau(\phi)\right)\partial_{Z}m_{1}+&\\
&+r^{3}\mu(Z)\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+2r^{5}\mu(Z)\partial_{\phi}m_{1}-2r^{5}\tau(\phi)\partial_{Z}m_{2}+&\\
&+\left[r^{3}\left(r^{2}\sigma(r)-\tau(\phi)\right)\sigma^{\prime}(r)-2r^{6}\mu(Z)^{2}+r^{5}\mu(Z)\rho^{\prime}(r)+\right.&\\
&\left.+r^{3}\mu(Z)\psi(\phi)+2r^{2}\sigma(r)\tau(\phi)-2\tau(\phi)^{2}-2r^{5}\partial_{r}m_{2}\right]\psi^{\prime}(\phi)-&\\
&-\left[r^{2}\left(r^{3}\mu(Z)-r\rho(r)+\psi(\phi)\right)\sigma(r)\right.+&\\
&+\left.\tau(\phi)\left(r\rho(r)-\psi(\phi)\right)\right]\left(r^{4}\mu^{\prime}(Z)-\tau^{\prime}(\phi)\right)&{}={}&0.&\end{aligned}

(1.90)

Substituting for the derivatives of
m
from
section

1.4
into eq. (
1.90
), we obtain a system of linear inhomogeneous algebraic equations for the first derivatives of the scalar potential
W
.

The final form of the reduced equations, which we analyse hereafter, is as follows. (Indexes of
W
mean partial derivatives.)

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\left(r^{3}\sigma^{\prime}(r)+2\tau(\phi)\right)-\tau^{\prime}(\phi)\left(r\rho(r)-\psi(\phi)\right)={}

\displaystyle 0,

(1.91)

\displaystyle\mu(Z)\psi^{\prime}(\phi)+r^{3}\sigma(r)\mu^{\prime}(Z)={}

\displaystyle 0,

(1.92)

\displaystyle W_{r\phi}={}

\displaystyle-\frac{2}{r}W_{\phi}+\frac{1}{4r^{5}}\left[\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\left(r^{3}(\rho^{\prime\prime}(r)-\mu(Z))-r^{2}\rho^{\prime}(r)+r\rho(r)-3\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)-4\psi(\phi)\right)+\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\tau^{\prime}(\phi)\left(r^{3}\sigma^{\prime}(r)+2\tau(\phi)\right)-2r^{4}\tau(\phi)\mu^{\prime}(Z)-\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)\left(\psi(\phi)-r\rho(r)\right)\right],

\displaystyle W_{\phi Z}={}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4r^{2}}\left[r^{2}\mu^{\prime\prime}(Z)\left(\tau(\phi)-r^{2}\sigma(r)\right)+\tau^{\prime\prime}(\phi)\mu(Z)\right],

(1.93)

\displaystyle W_{rZ}={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4r^{3}}\left[r\mu^{\prime}(Z)\left(r^{2}\rho^{\prime}(r)+\psi(\phi)-2r^{3}\mu(Z)\right)+2\mu(Z)\tau^{\prime}(\phi)\right],

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&r^{2}\mu(Z)&r^{2}\sigma(r)-\tau(\phi)\\
\psi^{\prime}(\phi)&\rho(r)-r^{2}\mu(Z)-\frac{\psi(\phi)}{r}&\tau(\phi)\\
0&4r^{7}\mu(Z)&-4r^{5}\tau(\phi)\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}W_{r}\\
W_{\phi}\\
W_{Z}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
-\frac{\hbar^{2}(\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)+\psi^{\prime}(\phi))}{4r^{3}}\\
\alpha(r,\phi,Z)\end{pmatrix}

(1.94)

with

\displaystyle\alpha(r,\phi,Z)={}

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\left[\left(r^{5}\sigma(r)-r^{3}\tau(\phi)\right)\sigma^{\prime}(r)+r^{5}\mu(Z)\rho^{\prime}(r)-2\tau(\phi)^{2}\right.+

\displaystyle\left.+2r^{2}\sigma(r)\tau(\phi)-r^{3}\mu(Z)\left(r^{3}\mu(Z)+r\rho(r)-2\psi(\phi)\right)\right]+

\displaystyle+\tau^{\prime}(\phi)\left[\left(r\rho(r)-\psi(\phi)\right)\tau(\phi)+r^{2}\sigma(r)\left(r^{3}\mu(Z)-r\rho(r)+\psi(\phi)\right)\right]-

\displaystyle-r^{4}\mu^{\prime}(Z)\tau(\phi)\left(r\rho(r)-\psi(\phi)\right).

(1.95)

We denote the matrix in
eq.

1.94
by
M
.

The only change with respect to the classical case from
[
11
]
is the non-zero RHS in the system of linear inhomogeneous algebraic equations (
1.94
), corresponding to eq. (39) in the original article.

We separate the analysis of the reduced system into several cases with respect to the rank of the matrix
M
, following
[
11
]
. It can be either
3
,
2
or
1
. Rank
0
would mean that all the auxiliary functions vanish and with them the magnetic field as well, see
eq.

1.88
, so we rule this case out.

If the rank is
3
, then the determinant of
M
,

\det(M)=4r^{9}\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\mu(Z)\sigma(r),

(1.96)

is not zero and it implies a unique solution for each first derivative of
W
. The analysis of this case from
[
11
]
shows that this assumption leads to a contradiction and the reduced system is inconsistent. The analysis remains valid because it uses the matrix
M
and equations (
1.91
) and (
1.92
) only, which are unaffected by the quantum correction.

If the rank is either
2
or
1
instead, then
\det(M)=0
, and from
eq.

1.96
we see that there are
a priori
three possible cases:

a)

\psi^{\prime}(\phi)=0
,

b)

\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\neq 0
and
\mu(Z)=0
,

c)

\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\neq 0
,
\mu(Z)\neq 0
and
\sigma(r)=0
. We rule this case out due to inconsistency with
eq.

1.92
.

The assumption of case a) implies that the quantum correction vanishes; thus, we obtain the classical systems from
[
11
]
. Therefore, we continue with the case b) only, i.e.
\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\neq 0
and
\mu(Z)=0
. (The key results for case a) are summarised in the corresponding subsections of Section
2.1
, where we search for additional integrals of motion.)

Before we go to the specific subcases, we show that
\alpha=0
for
\alpha
from
section

1.4
. In the considered case it follows from
eq.

1.91
and
eq.

1.92
only, so the considerations are the same as in
[
11
]
:

Assuming
\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\neq 0
and
\mu(Z)=0
,
eq.

1.92
is satisfied trivially. By differentiating
eq.

1.91
with respect to
r
, we obtain

\left(r^{3}\sigma^{\prime}(r)\right)^{\prime}=\frac{\tau^{\prime}(\phi)}{\psi^{\prime}(\phi)}\left(r\rho(r)\right)^{\prime}.

(1.97)

This leads to 3 possibilities:

•

\left(r^{3}\sigma^{\prime}(r)\right)^{\prime}=\left(r\rho(r)\right)^{\prime}=0
, i.e.

\sigma(r)=\frac{C_{\sigma}}{r^{2}}+\tilde{C}_{\sigma},\qquad\rho(r)=\frac{C_{\rho}}{r}.

(1.98)

Substituting
eq.

1.98
into
eq.

1.91
we find

2\left(\tau(\phi)-C_{\sigma}\right)\psi^{\prime}(\phi)+\left(\psi(\phi)-C_{\rho}\right)\tau^{\prime}(\phi)=0

(1.99)

which directly implies that
\alpha
defined in
section

1.4
vanishes.

•

\left(r^{3}\sigma^{\prime}(r)\right)^{\prime}=\tau^{\prime}(\phi)=0
, i.e.

\sigma(r)=\frac{C_{\sigma}}{r^{2}}+\tilde{C}_{\sigma},\qquad\tau(\phi)=C_{\tau}.

(1.100)

Substituting
eq.

1.100
into
eq.

1.91
we find
C_{\sigma}=C_{\tau}
and that together with
eq.

1.100
implies again that
\alpha=0
in
section

1.4
.

•

\frac{\left(r^{3}\sigma^{\prime}(r)\right)^{\prime}}{\left(r\rho(r)\right)^{\prime}}=\frac{\tau^{\prime}(\phi)}{\psi^{\prime}(\phi)}=\lambda\neq 0,
implying that

\rho(r)=\frac{1}{\lambda}r^{2}\sigma^{\prime}(r)+\frac{C_{\rho}}{r},\qquad\tau(\phi)=\lambda\psi(\phi)+C_{\tau}.

(1.101)

Substituting
eq.

1.101
into
eq.

1.91
and differentiating it with respect to
\phi
we arrive at a contradiction with our assumptions, namely
\lambda\psi^{\prime}(\phi)=0
.

1.4.1
Case b)
\mathrm{rank}\,(M)=2
:
\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\neq 0
and
\mu(Z)=0

Here the second and third equations in (
1.4
) reduce to

\partial_{rZ}W=0,\quad\partial_{\phi Z}W=0,

(1.102)

implying separation of the
Z
coordinate from
r
and
\phi
in the scalar potential
W
, i.e.

W(r,\phi,Z)=W_{12}(r,\phi)+W_{3}(Z).

(1.103)

The reduced row echelon form of the extended matrix of the system of equations (
1.94
) reads

\begin{pmatrix}r\psi^{\prime}(\phi)&r\rho(r)-\psi(\phi)&0&-\frac{\hbar^{2}(\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)+\psi^{\prime}(\phi))}{4r^{2}}\\
0&0&\sigma(r)&0\\
0&0&\tau(\phi)&0\end{pmatrix}

(1.104)

Our assumption
\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\neq 0
implies that we have two possibilities to have
\mathrm{rank}\,(M)=2
, namely either
\sigma(r)\neq 0
or
\tau(\phi)\neq 0
. Both of them imply
W_{3}(Z)^{\prime}=0
and without loss of generality we can absorb the constant into redefinition of
W_{12}
, so we have
W(r,\phi,Z)=W_{12}(r,\phi)\equiv W(r,\phi)
.

1)

\sigma(r)\neq 0
:
We follow the further splitting into the subcases from
[
11
]
.
We use our assumption
\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\neq 0
to rewrite
eq.

1.91
in the following way:

r^{3}\sigma^{\prime}(r)+2\tau(\phi)-\frac{\tau^{\prime}(\phi)}{\psi^{\prime}(\phi)}(r\rho(r)-\psi(\phi))=0.

(1.105)

Differentiation with respect to
\phi
leads to the equation:

3\tau^{\prime}(\phi)+\psi(\phi)\frac{\tau^{\prime\prime}(\phi)\psi^{\prime}(\phi)-\tau^{\prime}(\phi)\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)}{\psi^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}}=r\rho(r)\frac{\tau^{\prime\prime}(\phi)\psi^{\prime}(\phi)-\tau^{\prime}(\phi)\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)}{\psi^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}}.

(1.106)

If
\tau^{\prime\prime}(\phi)\psi^{\prime}(\phi)-\tau^{\prime}(\phi)\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)\neq 0
, we can separate the variables
r
and
\phi
, else the expression vanishes and we conclude from
eq.

1.106
that
\tau^{\prime}(\phi)=0
, thus
\tau(\phi)=\tau_{0}
is a constant. We treat the subcases separately.

1.1)

\tau^{\prime\prime}(\phi)\psi^{\prime}(\phi)-\tau^{\prime}(\phi)\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)=0
, so
\tau(\phi)=\tau_{0}
: From
eq.

1.91
, which now reads
r^{3}\sigma^{\prime}(r)=-2\tau_{0}
, follows

\sigma(r)=\frac{\tau_{0}}{r^{2}}+\sigma_{0}.

(1.107)

The yet unsolved equations from eq. (
1.91
)–(
1.94
) constraining the scalar potential
W
read

\displaystyle r\psi^{\prime}(\phi)W_{r}+\left(r\rho(r)-\psi(\phi)\right)W_{\phi}+\frac{\hbar^{2}(\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)+\psi^{\prime}(\phi))}{4r^{2}}

\displaystyle=0,

(1.108)

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\left(r^{3}\rho^{\prime\prime}(r)-r^{2}\rho^{\prime}(r)+r\rho(r)-3\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)-4\psi(\phi)\right)+

\displaystyle+\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)\left(r\rho(r)-\psi(\phi)\right)-4r^{5}W_{r\phi}-8r^{4}W_{\phi}

\displaystyle=0.

(1.109)

The magnetic field takes the form

B^{\phi}=0,\quad B^{r}=0,\quad B^{Z}=-\frac{\rho^{\prime}(r)}{2}-\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+\psi(\phi)}{2r^{2}}.

(1.110)

Thus, we have a free motion in the
z
-direction (in the direction of the
z
-axis) and motion in the
xy
-plane under the influence of the scalar potential
W(r,\phi)
constrained by
eq.

1.108
and
eq.

1.109
and the perpendicular magnetic field
B^{Z}(r,\phi)
. Such 2D problem was discussed by McSween and Winternitz
[
22
]
in classical mechanics and by Bérubé and Winternitz
[
5
]
in quantum mechanics.

For both motions we have one integral of motion in addition to the separable Hamiltonian, namely the integral
X_{1}
for the motion in the
xy
-plane, which we list shortly together with corresponding magnetic field
B
and potential
W
, and
X_{2}
for the
z
-direction. The integral
X_{2}
reduces to the first order one

\tilde{X}_{2}=p_{Z}^{A}+\frac{\sigma_{0}}{2},

(1.111)

which in a suitably chosen gauge reads
p_{Z}
.

In
[
5
]
it was shown that there are 2 solutions to equations (
1.108
) and (
1.109
) for
\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\neq 0
, in both cases the magnetic field
B^{Z}
does not depend on
Z
. Omitting the lengthy details, we present the results only.

i.

The magnetic field independent of
\phi
and
Z
corresponds to

\begin{split}\psi(\phi)&=\psi_{0}+\psi_{1}\cos(\phi)+\psi_{2}\sin(\phi),\\
\rho(r)&=3\rho_{2}r^{4}-\rho_{1}r^{2}+\rho_{0}\end{split}

(1.112)

which means that this quantum system is the same as the classical (the correction vanishes).
The resulting magnetic field
B^{Z}
and potential
W
are

\begin{split}B^{Z}&=-6\rho_{2}r^{3}+\rho_{1}r,\\
W&=-2\rho_{2}r(\psi_{1}\cos(\phi)+\psi_{2}\sin(\phi))-\rho_{2}^{2}r^{6}+\frac{\rho_{2}\rho_{1}}{2}r^{4}-\rho_{2}W_{0}r^{2}.\end{split}

(1.113)

The corresponding integral of motion in the Cartesian coordinates is

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} X_{1}={}&(L_{z}^{A})^{2}+(3\rho_{2}r^{4}-\rho_{1}r^{2}+W_{0})L_{z}^{A}-\psi_{2}p_{x}^{A}+\psi_{1}p_{y}^{A}+\\
&+\psi_{1}(2\rho_{2}r^{2}-\rho_{1})x+\psi_{2}(2\rho_{2}r^{2}-\rho_{1})y+\\
&+\frac{(3\rho_{2}r^{4}-\rho_{1}r^{2}+2W_{0})(3\rho_{2}r^{2}-\rho_{1})r^{2}}{4},\end{aligned}

(1.114)

where we write
r=\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}
for brevity.

ii.

The magnetic field depends on
\phi
as well as
r
. We have

\rho(r)=\frac{\rho_{0}}{r}.

(1.115)

This leads to the special case of the following subcase
1)1.2)
with
\tau_{1}=0
. We therefore refer the reader there for details and list the results only.

The magnetic field
B
together with the potential
W
read

\displaystyle B^{r}={}

\displaystyle 0,\quad B^{\phi}={}0,\quad B^{Z}=\frac{2\beta_{1}\beta(\phi)^{2}+\beta_{2}}{4r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{5}},

(1.116)

\displaystyle W={}

\displaystyle\frac{W_{0}}{r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{2}}-\frac{\beta_{2}}{32\beta(\phi)^{4}r^{4}}+\hbar^{2}\frac{f_{1}\beta(\phi)^{4}-12\beta_{1}\beta(\phi)^{2}-5\beta_{2}}{32r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{6}},

(1.117)

where
\beta_{1}
,
\beta_{2}
are constants and
\beta(\phi)=\psi(\phi)-\rho_{0}
must satisfy

4\beta(\phi)^{4}\beta^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}+4\beta(\phi)^{6}-4\beta_{1}\beta(\phi)^{2}+f_{1}\beta(\phi)^{4}=\beta_{2}.

(1.118)

The corresponding integral
X_{1}
is defined by

\displaystyle s_{1}^{r}={}

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{4\beta_{1}\beta(\phi)^{2}+\beta_{2}-4\beta(\phi)^{6}-\beta(\phi)^{4}f_{1}}}{2\beta(\phi)^{2}},

\displaystyle s_{1}^{\phi}={}

\displaystyle-\frac{\beta(\phi)}{r},\qquad s_{1}^{Z}=0,

(1.119)

\displaystyle m_{1}={}

\displaystyle\frac{2W_{0}}{\beta(\phi)^{2}}-\frac{2\beta_{1}\beta(\phi)^{2}+\beta_{2}}{8\beta(\phi)^{4}r^{2}}+\hbar^{2}\frac{f_{1}\beta(\phi)^{4}-12\beta_{1}\beta(\phi)^{2}-5\beta_{2}}{16\beta(\phi)^{6}}.

1.2)

\tau^{\prime\prime}(\phi)\psi^{\prime}(\phi)-\tau^{\prime}(\phi)\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)\neq 0
. Separating the variables
r
and
\phi
in
eq.

1.106
,

\frac{3\tau^{\prime}(\phi)\psi^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}}{\tau^{\prime\prime}(\phi)\psi^{\prime}(\phi)-\tau^{\prime}(\phi)\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)}+\psi(\phi)=\rho_{0}=r\rho(r)

(1.120)

with separation constant
\rho_{0}
, we obtain

\rho(r)=\frac{\rho_{0}}{r},\qquad\tau(\phi)=\tau_{0}+\frac{\tau_{1}}{(\psi(\phi)-\rho_{0})^{2}}.

(1.121)

Using these results in
eq.

1.91
, we get
\sigma(r)=\frac{\tau_{0}}{r^{2}}+\sigma_{0}
.

There are two unsolved equations among eq. (
1.4
)–(
1.94
). Simplifying them using the previous results, we get

\displaystyle r\psi^{\prime}(\phi)W_{r}+\left(\rho_{0}-\psi(\phi)\right)W_{\phi}+\hbar^{2}\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)+\psi^{\prime}(\phi)}{4r^{2}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle 3\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+4\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\left(\psi(\phi)-\rho_{0}\right)+\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)(\psi(\phi)-\rho_{0})+

(1.122)

\displaystyle+\frac{4\tau_{1}^{2}}{\left(\psi(\phi)-\rho_{0}\right)^{5}}\psi^{\prime}(\phi)+4r^{5}W_{r\phi}+8r^{4}W_{\phi}

\displaystyle=0.

We substitute
\beta(\phi)=\psi(\phi)-\rho_{0}
and integrate the second equation with respect to
\phi
to obtain

\displaystyle r\beta^{\prime}(\phi)W_{r}-\beta(\phi)W_{\phi}+\hbar^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)+\beta^{\prime}(\phi)}{4r^{2}}={}

\displaystyle 0,

(1.123)

\displaystyle\beta(\phi)\beta^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+\beta^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}+2\beta(\phi)^{2}-\frac{\tau_{1}^{2}}{\beta(\phi)^{4}}+

\displaystyle+4r^{5}W_{r}+8r^{4}W(r,\phi)+f(r)={}

\displaystyle 0,

(1.124)

where
f(r)
is an arbitrary function arising in the integration.

Substituting for
W_{r}
from
eq.

1.124
into
eq.

1.123
we find expressions for both
W_{r}
and
W_{\phi}
, where only the expression for
W_{\phi}
has the correction,

\partial_{\phi}W=-\frac{2\beta^{\prime}W}{\beta}-\frac{\beta^{\prime}(2\beta^{6}+\beta^{5}\beta^{\prime\prime}+\beta^{4}\beta^{\prime 2}+\beta^{4}f\left(r\right)-\tau_{1}^{2})}{4r^{4}\beta^{5}}+\hbar^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime\prime}+\beta^{\prime}}{4r^{2}\beta}.

(1.125)

Substituting them into
item

1)1.2)
we obtain the following equation

\beta^{\prime}(\phi)\left(12\beta(\phi)^{2}+7\beta\beta^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+4\beta^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}-f^{\prime}(r)r+4f(r)\right)+\beta(\phi)^{2}\beta^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)=0,

(1.126)

which we differentiate by
r
and get

f(r)=\frac{f_{1}}{4}+f_{2}r^{4},

(1.127)

where
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
are integration constants. With the form of
f(r)
from
eq.

1.127
, we can determine the dependence of the scalar potential
W
on
r
from
eq.

1.124
, treating the yet unknown function
\beta(\phi)
as a parameter:

W=-\frac{f_{2}}{8}+\frac{\tilde{W}(\phi)}{r^{2}}+\frac{\beta(\phi)\beta^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+\beta^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}+\frac{f_{1}}{4}-\frac{\tau_{1}^{2}}{\beta(\phi)^{4}}+2\beta(\phi)^{2}}{8r^{4}}.

(1.128)

We determine the function
\tilde{W}(\phi)
from an equation obtained by inserting
W
from eq. (
1.128
) into
eq.

1.125
and subtracting
eq.

1.126
with
f(r)
from
eq.

1.127
, i.e. we subtract

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}(\phi)\left(7\beta(\phi)\beta^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+4\beta^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}+12\beta(\phi)^{2}+f_{1}\right)+\beta(\phi)^{2}\beta^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)=0.

(1.129)

The resulting equation

\frac{\hbar^{2}\beta^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)+(\hbar^{2}-8\tilde{W}(\phi))\beta^{\prime}(\phi)-4(\tilde{W}^{\prime}(\phi))\beta(\phi)}{4r^{2}\beta(\phi)}=0

(1.130)

yields

\tilde{W}(\phi)=\frac{W_{0}}{\beta(\phi)^{2}}+\hbar^{2}\frac{2\beta(\phi)\beta^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+\beta(\phi)^{2}-\beta^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}}{8\beta(\phi)^{2}},

(1.131)

where
W_{0}
is an arbitrary constant. The final form of the scalar potential after a constant shift eliminating
-\frac{f_{1}}{8}
is

\begin{split}W={}&\frac{W_{0}}{r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{2}}+\frac{\beta(\phi)\beta^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+\beta^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}+\frac{f_{1}}{4}-\frac{\tau_{1}^{2}}{\beta(\phi)^{4}}+2\beta(\phi)^{2}}{8r^{4}}+\\
&+\hbar^{2}\frac{2\beta(\phi)\beta^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+\beta(\phi)^{2}-\beta^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}}{8r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{2}}.\end{split}

(1.132)

We have therefore almost explicit form of the potential, the only undetermined function is
\beta(\phi)
, which must satisfy the only remaining equation (
1.129
). We reduce its order as follows
[
11
]
: we multiply by
\beta(\phi)
and integrate, then multiply by
\beta^{\prime}(\phi)\beta(\phi)
and integrate again. The result is

4\beta(\phi)^{4}\beta^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}+4\beta(\phi)^{6}-4\beta_{1}\beta(\phi)^{2}+f_{1}\beta(\phi)^{4}=\beta_{2}

(1.133)

where
\beta_{1},

\beta_{2}
are the constants of integration. According to
[
5
]
,
eq.

1.133
can be written as a quadrature expressing the independent variable
\phi
as a function of
\beta
in terms of elliptic integrals but the authors analyse further some special cases only. For more details see the cited article, we will later consider only the special case
\beta_{2}=0
.

We can use
eq.

1.133
to further simplify the scalar potential (
1.132
)

W=\frac{W_{0}}{r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{2}}-\frac{(4\tau_{1}^{2}+\beta_{2})}{32r^{4}\beta(\phi)^{4}}+\hbar^{2}\frac{f_{1}\beta(\phi)^{4}-12\beta_{1}\beta(\phi)^{2}-5\beta_{2}}{32r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{6}}.

(1.134)

The magnetic field is also expressed in terms of the function
\beta(\phi)
and its derivatives

\displaystyle B^{r}=-\frac{\tau_{1}\beta^{\prime}(\phi)}{r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{3}},\quad B^{\phi}=\frac{\tau_{1}}{r^{3}\beta(\phi)^{2}},\quad B^{Z}=-\frac{\beta(\phi)+\beta^{\prime\prime}(\phi)}{2r^{2}},

(1.135)

or substituting for the derivatives from eq. (
1.129
) and its integrated form eq. (
1.133
)

\displaystyle B^{r}={}

\displaystyle-\tau_{1}\frac{\sqrt{4\beta_{1}\beta(\phi)^{2}+\beta_{2}-4\beta(\phi)^{6}-f_{1}\beta(\phi)^{4}}}{2r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{5}},

\displaystyle B^{\phi}={}

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{1}}{r^{3}\beta(\phi)^{2}},\quad B^{Z}=\frac{2\beta_{1}\beta(\phi)^{2}+\beta_{2}}{4r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{5}}.

(1.136)

(The square root appears due to substitution for
\beta^{\prime}(\phi)
from eq. (
1.133
), so the sign depends on the choice of the branch in that equation.)

We note that eq. (
1.133
) is the same in classical and quantum cases. This implies that the magnetic field is the same in both cases, only the scalar potential
W
obtains an
\hbar^{2}
-proportional correction depending on
\beta
.

The integrals from
eq.

1.76
are determined by

\displaystyle s_{1}^{r}={}

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{4\beta_{1}\beta(\phi)^{2}+\beta_{2}-4\beta(\phi)^{6}-\beta(\phi)^{4}f_{1}}}{2\beta(\phi)^{2}},

\displaystyle s_{1}^{\phi}={}

\displaystyle-\frac{\beta(\phi)}{r},\qquad s_{1}^{Z}=\tau_{0}+\frac{\tau_{1}}{\beta(\phi)^{2}},

\displaystyle m_{1}={}

\displaystyle\frac{2W_{0}}{\beta(\phi)^{2}}-\frac{4\beta(\phi)^{2}\tau_{0}\tau_{1}+2\beta_{1}\beta(\phi)^{2}+4\tau_{1}^{2}+\beta_{2}}{8\beta(\phi)^{4}r^{2}}+

\displaystyle+\hbar^{2}\frac{f_{1}\beta(\phi)^{4}-12\beta_{1}\beta(\phi)^{2}-5\beta_{2}}{16r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{6}},

(1.137)

\displaystyle s_{2}^{r}={}

\displaystyle 0,\qquad s_{2}^{\phi}=0,\qquad s_{2}^{Z}=\sigma_{0}-\frac{\tau_{1}}{r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{2}},

\displaystyle m_{2}={}

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{1}}{\beta(\phi)^{2}r^{2}}\left(\frac{\tau_{1}}{4\beta(\phi)^{2}r^{2}}-\frac{\sigma_{0}}{2}\right).

Writing the integral
X_{2}
in full, we get

X_{2}=\left(p_{Z}^{A}-\frac{\tau_{1}}{2r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{2}}\right)^{2}+\sigma_{0}\left(p_{Z}^{A}-\frac{\tau_{1}}{2r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{2}}\right).

(1.138)

so this integral of motion reduces to the first order one

\tilde{X}_{2}=p_{Z}^{A}-\frac{\tau_{1}}{2r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{2}}

(1.139)

and is in suitable gauge
X_{2}=p_{Z}
, as can be checked in eq. (
1.135
).

We can rewrite
eq.

1.133
using a substitution
\gamma(\phi)=\beta(\phi)^{2}
to obtain

\gamma(\phi)\gamma^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}+4\gamma(\phi)^{3}-4\beta_{1}\gamma(\phi)+f_{1}\gamma(\phi)^{2}=\beta_{2}.

(1.140)

Let us analyse the special case
\beta_{2}=0
. We obtain the following solution:

\gamma(\phi)=\frac{\sqrt{64\beta_{1}+f_{1}^{2}}\sin\left(2(\phi-\phi_{0})\right)-f_{1}}{8}.

(1.141)

Under the assumptions that
f_{1}<0
,
-\frac{f_{1}^{2}}{64}<\beta_{1}<0
, the corresponding
\beta(\phi)
is well defined, bounded and positive,

\beta(\phi)=\sqrt{\frac{\sqrt{64\beta_{1}+f_{1}^{2}}\sin\left(2(\phi-\phi_{0})\right)-f_{1}}{8}}.

(1.142)

The magnetic field now reads:

\displaystyle B^{r}={}

\displaystyle-\frac{8\tau_{1}\sqrt{64\beta_{1}+f_{1}^{2}}\cos\left(2(\phi-\phi_{0})\right)}{r^{2}\left(\sqrt{64\beta_{1}+f_{1}^{2}}\sin\left(2(\phi-\phi_{0})\right)-f_{1}\right)^{2}},

\displaystyle B^{\phi}={}

\displaystyle\frac{8\tau_{1}}{r^{3}\left(\sqrt{64\beta_{1}+f_{1}^{2}}\sin\left(2(\phi-\phi_{0})\right)-f_{1}\right)},

(1.143)

\displaystyle B^{Z}={}

\displaystyle\frac{\beta_{1}}{2r^{2}}\left(\frac{\sqrt{64\beta_{1}+f_{1}^{2}}\sin\left(2(\phi-\phi_{0})\right)-f_{1}}{8}\right)^{-\frac{3}{2}}

For solution from
eq.

1.141
we find

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=\frac{8W_{0}}{r^{2}\left(a\sin\left(2(\phi-\phi_{0})\right)-f_{1}\right)}-\frac{8\tau_{1}^{2}}{r^{4}\left(a\sin\left(2(\phi-\phi_{0})\right)-f_{1}\right)^{2}}+

\displaystyle+\hbar^{2}\frac{a^{2}f_{1}(\sin(2(\phi-\phi_{0}))^{2}+1)-2a^{3}\sin(2(\phi-\phi_{0}))+32\beta_{1}f_{1}-320\beta_{2}}{4r^{2}\left(a\sin(2(\phi-\phi_{0}))-f_{1}\right)^{3}},

(1.144)

where
a=\sqrt{f_{1}^{2}+64\beta_{1}}
.

Choosing the coordinates so that
\phi_{0}=0
and transforming the magnetic field into the Cartesian coordinates using
section

1.2
, we get

\displaystyle B^{x}(\vec{x})

\displaystyle=-\frac{8\tau_{1}\left(\sqrt{f_{1}^{2}+64\beta_{1}}x-f_{1}y\right)}{\left(f_{1}(x^{2}+y^{2})-2\sqrt{f_{1}^{2}+64\beta_{1}}xy\right)^{2}},

(1.145)

\displaystyle B^{y}(\vec{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{8\tau_{1}\left(\sqrt{f_{1}^{2}+64\beta_{1}}y-f_{1}x\right)}{\left(f_{1}(x^{2}+y^{2})-2\sqrt{f_{1}^{2}+64\beta_{1}}xy\right)^{2}},

(1.146)

\displaystyle B^{z}(\vec{x})

\displaystyle=-\frac{8\sqrt{2}\beta_{1}}{\left(f_{1}(x^{2}+y^{2})-2\sqrt{f_{1}^{2}+64\beta_{1}}xy\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}},

(1.147)

\displaystyle W(\vec{x})

\displaystyle=-\frac{8W_{0}\left(f_{1}(x^{2}+y^{2})-2\sqrt{f_{1}^{2}+64\beta_{1}}xy\right)+8\tau_{1}^{2}}{\left(f_{1}(x^{2}+y^{2})-2\sqrt{f_{1}^{2}+64\beta_{1}}xy\right)^{2}}+

(1.148)

\displaystyle+\hbar^{2}\frac{(r^{4}+4x^{2}y^{2})f_{1}^{3}-4a^{3}r^{2}xy+\beta_{1}f_{1}(96r^{4}+256y^{2}x^{2})-320r^{4}\beta_{2}}{4(f_{1}r^{2}-2ayx)^{3}}.

2)

\tau(\phi)\neq 0
: We can use considerations from the previous case as in the classical case, because we have never divided by
\sigma(r)
, which may now vanish, and never assumed
\tau(\phi)=0
. We only need some constraints on the constants to assure
\tau(\phi)\neq 0
. We therefore obtain the system which splits into independent motions in
xy
-plane and
z
-direction (the motion in 2D was considered in
[
5
]
) with magnetic field from
eq.

1.113
and the system with the magnetic field from
item

1)1.2)
and scalar potential from
eq.

1.134
. (If
\tau_{1}=0
in the second system, we get the separating system from
eq.

1.116
in subcase
1)(1.1))i.
)

1.4.2
Case b)
\mathrm{rank}\,(M)=1
:
\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\neq 0
and
\mu(Z)=0

From
section

1.4
follows the separation of the scalar potential

W(r,\phi,Z)=W_{12}(r,\phi)+W_{3}(Z).

(1.149)

Let us have a look at extended matrix
eq.

1.104
for the system of equations (
1.94
). For the (non-extended) matrix of the system
M
to have rank 1, the auxiliary functions
\tau(\phi)
and
\sigma(r)
must be zero, therefore
W_{3}(Z)
remains unconstrained, which is in contrast with respect to rank
(M)=2
.
The remaining equations of the system (
1.91
)–(
1.94
) are the same as
eq.

1.108
and
eq.

1.109
, which read

\displaystyle r\psi^{\prime}(\phi)W_{r}+\left(r\rho(r)-\psi(\phi)\right)W_{\phi}+\hbar^{2}\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)+\psi^{\prime}(\phi)}{4r^{2}}=0,

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\left(r^{3}\rho^{\prime\prime}(r)-r^{2}\rho^{\prime}(r)+r\rho(r)-3\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)-4\psi(\phi)\right)+

(1.150)

\displaystyle+\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)\left(r\rho(r)-\psi(\phi)\right)-4r^{5}W_{r\phi}-8r^{4}W_{\phi}=0.

The magnetic field reads

B^{r}=0,\quad B^{\phi}=0,\quad B^{Z}=-\frac{1}{2r^{2}}\left(\rho^{\prime}(r)r^{2}+\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+\psi(\phi)\right).

(1.151)

We have therefore obtained a separable motion – motion in the
xy
-plane under the influence of the scalar potential
W(r,\phi)
, which has a quantum correction, plus the perpendicular magnetic field
B^{Z}(r,\phi)
and the motion in the direction of the
z
-axis under the influence of the scalar potential
W_{3}(Z)
, which is not constrained, in contrast with the case of rank
(M)=2
. The
xy
-plane motion was analysed in
[
5
]
and
[
22
]
. For both motions we have one integral of motion in addition to the separable Hamiltonian, namely integral
X_{1}
for the motion in the
xy
-plane and
X_{2}
for the
z
-direction.

There are 2 systems in 2D that are interesting for us in this case
[
5
]
. They are those in case
1)1.1)
, so namely with the potential
W_{12}(r,\phi)
and magnetic field
B
from
eq.

1.113
and
eq.

1.116
, the form of the integral
X_{1}
can be seen under the cited equations. The
Z
component of the potential,
W_{3}(Z)
, remains unconstrained and the corresponding integral of motion
X_{2}
will not reduce to a linear one,

X_{2}=(p_{Z}^{A})^{2}+2W_{3}(Z),

(1.152)

as can be seen from
section

1.4
, where all
s^{i}
vanish.

To sum up: In this section we obtained all quantum quadratically integrable systems of cylindrical type by solving the corresponding determining equations. We followed the analysis from the classical case
[
11
]
because only one of the equations differs form the classical case, namely eq. (
1.82
). The determining equations were reduced to equations (
1.91
)–(
1.94
) containing 5 auxiliary functions of one variable
\rho(r)
,
\sigma(r)
,
\psi(\phi)
,
\tau(\phi)
and
\mu(Z)
which determine the functions
s^{i}
and
B^{i}
, see eq. (
1.87
) and (
1.88
).

Equation (
1.94
) contains a matrix which depends on the auxiliary functions only and allows us to split the considerations according to its rank. In
[
11
]
it was shown that rank 0 and 3 are either impossible (assuming a non-vanishing magnetic field) or inconsistent and the arguments remain valid in quantum mechanics. Ranks 1 and 2 split further into subcases a)
\psi^{\prime}(\phi)=0
and b)
\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\neq 0
,
\mu(Z)=0
. Case a) implies vanishing of the quantum correction and the obtained systems are therefore the same as in classical mechanics. Those were not analysed further here, and we refer the reader to
[
11
]
. (The key results are cited in the corresponding subsections of Section
2.1
, where we search for the first order superintegrable system among them.) In case b) the quantum correction is
a priori
non-trivial, but vanishes due to the consistency conditions on the scalar potential
W
in case
1)1.1)
subcase
1)(1.1))i.
and the corresponding subcase in this subsection. The analysis shows that the remaining subcases have the same magnetic field as their classical counterparts and only the scalar potential
W
is modified by a
\hbar^{2}
-proportional correction.
