In all cases there is at least one free parameter, a constant or even a function. In the next chapter we search for values of the constants and functions so that the system admits additional integrals of motion, which makes the system superintegrable.

Chapter 2
Found superintegrable systems

After the analysis of the cylindrical integrable systems both in classical mechanics
[
11
]
and in quantum mechanics (Section
1.4
) has been completed, we go one step further, namely look for superintegrable systems among the integrable ones.

The goal is to find additional integrals of motion. We use the usual
ansatz
that the integrals of motion are first or second order polynomials in momenta. We tried to solve the second order case in general, but we encountered considerable computational difficulties. Although the third order equations (
1.2
) do depend on neither magnetic nor electric field and can be solved, see eq. (
1.20
)–(
1.23
) for the solution, we still have to solve 10 differential equations (
1.2
)–(
1.17
) with 20 arbitrary constants
\alpha_{ij}
from eq. (
1.20
)–(
1.23
) and 4 unknown functions
s^{r},

s^{\phi},

s^{Z},

m
of 3 variables
r,

\phi,

Z
each in addition to the magnetic field
B
and scalar potential
W
(simplified by assuming integrability).

Despite a lot of effort, we obtained a rather limited results by analysing the second order case in general, which we present in Subsection
2.2.1
. (We obtained some first order systems here as well). Therefore, we have to be less ambitious and consider the first order case (Section
2.1
) and a physically motivated second order
ansatz
, namely
L^{2}+\ldots
in Subsection
2.2.2
and
L_{x}p_{y}-L_{y}p_{x}+\ldots
in Subsection
2.2.3
. We solve these in a systematic way, albeit the second order systems in classical mechanics only because the quantum corrections for these integrals are non-trivial.

2.1
First order integrals

2.1.1
General considerations

Before we start analysing the cases, let us outline the general procedure and prove that the first order integrals can contain neither
L_{x}
nor
L_{y}
.

The task is to solve equations (
1.27
)–(
1.29
), where we substitute the magnetic field from eq. (
1.88
) with the form of the auxiliary functions corresponding to the considered case.

We start from the highest order equations (
1.27
), which do not depend on the magnetic field nor the scalar potential and can be solved in general. As we have already mentioned in Section
1.2
, the solution (
1.30
) implies that the first order terms are in the Euclidean Lie algebra generated by

p_{x},\ p_{y},\ p_{z},\ L_{x},\ L_{y},\ L_{z},

(2.1)

the solution therefore depends on
6
constants
k_{1},\ldots,

k_{6}.
We associate the constants with the gauge invariant form of generators of the Euclidean algebra, namely

Y=k_{1}p_{x}^{A}+k_{2}p_{y}^{A}+k_{3}p_{z}^{A}+k_{4}L_{x}^{A}+k_{5}L_{y}^{A}+k_{6}L_{z}^{A}+m(x,y,z).

(2.2)

We next solve the first order equations (
1.2
) with the solution (
1.30
) substituted. The best way to proceed is to assume that the function
m
is smooth enough and impose the Clairaut compatibility conditions
\partial_{ab}m=\partial_{ba}m
on the second derivatives of
m(r,\phi,Z)
. Taking into account the solution (
1.30
) to the second order equations (
1.27
) and the form of magnetic field in terms of the auxiliary functions (
1.88
), the compatibility equations read

\displaystyle[(k_{4}Z-k_{2})\cos(\phi)+(k_{5}Z+k_{1})\sin(\phi)-k_{6}r](\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)+\psi^{\prime}(\phi))+

\displaystyle+3[(k_{5}Z+k_{1})\cos(\phi)-(k_{4}Z-k_{2})\sin(\phi)]\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+

\displaystyle+[(k_{5}Z+k_{1})\cos(\phi)-(k_{4}Z-k_{2})\sin(\phi)]r^{2}(\rho^{\prime}(r)-r\rho^{\prime\prime}(r))+

(2.3)

\displaystyle+[-3k_{5}r\cos(\phi)+3k_{4}r\sin(\phi)+2k_{3}]r^{4}\mu^{\prime}(Z)+

\displaystyle+r(k_{5}\cos(\phi)-k_{4}\sin(\phi))\tau^{\prime}(\phi)-r^{4}(k_{4}\cos(\phi)+k_{5}\sin(\phi))\sigma^{\prime}(r)+

\displaystyle+3[(k_{5}Z+k_{1})\cos(\phi)-(k_{4}Z-k_{2})\sin(\phi)]\psi(\phi)-

\displaystyle-2[k_{4}\cos(\phi)+k_{5}\sin(\phi)]r\tau(\phi)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle[-(k_{5}Z+k_{1})\cos(\phi)+(k_{4}Z-k_{2})\sin(\phi)]r^{4}\sigma^{\prime\prime}(r)+

\displaystyle+(k_{4}\cos(\phi)+k_{5}\sin(\phi))r\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+

\displaystyle+[3(k_{4}Z-k_{2})\cos(\phi)+3(k_{5}Z+k_{1})\sin(\phi)-2k_{6}r]\tau^{\prime}(\phi)-

\displaystyle-((k_{4}Z-k_{2})\cos(\phi)+\sin(\phi)(k_{5}Z+k_{1}))r^{4}\mu^{\prime}(Z)+

(2.4)

\displaystyle+[k_{4}\cos(\phi)+k_{5}\sin(\phi)]r^{3}\rho^{\prime}(r)+

\displaystyle+6[(k_{5}Z+k_{1})\cos(\phi)-(k_{4}Z-k_{2})\sin(\phi)]\tau(\phi)-

\displaystyle-[k_{4}\cos(\phi)+k_{5}\sin(\phi)]r(2r^{3}\mu(Z)-\psi(\phi))

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle[(k_{4}Z-k_{2})\cos(\phi)+(k_{5}Z+k_{1})\sin(\phi)-k_{6}r]\tau^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+

\displaystyle+(-k_{5}r\cos(\phi)+k_{4}r\sin(\phi)+k_{3})r^{5}\mu^{\prime\prime}(Z)-

\displaystyle-(k_{5}\cos(\phi)-k_{4}\sin(\phi))r\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+

\displaystyle+3[(k_{5}Z+k_{1})\cos(\phi)-\sin(\phi)(k_{4}Z-k_{2})]\tau^{\prime}(\phi)-

\displaystyle-[(k_{4}Z-k_{2})\cos(\phi)+(k_{5}Z+k_{1})\sin(\phi)]r^{3}\sigma^{\prime}(r)+

(2.5)

\displaystyle+[(k_{5}Z+k_{1})\cos(\phi)-(k_{4}Z-k_{2})\sin(\phi)]r^{4}\mu^{\prime}(Z)-

\displaystyle-4(k_{5}\cos(\phi)-k_{2}\sin(\phi))(r^{3}\rho^{\prime}(r))-

\displaystyle-2[(k_{4}Z+k_{2})\cos(\phi)+(k_{5}Z+k_{1})\sin(\phi)]\tau(\phi)-

\displaystyle-[k_{4}\sin(\phi)-k_{5}\cos(\phi)]r(2r^{3}\mu(Z)-\psi(\phi))

\displaystyle=0.

(We have multiplied the equations by the power of
r
which was originally in the denominator. Because the equations must be satisfied for all
r
,
\phi
,
Z
, we implicitly assume
r\neq 0
and we do these simplifications automatically, often without mentioning them.)

Although the equations are long, they are manageable because they can be separated by taking derivatives with respect to
r
and/or
Z
(and dividing/multiplying by
r
).

There is one thing that can be proved using the equations above only, i.e. for all integrable cases: If we assume that the magnetic field is real and does not vanish, these systems do not admit the first order integrals corresponding to the constants
k_{4}
,
k_{5}
, namely
L_{x}
and
L_{y}
. We prove this fact by contradiction and at the same time illustrate the procedure used in the following subsections, which consists in repeated solving of equations with the highest powers of
Z
and
r
(
\mu(Z)
reduces to a polynomial) and substituting the results.

To prove the previous assertion, we assume that at least one of the constants
k_{4}
and
k_{5}
does not vanish and we show it implies zero or complex magnetic field. Taking the second derivative with respect to
Z
of equation (
2.3
), we obtain (dividing by
r^{4}
)

[-3k_{5}r\cos(\phi)+3k_{4}r\sin(\phi)+2k_{3}]\mu^{\prime\prime\prime}(Z)=0.

(2.6)

Differentiating it with respect to
r
, our assumption together with the fact that the equation must be satisfied for all
r,

\phi,

Z
implies

\mu(Z)=\mu_{2}Z^{2}+\mu_{1}Z+\mu_{0}.

(2.7)

Inserting it into eq. (
2.4
)–(
2.5
) and differentiating twice with respect to
Z
, we obtain

k_{5}\mu_{2}\sin(\phi)+k_{4}\mu_{2}\cos(\phi)=0,\quad k_{5}\mu_{2}\sin(\phi)-k_{4}\mu_{2}\cos(\phi)=0,

(2.8)

which imply linearity of
\mu(Z)
,
\mu_{2}=0
.

Using the result in eq. (
2.3
) differentiated with respect to
Z
and
r
, we obtain

k_{5}(r^{3}\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}(r)+2r^{2}\rho^{\prime\prime}(r)-2r\rho^{\prime}(r))\cos(\phi)-k_{4}(r^{3}\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}(r)+2r^{2}\rho^{\prime\prime}(r)-2r\rho^{\prime}(r))\sin(\phi)=0,

(2.9)

which has the following solution

\rho(r)=\rho_{0}+\frac{\rho_{1}}{r}+\rho_{2}r^{2}.

(2.10)

Differentiating eq. (
2.4
) with respect to
Z
and
r
, we get

(k_{5}r\sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}(r)+4k_{5}\sigma^{\prime\prime}(r)+12k_{4}\mu_{1})\cos(\phi)-(k_{4}r\sigma^{\prime\prime\prime}(r)+4k_{4}\sigma^{\prime\prime}(r)-12k_{5}\mu_{1})\sin(\phi)=0.

(2.11)

Solving the brackets separately, the solutions are consistent only if
\mu_{1}=0
and read

\sigma(r)=\frac{\sigma_{2}}{r^{2}}+\sigma_{1}r+\sigma_{0}.

(2.12)

Inserting
\sigma(r)
in into eq. (
2.5
), we get

\sigma_{1}\left(k_{4}\cos(\phi)+k_{5}\sin(\phi)\right)=0,

(2.13)

so we see that
\sigma_{1}=0
.

We now solve eq. (
2.3
)–(
2.5
) differentiated with respect to
Z
only to obtain
\psi(\phi)
and
\tau(\phi)
with the result

\displaystyle\psi(\phi)

\displaystyle=\rho_{1}+\frac{c_{1}\cos(2\phi)+c_{2}\sin(2\phi)+c_{3}}{2(k_{4}\cos(\phi)+k_{5}\sin(\phi))},

(2.14)

\displaystyle\tau(\phi)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{2}+\frac{2c_{4}-(k_{4}^{2}+k_{5}^{2})}{2(k_{4}\cos(\phi)+k_{5}\sin(\phi))^{2}}.

(2.15)

With this solution, the coefficient of
Z
in equations (
2.3
)–(
2.5
) vanishes, but the equations are not satisfied yet.
Differentiating eq. (
2.4
) with respect to
r
four times, we obtain

(\mu_{0}-\rho_{2})(k_{4}\cos(\phi)+k_{5}\sin(\phi))=0,

(2.16)

therefore
\rho_{2}=\mu_{0}
.

Inserting this result, we differentiate eq. (
2.3
)–(
2.5
) once with respect to
r
, take the numerators only and collect the coefficients of the polynomial in sines and cosines. Thus, we obtain a system of 7 algebraic equations:

\sigma_{2}k_{4}^{4}k_{5}+3(c_{1}-c_{3})k_{4}^{3}k_{6}+2(\sigma_{2}k_{5}^{2}+3c_{2}k_{6}-c_{4})k_{4}^{2}k_{5}-3(c_{1}+c_{3})k_{4}k_{5}^{2}k_{6}+\sigma_{2}k_{5}^{5}-2c_{4}k_{5}^{3}=0,

(2.17)

\sigma_{2}k_{4}^{5}+2(\sigma_{2}k_{5}^{2}-c_{4})k_{4}^{3}-3(c_{1}-c_{3})k_{4}^{2}k_{6}k_{5}+[\sigma_{2}k_{5}^{4}-2(3c_{2}k_{6}-c_{4})k_{5}^{2}]k_{4}+3(c_{1}+c_{3})k_{5}^{3}k_{6}=0,

(2.18)

2\sigma_{2}k_{4}^{3}k_{6}+(c_{3}-c_{1})k_{4}^{2}k_{5}+2[(\sigma_{2}k_{6}-c_{2})k_{5}^{2}-2c_{4}k_{6}]k_{4}+(c_{1}+c_{3})k_{5}^{3}=0,

(2.19)

(c_{3}-c_{1})k_{4}^{3}-2(\sigma_{2}k_{6}+c_{2})k_{4}^{2}k_{5}+(c_{1}+c_{3})k_{4}k_{5}^{2}-2\sigma_{2}k_{5}^{3}k_{6}+4c_{4}k_{5}k_{6}=0,

(2.20)

3\sigma_{2}k_{4}^{4}k_{6}+(c_{3}-c_{1})k_{5}k_{4}^{3}+2[(2\sigma_{2}k_{6}-c_{2})k_{5}^{2}-3c_{4}k_{6}]k_{4}^{2}+(c_{1}+c_{3})k_{4}k_{5}^{3}+k_{5}^{4}k_{6}\sigma_{2}-2c_{4}k_{5}^{2}k_{6}=0,

(2.21)

(c_{3}-c_{1})k_{4}^{4}-2(2k_{6}\sigma_{2}+c_{2})k_{5}k_{4}^{3}+2c_{1}k_{4}^{2}k_{5}^{2}+2[(c_{2}-2\sigma_{2}k_{6})k_{5}^{3}+4c_{4}k_{5}k_{6}]k_{4}-(c_{1}+c_{3})k_{5}^{4}=0,

(2.22)

(c_{1}-c_{3})k_{5}k_{4}^{3}-\sigma_{2}k_{4}^{4}k_{6}+2[2(c_{2}k_{5}^{2}+c_{4}k_{6})k_{4}^{2}-(c_{1}+c_{3})k_{5}^{3}k_{4}+\sigma_{2}k_{5}^{4}k_{6}-2c_{4}k_{5}^{2}k_{6}]=0.

(2.23)

It has 3 real solutions excluding
k_{4}=k_{5}=0
, namely

1.

c_{1}=c_{3},\quad c_{4}=\frac{\sigma_{2}}{2}k_{4}^{2},\quad k_{5}=0,

2.

c_{1}=-c_{3},\quad c_{4}=\frac{\sigma_{2}}{2}k_{5}^{2},\quad k_{4}=0,

3.

c_{4}=\frac{\sigma_{2}}{2}(k_{4}^{2}+k_{5}^{2}),\quad c_{2}=\frac{(-c_{1}+c_{3})k_{4}^{2}+(c_{1}+c_{3})k_{5}^{2}}{2k_{5}k_{4}}.

(The remaining constants are unconstrained in all three cases.) All of them, however, lead to vanishing of the magnetic field.
Therefore, we have proved that in order to have real non-vanishing magnetic field we need

k_{4}=k_{5}=0.

(2.24)

The remaining part of equations (
2.3
)–(
2.5
) can be solved for each integrable system of cylindrical type in the same manner. In each step we have to consider which of the constants
k_{i}
from solution (
1.30
) should be set to zero and which non-zero, because any vanishing constant means that the corresponding integral is not allowed by the system, but non-vanishing constants constrain auxiliary functions in such a way that the corresponding systems have zero magnetic field.

Having solved the compatibility conditions (
2.3
)–(
2.5
), we solve eq. (
1.2
) and eq. (
1.29
) to obtain
m(r,\phi,Z)
and
W(r,\phi,Z)
, respectively. We have to check compatibility of the obtained scalar potential
W(r,\phi,z)
with the restrictions imposed by the considered cylindrical system.

Let us now treat the specific cases from
[
11
]
and Section
1.4
.

2.1.2
Case
\mathrm{rank}(M)=2
subcase 2a)

The assumptions of this subcase read

\psi(\phi)=0,\quad\mu(Z)=\mu_{0},\quad\tau(\phi)=\tau_{0},

(2.25)

with the corresponding magnetic field and scalar potential

W=W(r),\quad B^{r}=0,\quad B^{\phi}=\frac{\tau_{0}}{r^{3}}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{\prime}(r),\quad B^{Z}=\mu_{0}r-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{\prime}(r).

(2.26)

In this case we assume
\psi(\phi)=0
, so the quantum correction vanishes and therefore the system is the same in classical and quantum mechanics.

From
[
11
]
we know, that this system admits the integrals

\tilde{X}_{1}=p_{\phi}^{A}+\frac{\rho(r)}{2}-\frac{\mu_{0}r^{2}}{2},\quad\tilde{X}_{2}=p_{Z}^{A}+\frac{\sigma(r)}{2}-\frac{\tau_{0}}{2r^{2}},

(2.27)

so we set
k_{3}=k_{6}=0
.

The additional conditions which split the case into further subcases in
[
11
]
all imply the scalar potential
W
and magnetic field
B
from eq. (
2.26
) with different constraints on the functions and constants. However, we lose no generality by redefining
\sigma(r)
and
\rho(r)
to set
\mu_{0}=\tau_{0}=0
and proceeding with the assumption
\sigma(r)\rho(r)\neq 0
. (We can always choose the constant term which does not appear in the magnetic field and can be eliminated by the choice of gauge in the integrals.) Therefore, we do not need to split into subcases.

Recalling eq. (
2.24
), the compatibility conditions (
2.3
)–(
2.5
) can be solved at once with the result

\rho(r)=\rho_{1}r^{2}+\rho_{0},\quad\sigma(r)=\sigma_{0},

(2.28)

where
\rho_{1}\neq 0
because otherwise the corresponding magnetic field

B^{r}(r,\phi,Z)=0,\quad B^{\phi}(r,\phi,Z)=0,\quad B^{Z}(r,\phi,Z)=-r\rho_{1}

(2.29)

vanishes. We write the magnetic field also in the Cartesian coordinates (transformed using eq. (
1.2
))

B^{x}(x,y,z)=0,\quad B^{y}(x,y,z)=0,\quad B^{z}(x,y,z)=-\rho_{1}.

(2.30)

We can now solve eq. (
1.2
) to get

m=\rho_{1}r(-k_{1}\sin(\phi)+k_{2}\cos(\phi))-k_{3}\frac{\sigma_{0}}{2}-k_{6}\frac{\rho_{0}}{2}.

(2.31)

(The last term is the integration constant, which we choose so that
\tilde{X}_{1}
and
\tilde{X}_{2}
do not contain unnecessary constants.)
The zeroth order equation (
1.29
) becomes

\left(k_{1}\cos(\phi)+k_{2}\sin(\phi)\right)W^{\prime}(r)=0,

(2.32)

we thus see that in order to have additional integrals (one of the constants
k_{1},

k_{2}
non-zero) we need
W
constant, without loss of generality

W=0.

(2.33)

This is a well-known system, considered e.g. in
[
15
]
. It was considered in
[
20
, eq. (42)]
, with different choice of the frame of reference.
The integrals read (in the Cartesian coordinates)

Y_{1}=p_{x}^{A}-\rho_{1}y,\quad Y_{2}=p_{y}^{A}+\rho_{1}x,\quad\tilde{X}_{1}=L_{z}^{A}+\frac{\rho_{1}(x^{2}+y^{2})}{2},\quad\tilde{X}_{2}=p_{z}^{A},

(2.34)

where
L_{z}^{A}=xp_{y}^{A}-yp_{x}^{A}
. The integrals are clearly mutually independent but the Hamiltonian can be rewritten as

H=\frac{1}{2}\left(Y_{1}^{2}+Y_{2}^{2}+\tilde{X}_{2}^{2}\right)-\rho_{1}X_{4}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(p_{x}^{A}\right)^{2}+\left(p_{y}^{A}\right)^{2}+\left(p_{z}^{A}\right)^{2}\right).

(2.35)

The fifth independent integral, which makes the system maximally superintegrable, is not polynomial in momenta
[
20
]
, namely

X_{5}=(p_{y}^{A}+\rho_{1}x)\sin\left(\frac{\rho_{1}z}{p_{z}^{A}}\right)-(p_{x}^{A}-\rho_{1}y)\cos\left(\frac{\rho_{1}z}{p_{z}^{A}}\right),

(2.36)

where we assume that
p_{z}^{A}
. (Otherwise the system collapses to 2D.)

Let us follow
[
20
]
and solve the Hamiltonian equations of motion and the Schrödinger equation. We choose the gauge so that two of the integrals
Y_{1},

Y_{2},

\tilde{X}_{2}
are simply momenta

\vec{A}(\vec{x})=\left(0,-\rho_{1}x,0\right).

(2.37)

The Hamiltonian equations of motion read

\displaystyle\dot{x}=p_{x},\quad\dot{y}=p_{y}+\rho_{1}x,\quad\dot{z}=p_{z},

(2.38)

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{x}=-\rho_{1}(p_{y}+\rho_{1}x),\quad\dot{p}_{y}=0,\quad\dot{p}_{z}=0.

(2.39)

The solution to these equations with the usual initial conditions
x(0)=x_{0},

p_{x}(0)=p_{x}^{0}
etc. is

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} x(t)&=\frac{p_{x}^{0}}{\rho_{1}}\sin(\rho_{1}t)+\left(x_{0}+\frac{p_{y}^{0}}{\rho_{1}}\right)\cos(\rho_{1}t)-\frac{p_{y}^{0}}{\rho_{1}},\\
y(t)&=\left(x_{0}+\frac{p_{y}^{0}}{\rho_{1}}\right)\sin(\rho_{1}t)-\frac{p_{x}^{0}}{\rho_{1}}\cos(\rho_{1}t)+y_{0}+\frac{p_{x}^{0}}{\rho_{1}},\\
z(t)&=p_{z}^{0}t+z_{0},\\
p_{x}(t)&=-\left(\rho_{1}x_{0}+p_{y}^{0}\right)\sin(\rho_{1}t)+p_{x}^{0}\cos(\rho_{1}t),\quad p_{y}(t)=p_{y}^{0},\quad p_{z}(t)=p_{z}^{0}.\end{aligned}

(2.40)

If
p_{z}^{0}\neq 0
, the trajectory is a helix with its axis parallel to the
z
-axis, otherwise it collapses to a circle in the plane
z=z_{0}
.

Let us now turn to the Schrödinger equation. Writing the Hamiltonian (
2.35
) in full

\hat{H}=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\left(\partial_{xx}+\partial_{yy}+\partial_{zz}\right)-\mathrm{i}\hbar\rho_{1}x\partial_{y}+\frac{\rho_{1}^{2}}{2}x^{2},

(2.41)

we see that the stationary Schrödinger equation
\hat{H}\psi=E\psi
separates in the Cartesian coordinates

\displaystyle\psi(\vec{x})=f(x)\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\lambda_{2}y\right)\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\lambda_{3}z\right),

(2.42)

\displaystyle\hbar^{2}f^{\prime\prime}(x)=\left((\rho_{1}x-\lambda_{2})^{2}+\lambda_{3}^{2}-2E\right)f(x),

(2.43)

\displaystyle Y_{2}\psi(\vec{x})=\lambda_{2}\psi(\vec{x}),\quad\tilde{X}_{2}\psi(\vec{x})=\lambda_{3}\psi(\vec{x}).

(2.44)

The reduced Schrödinger equation corresponds to the 1D harmonic oscillator with energy
E-\frac{\lambda^{2}_{3}}{2}
, angular frequency
\omega=\rho_{1}
with the centre of force located at
x=\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\rho_{1}}
. Therefore, the energy spectrum of the Hamiltonian (
2.35
)

E=\frac{\lambda_{3}^{2}}{2}+\hbar\rho_{1}\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right),

(2.45)

is continuous due to the arbitrary momentum
\lambda_{3}
and the generalized eigenvectors are

\psi_{n,\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}}(\vec{x})=K_{n}\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}(\lambda_{2}y+\lambda_{3}z)\right)H_{n}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\rho_{1}}{\hbar}}\left(x-\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\rho_{1}}\right)\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\rho_{1}}{2\hbar}\left(x-\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\rho_{1}}\right)^{2}\right),

(2.46)

where
H_{n}
are the Hermite polynomials and
K_{n}
is their normalization constant.

Let us see if the Schrödinger equation separates in the cylindrical coordinates as well. Using the gauge (
2.37
), the Hamiltonian reads

\begin{split}\hat{H}={}&-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\partial_{r})+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\phi\phi}+\partial_{ZZ}\right)-\\
&-\mathrm{i}\hbar\rho_{1}r\cos(\phi)\left(\sin(\phi)\partial_{r}+\frac{\cos(\phi)}{r}\partial_{\phi}\right)+\frac{\rho_{1}}{2}r^{2}(\cos(\phi))^{2}.\end{split}

(2.47)

It is clear that we can separate the
Z
coordinate from the
r
and
\phi
. However, it is not possible to separate
r
and
\phi
: The equation to separate is (divided by
f(r)g(\phi)
)

\begin{split}-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{g^{\prime\prime}(\phi)}{g(\phi)}-\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(r)}{f(r)}\right)-\mathrm{i}\hbar\rho_{1}r\sin(\phi)\cos(\phi)\frac{f^{\prime}(r)}{f(r)}=\\
=\mathrm{i}\hbar\rho_{1}\cos(\phi)^{2}\frac{g^{\prime}(\phi)}{g(\phi)}-\frac{\rho_{1}^{2}}{2}r^{2}\cos(\phi)^{2}+E,\end{split}

(2.48)

where the last term on the left-hand side and the second term on the right-hand side will always depend both on
r
an
\phi
.

However, if we choose the gauge which makes
\tilde{X}_{1}
simply
L_{z}

[
6
]
, namely

\vec{A}(\vec{x})=\left(\frac{\rho_{1}y}{2},-\frac{\rho_{1}x}{2},0\right),\qquad A_{r}=0,\quad A_{\phi}=-\frac{\rho_{1}r^{2}}{2},\quad A_{Z}=0,

(2.49)

the Hamiltonian reads

\begin{split}\hat{H}={}&-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\partial_{r})+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\phi\phi}+\partial_{ZZ}\right)+\mathrm{i}\hbar\frac{\rho_{1}}{2}\partial_{\phi}+\frac{\rho_{1}^{2}r^{2}}{8}.\end{split}

(2.50)

This time
L_{z}=-\mathrm{i}\hbar\partial_{\phi}
commutes with the Hamiltonian and therefore the wave-function

\psi(r,\phi,Z)=f(r)\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\eta_{2}\phi\right)\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\eta_{3}Z\right)

(2.51)

leads to a separation of variables, with the
r
coordinate equation reading

-\hbar^{2}\left(f^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}f^{\prime}(r)\right)+\left(\frac{\eta_{2}^{2}}{r^{2}}+\eta_{3}^{2}-\rho_{1}\eta_{2}\right)f(r)+\frac{\rho_{1}^{2}r^{2}f(r)}{4}=2Ef(r).

(2.52)

Its solution in terms of Whittaker functions
M_{\mu,\nu}(z),

W_{\mu,\nu,}(z)

[
1
, Chapter 13]
,
[
7
, Section 13.14]
reads

f(r)=\frac{1}{r}\left(c_{1}M_{\mu,\nu}\left(\frac{\rho_{1}}{\sqrt{2}\hbar}r^{2}\right)+c_{2}M_{\mu,\nu}\left(\frac{\rho_{1}}{\sqrt{2}\hbar}r^{2}\right)\right),

(2.53)

where
\mu
,
\nu
are constants

\mu=\frac{(\rho_{1}\eta_{2}-\eta_{3}^{2}+2E)}{\sqrt{2}\hbar\rho_{1}},\quad\nu=\frac{\eta_{2}}{\sqrt{2}\hbar}.

(2.54)

We solve the stationary Hamilton-Jacobi equation as well. Since we work with time-independent Hamiltonians, we can separate the time coordinate
t
from the Hamilton’s principal function
S
with separation constant
-E
and we use Hamilton’s characteristic function
U
, namely
S=U-Et
. Using the Hamiltonian (
2.35
) and the gauge (
2.37
) the equation reads

\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial x}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial y}-\rho_{1}x\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial z}\right)^{2}\right]=E.

(2.55)

We have 2 cyclic coordinates
y
and
z
; thus, we have the separation

U(x,y,z)=u(x)+p_{y}y+p_{z}z,

(2.56)

with
u(x)
satisfying

(u^{\prime}(x))^{2}+(p_{y}-\rho_{1}x)^{2}+p_{z}^{2}=2E.

(2.57)

For later use let us consider more generally an equation of type

(u^{\prime}(x))^{2}+\alpha_{1}x^{2}+\alpha_{2}x+\alpha_{3}=0

(2.58)

whose solution is

\begin{split}u(x)={}&\pm\left[\left(\frac{\alpha_{2}^{2}}{8\alpha_{1}^{3/2}}-\frac{\alpha_{3}}{2\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}}\right)\arctan\left(\frac{2\alpha_{1}x+\alpha_{2}}{2\sqrt{\alpha_{1}}\sqrt{-\alpha_{1}x^{2}-\alpha_{2}x-\alpha_{3}}}\right)\right.+\\
&+\left.\left(\frac{x}{2}+\frac{\alpha_{2}}{4\alpha_{1}}\right)\sqrt{-\alpha_{1}x^{2}-\alpha_{2}x-\alpha_{3}}\right]+c_{1}.\end{split}

(2.59)

In particular, the Hamilton’s characteristic function reads in our case

\begin{split}U={}&\frac{(p_{z}^{2}-2E)}{2\rho_{1}}\arctan\left(\frac{(-\rho_{1}x+p_{y})}{\sqrt{-\rho_{1}^{2}x^{2}+2p_{y}\rho_{1}x+a}}\right)-\\
&-\frac{(\rho_{1}x-p_{y})}{2\rho_{1}}\sqrt{-\rho_{1}^{2}x^{2}+2p_{y}\rho_{1}x+a}+p_{y}y+p_{z}z,\end{split}

(2.60)

where
a=2E-p_{y}^{2}-p_{z}^{2}
.

In the cylindrical coordinates we use the gauge (
2.49
), in which the Hamiltonian does not depend on
\phi
and
Z
, therefore Hamilton’s characteristic function

U(r,\phi,Z)=u(r)+p_{\phi}\phi+p_{Z}Z

(2.61)

reduces the Hamilton-Jacobi equation to

\frac{1}{2}\left[u^{\prime}(r)^{2}+\frac{(2p_{\phi}-\rho_{1}r^{2})^{2}}{4r^{2}}+p_{Z}^{2}\right]=E.

(2.62)

Let us again consider more general form of the equation for later use, namely

(u^{\prime}(r))^{2}=\frac{ar^{4}+br^{2}+c}{r^{2}}.

(2.63)

Assuming we take the square of a positive real number, the result to the last equation is of the following type (
v=r^{2}
)

\begin{split}u(v)=\int\!{\frac{\sqrt{a{v}^{2}+bv+c}}{v}}{\rm d}v=&\sqrt{a{v}^{2}+bv+c}+{\frac{b}{2\sqrt{a}}\ln\left({\frac{b+2av}{2\sqrt{a}}}+\sqrt{a{v}^{2}+bv+c}\right)}\\
&-\sqrt{c}\arctan\left(\frac{bv+2c}{2\sqrt{c}\sqrt{a{v}^{2}+bv+c}}\right)+C.\end{split}

(2.64)

The integration constant can absorb the purely imaginary constants arising from the possibly negative value of
a
in the second term, so we can use this form of the solution in that case as well with
a
replaced by
|a|
. If
c<0
, we must change the third term to argtanh and
c
to
|c|
. The explicit expression for the solution of the Hamilton-Jacobi equation becomes too complicated for practical use and we have already calculated the trajectories. Thus, we do not present it here.

2.1.3
Case
\mathrm{rank}(M)=2
subcase 2b 1.1)

This case corresponds to

\tau(\phi)=\tau_{0},\quad\sigma(r)=\frac{\tau_{0}}{r^{2}}+\sigma_{0},\quad\mu(Z)=0,\quad\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\neq 0,

(2.65)

which yields the following magnetic field

B^{\phi}=0,\quad B^{r}=0,\quad B^{Z}=-\rho^{\prime}(r)-\frac{\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+\psi(\phi)}{2r^{2}}

(2.66)

and the separable scalar potential with vanishing
Z
component
W(r,\phi,Z)=W_{12}(r,\phi)\equiv W(r,\phi)
satisfying
eq. (
1.108
) and eq. (
1.109
).

Recalling eq. (
2.24
), we have only one non-trivial compatibility equation, namely eq. (
2.3
). Differentiating it twice with respect to
r
to eliminate
\psi(\phi)
, we get an equation for
\rho(r)

(k_{1}\cos(\phi)+k_{2}\sin(\phi))[r^{3}\rho^{(4)}(r)+5r^{2}\rho^{\prime\prime\prime}(r)+2r\rho^{\prime\prime}(r)-2\rho^{\prime}(r)]=0.

(2.67)

Because the vanishing of the constants
k_{1},

k_{2}
would mean no additional integrals of motion (we would the second order cylindrical integrals or their reduced first order form only), we need
\rho(r)
of the form

\rho(r)=\rho_{3}\ln(r)+\rho_{2}r^{2}+\frac{\rho_{1}}{r}+\rho_{0}.

(2.68)

Using this in eq. (
2.3
) once differentiated with respect to
r
, we obtain

k_{6}(\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)+\psi^{\prime}(\phi))-2\rho_{3}(k_{2}\sin(\phi)+k_{1}\cos(\phi))=0,

(2.69)

which splits the considerations into two subcases:
k_{6}\neq 0
and
k_{6}=0
.

I.

k_{6}\neq 0
: In this subcase we solve eq. (
2.69
) for
\psi(\phi)
and get

\psi(\phi)=-\frac{1}{k_{6}}[((k_{1}\phi+k_{2})\rho_{3}+\psi_{2}k_{6})\cos(\phi)+((k_{2}\phi-k_{1})\rho_{3}-\psi_{1}k_{6})\sin(\phi)]+\psi_{3}.

(2.70)

Using this in eq. (
2.3
) (without differentiation), we get an algebraic equation with only one solution (excluding
k_{1}=k_{2}=0
):
\psi_{3}=\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{3}=0
, i.e.

\psi(\phi)=-\psi_{2}\cos(\phi)+\psi_{1}\sin(\phi)+\rho_{1},\quad\rho(r)=\frac{\rho_{2}r^{3}+\rho_{1}}{r}+\rho_{0},

(2.71)

so we obtained the constant magnetic field

B^{r}=0,\quad B^{\phi}=0,\quad B^{Z}=-\rho_{2}r.

(2.72)

Translating this into the Cartesian coordinates using eq. (
1.2
), it reads

B^{x}=0,\quad B^{y}=0,\quad B^{z}=-\rho_{2}.

(2.73)

We note that
\psi(\phi)
from eq. (
2.71
) implies vanishing of the quantum correction, so the quantum version of the system is identical to the classical version in this subcase.

Having solved the compatibility conditions, we solve the original equations (
1.2
) and (
1.29
) to obtain

\displaystyle W(r,\phi)

\displaystyle=W\left(\frac{k_{6}r^{2}}{2}+k_{2}r\cos(\phi)-k_{1}r\sin(\phi)\right),

(2.74)

\displaystyle m(r,\phi,Z)

\displaystyle=\rho_{2}\left(k_{2}r\cos(\phi)-k_{1}r\sin(\phi)+\frac{k_{6}r^{2}}{2}\right).

(2.75)

(We set the integration constant to 0 in
m
so that we get
\tilde{X}_{1}=p_{Z}^{A}
in accordance with eq. (
2.34
) in Subsection
2.1.2
.)

The potential must, however, satisfy eq. (
1.108
) and eq. (
1.109
), where the quantum correction vanishes.
Inserting the obtained form of the scalar potential into the first of them, we get

W^{\prime}\cdot[(k_{2}\rho_{2}\sin(\phi)+k_{1}\rho_{2}\cos(\phi))r^{3}+((k_{2}\rho_{0}-k_{6}\psi_{2})\sin(\phi)+\\
+(k_{1}\rho_{0}-k_{6}\psi_{1})\cos(\phi))r+k_{1}\psi_{2}-k_{2}\psi_{1}]=0.

(2.76)

If we assume that the scalar potential
W
is not constant, the terms in square bracket must vanish separately for all powers of
r
. The
r^{3}
term in the bracket implies either
k_{1}=k_{2}=0
, which means only 2 reduced cylindrical integrals, or
\rho_{2}=0
and vanishing magnetic field. Thus, the only interesting case for us is the constant scalar potential

W(r,\phi)=W_{0}.

(2.77)

Equation (
1.109
) is now also satisfied. Thus, we have arrived at the system (
2.34
) and we refer the reader to Subsection
2.1.2
.

II.

k_{6}=0
: With this assumption eq. (
2.68
) implies
\rho_{3}=0
and together with eq. (
2.3
) we get

\displaystyle\rho(r)

\displaystyle=\frac{\rho_{2}r^{3}+\rho_{1}}{r}+\rho_{0},

(2.78)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi(\phi)&=\frac{c_{1}\cos(2\phi)+c_{2}\sin(2\phi)+c_{3}}{k_{2}\cos(\phi)-k_{1}\sin(\phi)}+\rho_{1}.\end{split}

(2.79)

The corresponding magnetic field
B
reads

B^{r}=0,\quad B^{\phi}=0,\quad B^{Z}=-\rho_{2}r+\frac{\xi}{r^{2}(k_{1}\sin(\phi)-k_{2}\cos(\phi))^{3}},

(2.80)

where
\xi=-(c_{1}+c_{3})k_{1}^{2}-2c_{2}k_{1}k_{2}+k_{2}^{2}(c_{1}-c_{3})
.
Translating this into the Cartesian coordinates using eq. (
1.2
), it reads

B^{x}=0,\quad B^{y}=0,\quad B^{z}=-\rho_{2}+\frac{\xi}{(k_{1}y-k_{2}x)^{3}}.

(2.81)

In order to simplify the equations, we use the formula

k_{1}\sin(\phi)-k_{2}\cos(\phi)=\sqrt{k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}}\cos(\phi+\phi_{0}),\quad\cos(\phi_{0})=-\frac{k_{2}}{\sqrt{k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}}},

(2.82)

rotate the coordinate system so that
\phi_{0}=0
and redefine the constants
c_{i}
in
\psi(\phi)
to get

\psi(\phi)=\frac{c_{1}\cos(2\phi)+c_{2}\sin(2\phi)+c_{3}}{\cos(\phi)}+\rho_{1}.

(2.83)

The corresponding magnetic field
B
reads in the cylindrical coordinates

B^{r}=0,\quad B^{\phi}=0,\quad B^{Z}=-\rho_{2}r+\frac{c_{1}-c_{3}}{r^{2}(\cos(\phi))^{3}}

(2.84)

and in the Cartesian coordinates

B^{x}=0,\quad B^{y}=0,\quad B^{z}=-\rho_{2}+\frac{c_{1}-c_{3}}{x^{3}}.

(2.85)

Having solved the compatibility conditions, we solve the original equations (
1.2
) and (
1.29
) to obtain

\displaystyle W={}

\displaystyle W(r\cos(\phi))=W(x),

(2.86)

\displaystyle m={}

\displaystyle\rho_{2}r-\frac{c_{1}-c_{3}}{2r^{2}(\cos(\phi))^{2}}=\rho_{2}\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}-\frac{c_{1}-c_{3}}{2x^{2}}.

(2.87)

(We omit the constant of integration, which is only added to the integral of motion.)

We need to satisfy 2 more equations to ensure integrability, namely eq. (
1.108
) and eq. (
1.109
).
The first of them is

\begin{split}&\cos(\phi)^{3}r^{3}\left[((\rho_{2}r^{3}+\rho_{0}r)\cos(\phi)+2(c_{1}-c_{3}))\sin(\phi)-2c_{2}\cos(\phi)\right]W^{\prime}(r\cos(\phi))\\
&+\tfrac{3}{2}\hbar^{2}(c_{1}-c_{3})\sin(\phi)=0.\end{split}

(2.88)

(We differentiate
W
with respect to its argument, i.e.
r\cos(\phi)
.)

In the classical case,
\hbar\to 0
, the conditions for non-vanishing magnetic field
c_{1}\neq c_{3}
and
\rho_{2}\neq 0
imply constant scalar potential
W
.

In the quantum case, there is another possibility in addition to
W=W_{0}
: If
c_{1}\neq c_{3}
, we must have

\rho_{2}=0,\quad\rho_{0}=0,\quad c_{2}=0,\quad W=-\frac{3\hbar^{2}}{4(r\cos(\phi))^{2}}=-\frac{3\hbar^{2}}{4x^{2}}.

(2.89)

Inserting this potential
W
into eq. (
1.109
) with
\rho(r)
from eq. (
2.78
) and
\psi
from eq. (
2.83
), the numerator becomes

(c_{1}-c_{3})[(\rho_{2}r^{3}+\rho_{0}r)\sin(\phi)\cos(\phi)+2(c_{1}-c_{3})\sin(\phi)-2c_{2}\cos(\phi)]=0.

(2.90)

Thus, we have a contradiction due to
c_{1}\neq c_{3}
and the potential must be constant in quantum mechanics as well.

If we insert constant potential
W=W_{0}
into eq. (
1.109
), it is satisfied if and only if
c_{1}=c_{3}
, because we obtain the same numerator as in eq. (
2.90
). The result is, therefore, that both the magnetic field
B^{z}
and scalar potential
W
must be constant. We have again the well-known maximally superintegrable system from
[
15
,
20
]
, see Subsection
2.1.2
.

2.1.4
Case
\mathrm{rank}(M)=2
subcase 2b 1.2)

This case means

\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\neq 0,\quad\mu(Z)=0,\quad\rho(r)=\frac{\rho_{0}}{r},\quad\tau(\phi)=\tau_{0}+\frac{\tau_{1}}{(\psi(\phi)-\rho_{0})^{2}},\quad\sigma(r)=\frac{\tau_{0}}{r^{2}}+\sigma_{0},

(2.91)

thus, the following magnetic field

B^{r}(r,\phi,Z)=-\frac{\tau_{1}\beta^{\prime}(\phi)}{r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{3}},\quad B^{Z}(r,\phi,Z)=-\frac{\beta(\phi)+\beta^{\prime\prime}(\phi)}{2r^{3}},\quad B^{\phi}(r,\phi,Z)=\frac{\tau_{1}}{r^{3}\beta(\phi)^{2}},

(2.92)

and the scalar potential (
1.132
)

\begin{split}W={}&\frac{W_{0}}{r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{2}}+\frac{\beta(\phi)\beta^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+\beta^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}+\frac{f_{1}}{4}-\frac{\tau_{1}^{2}}{\beta(\phi)^{4}}+2\beta(\phi)^{2}}{8r^{4}}+\\
&+\frac{\hbar^{2}(2\beta(\phi)\beta^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+\beta(\phi)^{2}-\beta^{\prime}(\phi)^{2})}{8r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{2}},\end{split}

(2.93)

where
\beta(\phi)
is the auxiliary function
\psi(\phi)
after a shift

\beta(\phi)=\psi(\phi)-\rho_{0}.

(2.94)

The function
\beta(\phi)
must satisfy eq. (
1.129
)
or equivalently its reduced form (
1.133
).
Using this equation, we can simplify the potential from eq. (
2.95
) and obtain the potential from eq. (
1.134
), namely

W=\frac{W_{0}}{r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{2}}-\frac{(4\tau_{1}^{2}+\beta_{2})}{32r^{4}\beta(\phi)^{4}}+\hbar^{2}\frac{f_{1}\beta(\phi)^{4}-12\beta_{1}\beta(\phi)^{2}-5\beta_{2}}{32r^{2}\beta(\phi)^{6}}.

(2.95)

where
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
are integration constants in the reduced equation (
1.133
).

Let us continue solving equations (
2.3
)–(
2.5
), taking eq. (
2.24
) into account. Differentiating eq. (
2.4
) with respect to
r
, we obtain (multiplied by
\beta(\phi)^{3}
, which must be non-zero)

k_{6}\tau_{1}\beta^{\prime}(\phi)=0.

(2.96)

Thus, we split our considerations into 4 subcases.

I.

\tau_{1}=0
,
k_{6}\neq 0
:
Differentiating eq. (
2.3
) with respect to
r
, we obtain

k_{6}(\beta^{\prime}(\phi)+\beta^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi))=0.

(2.97)

Assumption
k_{6}\neq 0
leads to

\beta(\phi)=c_{1}\cos(\phi)+c_{2}\sin(\phi)+c_{3},

(2.98)

but the constant
c_{3}
must vanish to satisfy eq. (
2.3
), implying no magnetic field.

II.

\tau_{1}=0
,
k_{6}=0
:
From the last remaining compatibility equation (
2.3
) we obtain
\beta(\phi)
of the form

\beta(\phi)=\frac{a_{1}+a_{2}\sin(2\phi)+a_{3}\cos(2\phi)}{k_{1}\sin(\phi)-k_{2}\cos(\phi)},

(2.99)

leading to a system with non-vanishing magnetic field in the
z
-direction only

B^{Z}(r,\phi,Z)=-\frac{(a_{1}+a_{3})k_{1}^{2}+2a_{2}k_{2}k_{1}+k_{2}^{2}(a_{1}-a_{3})}{r^{2}(k_{1}\sin(\phi)-k_{2}\cos(\phi))^{3}},

(2.100)

where
a_{i}
are constants. Transforming the magnetic field into the Cartesian coordinates using eq. (
1.2
), we get a somewhat simpler expression

B^{z}(x,y,z)=-\frac{(a_{1}+a_{3})k_{1}^{2}+2a_{2}k_{2}k_{1}+k_{2}^{2}(a_{1}-a_{3})}{(k_{1}y-k_{2}x)^{3}}.

(2.101)

Without loss of generality, we can simplify the previous expressions by using eq. (
2.82
), choosing our coordinate system and redefining the constants
a_{1},

a_{2}
and
a_{3}
so that
k_{1}=1
,
k_{2}=0
, i.e.

\beta(\phi)=\frac{a_{1}+a_{2}\sin(2\phi)+a_{3}\cos(2\phi)}{\sin(\phi)}.

(2.102)

The magnetic field simplifies to

B^{Z}(r,\phi,Z)=-\frac{(a_{1}+a_{3})}{r^{2}(\sin(\phi))^{3}},\quad B^{z}(x,y,z)=-\frac{(a_{1}+a_{3})}{y^{3}},

(2.103)

with all other components vanishing both in the cylindrical and Cartesian coordinates.

We have to check if
\beta(\phi)
of the form eq. (
2.102
) satisfies eq. (
1.129
). For that to be the case the constants must satisfy (omitting the trivial
a_{1}=a_{2}=a_{3}=0
)

a_{1}=-a_{3},\quad f_{1}=-16(a_{2}^{2}+a_{3}^{2}),

(2.104)

which, however, implies vanishing of the magnetic field. This subcase, therefore, does not admit any superintegrable system with additional first order integrals and non-vanishing magnetic field.

III.

\tau_{1}\neq 0
,
k_{6}\neq 0
: Here equation (
2.96
) implies
\beta(\phi)=\gamma=const
, which satisfies eq. (
1.129
) trivially. However, eq. (
2.4
) in this case reads

\tau_{1}(k_{1}\cos(\phi)+k_{2}\sin(\phi))=0,

(2.105)

which (recall
\tau_{1}\neq 0
) implies no additional integral in this case.

IV.

\tau_{1}\neq 0
,
k_{6}=0
: From eq. (
2.3
) we obtain

\beta(\phi)=c_{1}(k_{1}\sin(\phi)-k_{2}\cos(\phi))

(2.106)

Substituting it into the differential equation (
1.129
), we obtain

c_{1}\left(4(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2})c_{1}^{2}+f_{1}\right)(k_{1}\sin(\phi)+k_{2}\cos(\phi))=0,

(2.107)

so we need

c_{1}=\pm\sqrt{\frac{-f_{1}}{4(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2})}},\quad f_{1}<0,

(2.108)

because vanishing
\beta(\phi)
would imply division by 0.

We note that the quantum correction in eq. (
2.95
) vanishes for
\beta(\phi)
of the form (
2.106
). The system defined by the following magnetic field
B
and scalar potential
W
is, therefore, the same in classical and quantum mechanics.

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{2\tau_{1}^{2}\left(k_{2}^{2}+k_{1}^{2}\right)^{2}}{\left(k_{2}\cos(\phi)-k_{1}\sin(\phi)\right)^{4}f_{1}^{2}r^{4}}+\frac{4W_{0}\left(k_{2}^{2}+k_{1}^{2}\right)}{\left(k_{2}\cos(\phi)-k_{1}\sin(\phi)\right)^{2}f_{1}r^{2}}\right),

(2.109)

\displaystyle B^{r}

\displaystyle=-{\frac{4\left(k_{2}\sin(\phi)+k_{1}\cos(\phi)\right)\left(k_{2}^{2}+k_{1}^{2}\right)\tau_{1}}{\left(k_{2}\cos(\phi)-k_{1}\sin(\phi)\right)^{3}r^{2}f_{1}}},

(2.110)

\displaystyle{B^{\phi}}

\displaystyle=-{\frac{4\left(k_{2}^{2}+k_{1}^{2}\right)\tau_{1}}{r^{3}\left(k_{2}\cos(\phi)-k_{1}\sin\left(\phi\right)\right)^{2}f_{1}}},\quad{B^{Z}}=0.

(2.111)

We note that we can write

\frac{k_{2}\cos(\phi)-k_{1}\sin(\phi)}{\sqrt{k_{2}^{2}+k_{1}^{2}}}=\cos(\phi_{0})\cos(\phi)-\sin(\phi_{0})\sin(\phi)=\cos(\phi+\phi_{0}),

(2.112)

which corresponds to the rotational symmetry of the system. Without loss of generality, we can rotate the coordinate system so that
\phi_{0}=\frac{3\pi}{2}
, which corresponds to
k_{2}=0,

k_{1}=1
. The Cartesian form of the magnetic field and the scalar potential with the adjusted coordinate axis reads

\displaystyle W=-4\left(\frac{\tau_{1}^{2}}{2f_{1}^{2}y^{4}}+\frac{W_{0}}{f_{1}y^{2}}\right),\quad B^{x}={\frac{4\tau_{1}}{f_{1}y^{3}}},\quad{B^{y}}=0,\quad B^{z}=0

(2.113)

The corresponding first order integrals of motion are

\tilde{X}_{2}=p_{z}^{A}+{\frac{2\tau_{1}}{f_{1}y^{2}}},\quad Y_{1}=p_{x}^{A}.

(2.114)

The other cylindrical integral
X_{1}
is of the second order and reads

X_{1}=\left(xp_{y}^{A}-yp_{x}^{A}\right)^{2}-{\frac{4\tau_{1}\left({x}^{2}+y^{2}\right)}{f_{1}y^{2}}\left(p_{z}^{A}+\frac{2\tau_{1}}{f_{1}y^{2}}\right)}-{\frac{8W_{0}\left({x}^{2}+y^{2}\right)}{f_{1}y^{2}}},\\

(2.115)

This case in the chosen coordinates corresponds to the Case Ib) or Ic) in
[
19
]
with constants

a_{1}=a_{2}=0,\quad a_{3}=\frac{2\tau_{1}}{f_{1}},\quad b_{1}=b_{2}=0,\quad b_{3}=-\frac{4W_{0}}{f_{1}}.

(2.116)

There is, therefore, another second order integral of motion, namely

\displaystyle X_{3}

\displaystyle=\left(xp_{y}^{A}-yp_{x}^{A}\right)p_{y}^{A}-{\frac{4\tau_{1}x}{f_{1}y^{2}}\left(p_{z}^{A}+{\frac{2\tau_{1}}{f_{1}y^{2}}}\right)}-{\frac{8W_{0}x}{f_{1}y^{2}}},

(2.117)

which in the natural choice of gauge

A_{x}(x,y,z)=0,\quad A_{y}(x,y,z)=0,\quad A_{z}(x,y,z)=-\frac{2\tau_{1}}{f_{1}y^{2}}

(2.118)

simplifies to

\displaystyle X_{3}

\displaystyle=\left(xp_{y}-yp_{x}\right)p_{y}-{\frac{4\tau_{1}x}{f_{1}y^{2}}p_{z}}-{\frac{8W_{0}x}{f_{1}y^{2}}}.

(2.119)

There are, however, only 4 independent integrals of motion, because

(\tilde{X}_{2}^{2}+Y_{1}^{2}-2H)X_{1}+X_{3}^{2}=-\frac{4}{f_{1}}(\tilde{X}_{2}\tau_{1}+2W_{0})(2H-\tilde{X}_{2}^{2}).

(2.120)

The non-vanishing Poisson brackets read

\displaystyle\{X_{3},X_{1}\}_{\text{P.B.}}

\displaystyle=2{p_{x}^{A}}\left(xp_{y}^{A}-yp_{x}^{A}\right)^{2}-{\frac{16W_{0}\left({x}^{2}+y^{2}\right){p_{x}^{A}}}{f_{1}y^{2}}}-

(2.121)

\displaystyle-{\frac{8\tau_{1}\left({x}^{2}+y^{2}\right){p_{x}^{A}}}{f_{1}y^{2}}\left({p_{z}^{A}}+{\frac{2\tau_{1}}{f_{1}y^{2}}}\right)}=2Y_{1}X_{1},

\displaystyle\{X_{3},Y_{1}\}_{\text{P.B.}}

\displaystyle=\left(p_{y}^{A}\right)^{2}-{\frac{4\tau_{1}}{f_{1}y^{2}}\left(p_{z}^{A}+{\frac{2\tau_{1}}{f_{1}y^{2}}}\right)}-{\frac{8W_{0}}{f_{1}y^{2}}}=2H-\tilde{X}_{2}^{2}-Y_{1}^{2},

(2.122)

\displaystyle\{X_{1},Y_{1}\}_{\text{P.B.}}

\displaystyle=2p_{y}^{A}\left(xp_{y}^{A}-yp_{x}^{A}\right)-{\frac{8\tau_{1}x}{f_{1}y^{2}}\left(p_{z}^{A}+{\frac{2\tau_{1}}{f_{1}y^{2}}}\right)}-\frac{16W_{0}x}{f_{1}y^{2}}=2X_{3},

(2.123)

so the Poisson algebra is already closed in the sense that the Poisson bracket of any pair of integrals is a function (polynomial) in previously known integrals, and thus generates no new independent integral. Therefore, the system is not second order maximally superintegrable, as it was found in
[
19
]
.

Let us now compute the trajectories and see if the bounded ones are closed, which is typical for maximally superintegrable systems.
The Hamilton’s equations corresponding to the gauge-fixed Hamiltonian

H=\frac{1}{2}\left((p_{x})^{2}+(p_{y})^{2}+\left(p_{z}-\frac{2\tau_{1}}{f_{1}y^{2}}\right)^{2}\right)-{\frac{4W_{0}f_{1}y^{2}+2\tau_{1}^{2}}{f_{1}^{2}y^{4}}}

(2.124)

read

\displaystyle\dot{x}=p_{x},\quad\dot{y}=p_{y},\quad\dot{z}=p_{z}-\frac{2\tau_{1}}{f_{1}y^{2}},

(2.125)

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{x}=0,\quad\dot{p}_{y}=-\frac{4\tau_{1}p_{z}+8W_{0}}{f_{1}y^{3}},\quad\dot{p}_{z}=0.

(2.126)

The equations determining
x
and
p_{x}
correspond to the free motion of the particle in the
x
-direction

x(t)=p_{x0}t+x_{0}.

(2.127)

Because the momentum
p_{z}
is conserved, we find that
y(t)
must satisfy the following ODE

\ddot{y}=-\frac{4\tau_{1}p_{z}+8W_{0}}{f_{1}y^{3}}.

(2.128)

We note that the equation is not defined at
y(t)=0
.
If
\tau_{1}p_{z}+2W_{0}=0
, we have the free motion in this direction as well. We continue with the assumption that it is not the case.

Equation (
2.128
) does not depend on the independent variable
t
, so it admits
\dot{y}
as an integrating factor and (assuming it is non-zero) we obtain

\dot{y}(t)=\pm\frac{\sqrt{f_{1}(-C_{1}f_{1}y^{2}+4\tau_{1}p_{z}+8W_{0})}}{f_{1}y},

(2.129)

where the integration constant
C_{1}
is real and we assume that the square root is well defined.
Solving the separable ODE (
2.129
), we get

y(t)=\pm\frac{\sqrt{C_{1}f_{1}\left(C_{1}^{2}f_{1}(t-t_{0})^{2}-4p_{z}\tau_{1}-8W_{0}\right)}}{C_{1}f_{1}}.

(2.130)

We remind the reader that eq. (
2.128
) is not defined for
y(t)=0
, so we must restrict the independent variable
t
so that this does not occur.
The integration constant
t_{0}
translates the origin of time and
C_{1}
depends on the initial values and constants of the system

C_{1}=-\frac{f_{1}y_{0}^{2}\pm\sqrt{f_{1}^{2}y_{0}^{4}+16t_{0}^{2}f_{1}p_{z}\tau_{1}+32W_{0}t_{0}^{2}f_{1}}}{2t_{0}^{2}f_{1}}.

(2.131)

The sign of the square root must be chosen so that the square root in eq. (
2.130
) is well defined (positive argument) for
t
in a suitably chosen interval, which also determines the possible values of
t_{0}
.

The sign of
C_{1}
determines the motion of the particle in the
y
coordinate: If
C_{1}>0
, then the particle escapes to the infinity. If
C_{1}<0
, then it falls on the origin
y=0
, where eq. (
2.128
) has a singularity.

To solve the case
C_{1}=0
, we must return to the eq. (
2.129
), which leads to the singular solution

y(t)=\frac{2\sqrt{f_{1}(t-t_{0})\sqrt{f_{1}(p_{z}\tau_{1}+2W_{0})}}}{f_{1}}.

(2.132)

This case is unbounded or goes to
y=0
(where eq. (
2.128
) is ill defined) for
f_{1}>0
and
f_{1}<0
, respectively.

We can now solve the remaining equation in eq. (
2.125
), namely

\dot{z}=p_{z}-\frac{2\tau_{1}}{f_{1}y^{2}}.

(2.133)

The solution using
y(t)
from eq. (
2.130
) is

z(t)=-\frac{\tau_{1}C_{1}{\rm argtanh}\left({\frac{C_{1}^{2}f_{1}\left(t-t_{0}\right)}{2\sqrt{C_{1}^{2}f_{1}\left(\tau_{1}p_{z}+2W_{0}\right)}}}\right)}{\sqrt{C_{1}^{2}f_{1}\left(\tau_{1}p_{z}+2W_{0}\right)}}+p_{z}t+C_{2}.

(2.134)

The form of
z(t)
above assumes that the argument of argtanh is real, i.e.

f_{1}\left(\tau_{1}p_{z}+2W_{0}\right)>0,

(2.135)

and not equal to
\pm 1
(domain of argtanh).
On the other hand, if

f_{1}\left(\tau_{1}p_{z}+2W_{0}\right)<0,

(2.136)

we can rewrite the argtanh using the identity

{\rm argtanh}(z)=\mathrm{i}\arctan(\mathrm{i}z),\quad\forall z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{\pm 1,\pm\mathrm{i}\}

(2.137)

and cancel the imaginary unit with the one from the denominator. Because

\lim_{x\to\pm 1}{\rm argtanh}(x)=\pm\infty

(2.138)

and it is dominant with respect to
p_{z}t
,
z(t)
is unbounded in the first case. In the second case, arctan is bounded and
z(t)
is unbounded if and only if
p_{z}\neq 0
.

Using the singular solution eq. (
2.132
) in eq. (
2.133
), we get

z(t)=-\frac{\tau_{1}\ln(t-t_{0})}{2\sqrt{f_{1}(p_{z}\tau_{1}+2W_{0})}}+p_{z}t+C_{2},

(2.139)

which is clearly unbounded as
t
goes to
\infty
or
t_{0}
.

The last possibility,
y(t)=p_{y}^{0}t+y_{0}
, leads to

z(t)=p_{z}t+\frac{2\tau_{1}}{f_{1}p_{y}^{0}(p_{y}^{0}t+y_{0})}+C_{3},

(2.140)

which is unbounded if
p_{z}\neq 0
and singular at
t=0
if
p_{y}\neq 0
. (If
p_{y}^{0}=0
, the second term of
z(t)
would be
\frac{2\tau_{1}t}{f_{1}y_{0}^{2}}
, i.e. free motion with a modified momentum.)

To sum up, the motion in the
x
-direction is free. The motion in the
y
-direction can be free, unbounded, or bounded with the fall on the singular point
y=0
. The motion in the
z
-direction is always unbounded. Therefore, there are no bounded trajectories and we have no hint of higher order maximal superintegrability.

Let us now turn to the quantum version of the system and its stationary Schrödinger equation.
Looking again at the integrals (
2.114
) and the Hamiltonian (
2.124
) in the gauge (
2.118
), we see that we can write the Hamiltonian as

H=\frac{1}{2}(X_{3}^{2}+X_{4}^{2})+\frac{1}{2}X_{5},

(2.141)

where

X_{5}=[X_{1},X_{4}]=\left(p_{y}^{A}\right)^{2}-{\frac{4\tau_{1}}{f_{1}y^{2}}\left(p_{z}^{A}+{\frac{\tau_{1}}{f_{1}y^{2}}}\right)}-{\frac{8W_{0}}{f_{1}y^{2}}},

(2.142)

see eq. (
2.122
), which is, however, written in terms of the Poisson bracket. This confirms the separation of the Hamiltonian (
2.124
) in the Cartesian coordinates and the stationary Schrödinger equation reduces as follows.

\displaystyle\psi(\vec{x})=\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\lambda_{1}x\right)\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\lambda_{3}z\right)g(y),

(2.143)

\displaystyle-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}g^{\prime\prime}(y)=\left(\frac{4(\tau_{1}+2W_{0})}{f_{1}y^{2}}+E\right)g(y),

(2.144)

where
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{3}
are eigenvalues of the momentum operators
p_{x}
and
p_{z}
, respectively, i.e.

X_{3}\psi(\vec{x})=\lambda_{3}\psi(\vec{x}),\quad X_{4}\psi(\vec{x})=\lambda_{1}\psi(\vec{x}).

(2.145)

We substitute
g(y)=\sqrt{y}k(y)
and obtain (after simplifying)

y^{2}k^{\prime\prime}(y)+yk^{\prime}(y)+\left[\frac{2Ey^{2}}{\hbar^{2}}-\left(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{8a}{\hbar^{2}f_{1}}\right)\right]k(y)=0,

(2.146)

where
a=\tau_{1}+2W_{0}
, which is the Bessel equation
[
1
, Chapter 9]
,
[
7
, Chapter 10]
modulo a transformation of the independent variable. Equation (
2.144
) can, therefore, be solved in terms of Bessel functions of the first and second kind
{\sl J}(\alpha;x),

{\sl Y}(\alpha;x)
:

g\left(y\right)=c_{1}\sqrt{y}{{\sl J}\left({\frac{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{32a-\hbar^{2}f_{1}}}{2\hbar\sqrt{f_{1}}}};\sqrt{{\frac{2E}{\hbar^{2}}}}y\right)}+c_{2}\sqrt{y}{{\sl Y}\left({\frac{\mathrm{i}\sqrt{32a-\hbar^{2}f_{1}}}{2\hbar\sqrt{f_{1}}}};\sqrt{{\frac{2E}{\hbar^{2}}}}y\right)},

(2.147)

Let us also have a look at the Schrödinger equation in the cylindrical coordinates. The corresponding Hamiltonian reads

\hat{H}=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\partial_{r})+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\phi\phi}+\partial_{ZZ}\right)+\frac{2\mathrm{i}\hbar\tau_{1}}{f_{1}r^{2}(\sin(\phi))^{2}}\partial_{Z}-\frac{4W_{0}}{f_{1}r^{2}(\sin(\phi))^{2}}.

(2.148)

Because the Hamiltonian does not depend on
Z
, it commutes with
P_{Z}=-\mathrm{i}\hbar\partial_{Z}
, so we can separate the
Z
coordinate. We show that we can separate
r
and
\phi
as well. For that we use the
ansatz

\psi(r,\phi,Z)=f(r)g(\phi)\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\lambda_{3}z\right).

(2.149)

Substituting it into the stationary Schrödinger equation with the Hamiltonian (
2.148
), we get

\begin{split}&\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\left[\left(f^{\prime\prime}(r)+\frac{1}{r}f^{\prime}(r)\right)g(\phi)+\frac{1}{r^{2}}f(r)g^{\prime\prime}(\phi)\right]+\\
&+\left[\frac{2\tau_{1}\lambda_{3}+4W_{0}}{f_{1}r^{2}(\sin(\phi))^{2}}+\left(E-\frac{\lambda_{3}^{2}}{2}\right)\right]f(r)g(\phi)=0.\end{split}

(2.150)

Dividing by
\frac{f(r)g(\phi)}{r^{2}}
, we can separate the terms to get the following equations

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(r)=

\displaystyle-\frac{f^{\prime}(r)}{r}+\frac{c+2r^{2}(\lambda_{3}^{2}-2E)}{r^{2}\hbar^{2}}f(r),

(2.151)

\displaystyle g^{\prime\prime}(\phi)=

\displaystyle-\frac{c}{\hbar^{2}}+\frac{4g(\phi)(\tau_{1}\lambda_{3}-2W_{0})}{\sin(\phi)^{2}f_{1}\hbar^{2}},

(2.152)

where
c
is the separation constant.

Equation (
2.151
) multiplied by
r^{2}
is the Bessel equation
[
1
, Chapter 9]
,
[
7
, Section 10.2]
(modulo a transformation of independent variable), so the solution in terms of Bessel functions is

f(r)=c_{1}{\sl J}\left(\frac{\sqrt{c}}{\hbar},\sqrt{2E-\lambda_{3}^{2}}{\hbar}r\right)+c_{2}{\sl Y}\left(\frac{\sqrt{c}}{\hbar},\sqrt{2E-\lambda_{3}^{2}}{\hbar}r\right).

(2.153)

Equation (
2.152
) can be solved in terms of the hypergeometric function
{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b;c;Z)

[
1
, Chapter 15]
,
[
7
, Chapter 5]
, but the expression is too complicated for any practical use. Either way, we know that the spectrum is continuous due to the plane wave in
Z
-direction.

Let us consider the stationary case of Hamilton-Jacobi equation. Starting in the Cartesian coordinates, we have
p_{x}
and
p_{z}
as the integrals of motion corresponding to cyclic coordinates in our gauge (
2.118
). Thus, the Hamilton’s characteristic function reads

U(x,y,z)=p_{x}x+u(y)+p_{z}z,

(2.154)

where
u(y)
is determined by the following reduced equation.

\frac{1}{2}\left[p_{x}^{2}+(u^{\prime}(y))^{2}+\left(p_{z}-\frac{2\tau_{1}}{f_{1}y^{2}}\right)^{2}\right]-4\left(\frac{\tau_{1}^{2}}{2f_{1}^{2}y^{4}}+\frac{W_{0}}{f_{1}y^{2}}\right)=E.

(2.155)

The resulting quadrature

u(y)=\int\frac{\sqrt{f_{1}((2E-p_{x}^{2}-p_{z}^{2})f_{1}y^{2}+4p_{z}\tau_{1}+8W_{0})}}{f_{1}y}{\operatorname{d}}y

(2.156)

has the following solution

u(y)=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha f_{1}y^{2}+4\beta}{f_{1}}}-\frac{2\beta}{\sqrt{\beta f_{1}}}\ln\left(\frac{\sqrt{\beta f_{1}}\sqrt{f_{1}(\alpha f_{1}y^{2}+4\beta)}+2\beta f_{1}}{y}\right)+C,

(2.157)

where
\alpha=2E-p_{x}^{2}-p_{z}^{2}
and
\beta=p_{z}\tau_{1}+2W_{0}
.

In the cylindrical coordinates with the same gauge (
2.118
) we have only one cyclic coordinate, namely
Z
, so we use the
ansatz

U(r,\phi,Z)=v(r)+w(\phi)+p_{Z}Z.

(2.158)

We separate the equation as follows.

\displaystyle\frac{[(v^{\prime}(r))^{2}+p_{Z}^{2}]r^{2}}{2}-Er^{2}=c=-\frac{(w^{\prime}(\phi))^{2}}{2}+\frac{2p_{Z}\tau_{1}+4W_{0}}{f_{1}(\sin(\phi))^{2}}.

(2.159)

Solution to the separated equations is

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} v(r)&=\sqrt{\alpha r^{2}+2c}-\sqrt{2c}\ln\left(\frac{\sqrt{2c\alpha r^{2}+4c^{2}}+2c}{r}\right)+c_{1},\end{aligned}

(2.160)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} w(\phi)&=\frac{\sqrt{2c}\ln\left(\sqrt{2c^{2}f_{1}^{2}\cos(2\phi)+2cf_{1}\beta}\cos(\phi)-cf_{1}\cos(2\phi)-2\gamma\right)}{2\sqrt{\gamma}}+\\
&+\frac{\sqrt{\gamma}\ln\left(4\cos(\phi)\sqrt{\gamma cf_{1}\cos(2\phi)+\beta\gamma}+(6\gamma-\beta)\cos(2\phi)+\beta-\gamma\right)}{{\sqrt{f_{1}}}}-\\
&-\frac{2\sqrt{\gamma}\ln(\sin(\phi))}{\sqrt{f_{1}}}+c_{2},\end{aligned}

(2.161)

where
\alpha=2E-p_{Z}^{2}
,
\beta=4p_{Z}\tau_{1}+8W_{0}-cf_{1}
and
\gamma=p_{Z}\tau_{1}+2W_{0}
.

2.1.5
Case
\mathrm{rank}(M)=1
subcase 3a 1a)

The assumptions of this case are

\mu(Z)\neq 0,\quad\psi(\phi)=0,\quad\rho(r)=0,\quad\sigma(r)=0,

(2.162)

which means

B^{r}(r,\phi,Z)=\frac{\tau^{\prime}(\phi)}{2r^{2}}-\frac{\mu^{\prime}(Z)r^{2}}{2},\ B^{\phi}(r,\phi,Z)=\frac{\tau(\phi)}{r^{3}},\ B^{Z}(r,\phi,Z)=\mu(Z)r.

(2.163)

We note that
\psi(\phi)=0
implies vanishing quantum correction, so the classical and quantum systems coincide.

Recalling eq. (
2.24
), differentiating eq. (
2.4
) with respect to
Z
gives

(k_{2}\cos(\phi)-k_{1}\sin(\phi))\mu^{\prime\prime}(Z)=0.

(2.164)

Thus, in order to have additional integrals of motion, we need
\mu(Z)=\mu_{1}Z+\mu_{0}
. From eq. (
2.4
) twice differentiated with respect to
r
we immediately get
\mu_{1}=0
, so we have

\mu(Z)=\mu_{0}\neq 0.

(2.165)

Differentiating eq. (
2.4
) with respect to
r
once this time, we obtain the equation which leads to splitting of further considerations:

k_{6}\tau^{\prime}(\phi)=0,

(2.166)

i.e. we split into
k_{6}=0
and
\tau(\phi)=\tau_{0}
. It is convenient to further split the second subcase into
\tau_{0}=0
and
\tau_{0}\neq 0
.

I.

\tau(\phi)=\tau_{0}\neq 0
: Using these assumptions in eq. (
2.4
), which reads

\tau_{0}(k_{2}\cos(\phi)+k_{1}\sin(\phi))=0,

(2.167)

implies
k_{1}=k_{2}=0
, so the only first order integrals of the motion are

\tilde{X}_{1}=p_{Z}^{A}-\frac{\tau_{0}}{2r^{2}},\quad\tilde{X}_{2}=p_{\phi}^{A}-\frac{\mu_{0}r^{2}}{2},

(2.168)

i.e. the reduced cylindrical integrals, so this case is only integrable.

II.

\tau(\phi)=0
: This leads to the constant magnetic field in the
z
-direction

\quad B^{r}=0,\quad B^{\phi}=0,\quad B^{Z}=\mu_{0}r,

(2.169)

the scalar potential
W
of the form of eq. (111) from
[
11
]
with redefined
W_{1}(r)

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=W_{1}(r)+W_{3}(Z),

(2.170)

which must satisfy an additional constraint, namely eq. (
1.29
)

(k_{1}\cos(\phi)+k_{2}\sin(\phi))W_{1}^{\prime}(r)+k_{3}W_{3}^{\prime}(Z)=0.

(2.171)

The case
k_{1}=k_{2}=0
implies no additional integrals, we will therefore consider only

W_{1}(r)=W_{0}.

(2.172)

Subsequently we need
k_{3}=0
or
W_{3}(Z)=W_{3}
. We can set
W_{3}=0
by another redefinition of
W_{1}(r).
We have, therefore, 2 superintegrable subcases: The constant magnetic field from eq. (
2.169
) with either constant potential

W=W_{0},\text{ without loss of generality }W=0,

(2.173)

which has four first order integrals of motion and was already considered, see eq. (
2.34
) (with
\rho_{1}=-\mu_{0}
),
or the potential

W=W_{3}(Z)\equiv W_{3}(z)

(2.174)

with the first order integrals

Y_{1}=p_{x}^{A}+\mu_{0}y,\quad Y_{2}=p_{y}^{A}-\mu_{0}x,\quad\tilde{X}_{1}=L_{z}^{A}-\frac{\mu_{0}(x^{2}+y^{2})}{2},

(2.175)

complemented by the irreducible second order cylindrical integral

X_{2}=\left(p_{z}^{A}\right)^{2}+2W_{3}(z).

(2.176)

In both cases we have a relation connecting the integrals

H=\frac{1}{2}\left(Y_{1}^{2}+Y_{2}^{2}+X_{2}\right)+\mu_{0}\tilde{X}_{1},

(2.177)

where in the first case
X_{2}=\tilde{X}_{2}^{2}
, so these integrals ensure minimal superintegrability only.

The second system is Case B in
[
17
]
, where it was shown that this system is second order maximally superintegrable for 2 special forms of the scalar potential
W
:

\displaystyle W_{3}(Z)=\frac{c}{z^{2}}+\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}z^{2}}{8},\quad W_{3}(Z)=\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{2}z^{2},

(2.178)

where
c\geq 0
is a constant. (The remaining values
c<0
would allow fall on the
z=0
plane, where the potential is ill-defined.)
In
[
19
]
it was found that the system can be reduced to a 2D system with Hamiltonian of the form (with a permutation of coordinates)

K(\vec{X},\vec{P})=\frac{1}{2}\left(P_{1}^{2}+P_{2}^{2}\right)+\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{2}Y^{2}+W(Y).

(2.179)

Seen as a 3D system, it has 2 cyclic coordinates
Z,

P_{3}
, and is therefore maximally superintegrable if and only if the 2D system with Hamiltonian (
2.179
) is superintegrable. Note that the Hamiltonian (
2.179
) corresponds to a system without magnetic field, which is a well-studied problem. For superintegrable cases of order at most 3 see
[
23
]
. The quadratic cases are the potentials from eq. (
2.178
), where the second potential can be modified by a translation in
z
and a constant shift of the potential (because
\mu_{0}\neq 0
) to become

W_{3}(Z)=\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{2}z^{2}+cz.

(2.180)

For some higher order systems see
[
19
]
and references therein.

Let us solve the Hamiltonian equations of motion and the stationary Schrödinger equation for the cases in
[
17
]
, i.e. with scalar potential
W
from eq. (
2.178
). For that we choose the gauge

\vec{A}(\vec{x})=(0,\mu_{0}x,0).

(2.181)

For the first potential in eq. (
2.178
) the following independent second order integral of motion was found

X_{5}=\left(L_{x}^{A}\right)^{2}+\left(L_{y}^{A}\right)^{2}-\mu_{0}z^{2}L_{z}^{A}+\frac{\mu_{0}^{2}}{4}z^{2}(x^{2}+y^{2})+\frac{2c}{z^{2}}(x^{2}+y^{2}).

(2.182)

The equations of motion in the gauge (
2.181
) read
[
17
]

\displaystyle\dot{x}=p_{x},\quad\dot{y}=p_{y}+\mu_{0}x,\quad\dot{z}=p_{z},

(2.183)

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{x}=-\mu_{0}(p_{y}+\mu_{0}x),\quad\dot{p}_{y}=0,\quad\dot{p}_{z}=-\frac{1}{4}\left(\mu_{0}^{2}z-\frac{8c}{z^{3}}\right).

(2.184)

The explicit trajectories are

\displaystyle x(t)=

\displaystyle\frac{p_{x}^{0}}{\mu_{0}}\sin(\mu_{0}t)+\left(\frac{p_{y}^{0}}{\mu_{0}}+x_{0}\right)\cos(\mu_{0}t)-\frac{p_{y}^{0}}{\mu_{0}},

(2.185)

\displaystyle y(t)=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{p_{y}^{0}}{\mu_{0}}+x_{0}\right)\sin(\mu_{0}t)-\frac{p_{x}^{0}}{\mu_{0}}\cos(\mu_{0}t)+\frac{p_{x}^{0}}{\mu_{0}}+y_{0},

(2.186)

\displaystyle z(t)=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{0}^{2}z_{0}^{4}(\cos(\mu_{0}t)+1)+4\mu_{0}p_{z}^{0}z_{0}^{3}\sin(\mu_{0}t)+4\left((p_{Z}^{0})^{2}z_{0}^{2}+2c\right)(1-\cos(\mu_{0}t))}}{\sqrt{2}\mu_{0}z_{0}}

(2.187)

for
c>0
, otherwise (
c=0
) the potential is the harmonic oscillator and we get (
x(t)
and
y(t)
remain the same)

z(t)=2\left(\frac{p_{z}^{0}}{\mu_{0}}\sin\left(\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}t\right)+\frac{z_{0}}{2}\cos\left(\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}t\right)\right).

(2.188)

In both cases the trajectories are periodic and bounded (in the
c>0
case if
z_{0}\neq 0
, so that the solution is well defined).

For the second potential in eq. (
2.178
), the fifth independent integral reads
[
17
]

X_{5}=p_{x}^{A}L_{y}^{A}-p_{y}^{A}L_{x}^{A}-\mu_{0}zL_{z}^{A}.

(2.189)

The equations of motion in the gauge (
2.181
) read

\displaystyle\dot{x}=p_{x},\quad\dot{y}=p_{y}+\mu_{0}x,\quad\dot{z}=p_{,}

(2.190)

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{x}=-\mu_{0}(p_{y}+\mu_{0}x),\quad\dot{p}_{y}=0,\quad\dot{p}_{z}=-\mu_{0}^{2}z.

(2.191)

The explicit trajectories are

\displaystyle x(t)=

\displaystyle{}\frac{p_{y}^{0}}{\mu_{0}}\sin(\mu_{0}t)+\left(\frac{p_{y}^{0}}{\mu_{0}}+x_{0}\right)\cos(\mu_{0}t)-\frac{p_{y}^{0}}{\mu_{0}},

(2.192)

\displaystyle y(t)=

\displaystyle{}\left(\frac{p_{y}^{0}}{\mu_{0}}+x_{0}\right)\sin(\mu_{0}t)-\frac{p_{y}^{0}}{\mu_{0}}\cos(\mu_{0}t)+\frac{p_{x}^{0}}{\mu_{0}}+y_{0},

(2.193)

\displaystyle z(t)=

\displaystyle{}\frac{p_{z}^{0}}{\mu_{0}}\sin\left(\mu_{0}t\right)+z_{0}\cos\left(\mu_{0}t\right).

(2.194)

Concerning the Schrödinger equation, we first note that it is separable even in the general minimally superintegrable case with
W_{3}(Z)
. For that we choose the gauge (
2.181
), use the commutation of integrals
Y_{2},

X_{2}
and consider the wave function
\psi(\vec{x})
to be their (generalized) eigenfunction

Y_{2}\psi(\vec{x})=\lambda_{2}\psi(\vec{x}),\quad X_{2}\psi(\vec{x})=\lambda_{3}\psi(\vec{x}).

(2.195)

We write
\psi(\vec{x})
as

\psi(\vec{x})=f(x)\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\hbar}\lambda_{2}y\right)g(z),

(2.196)

where
g(z)
is (generalized) eigenfunction of
X_{2}
satisfying

-\hbar^{2}g^{\prime\prime}(z)+2W_{3}(z)g(z)=\lambda_{3}g(z)

(2.197)

(to be determined for the specific
W_{3}(z)
) and
f(x)
satisfies the reduced Schrödinger equation

\hbar^{2}f^{\prime\prime}(x)=\left[\left(\mu_{0}x+\lambda_{2}\right)^{2}+\lambda_{3}-2E\right]f(x).

(2.198)

The last equation is again the equation for 1D harmonic oscillator with energy
E-\frac{\lambda_{3}}{2}
, angular frequency
\omega=\mu_{0}
with the centre of force
x=-\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\mu_{0}}
. The corresponding eigenfunctions are

f_{n}(x)=K_{n}H_{n}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mu_{0}}{\hbar}}\left(x+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\mu_{0}}\right)\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\mu_{0}}{\hbar}\left(x+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\mu_{0}}\right)^{2}\right),

(2.199)

with
K_{n}
the normalization constant.

The spectrum of the Hamiltonian,

E=\hbar\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}(2n+1)+\frac{\lambda_{3}}{2},

(2.200)

is continuous regardless of
\lambda_{3}
, which must be determined from eq. (
2.197
), because
\psi(\vec{x})
from eq. (
2.196
) is in not normalizable.

The solution to eq. (
2.197
) with the first potential in eq. (
2.178
) obtained by Maple™ is in terms of Whittaker functions
M_{\mu,\nu}(z),

W_{\mu,\nu}(z)

[
1
, Chapter 13]
,
[
7
, Section 13.14]

g(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{z}}\left[c_{1}M_{\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\mu_{0}\hbar},\frac{\sqrt{\hbar^{2}+8c}}{4\hbar}}\left(\frac{\mu_{0}z^{2}}{2\hbar}\right)+c_{2}W_{\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\mu_{0}\hbar},\frac{\sqrt{h^{2}+8c}}{4\hbar}}\left(\frac{\mu_{0}z^{2}}{2\hbar}\right)\right]

(2.201)

and if the first arguments in them are not negative integers or 0, it can be rewritten in terms of confluent hypergeometric function
{}_{1}F_{1}(a,b,z)
(Kummer
M(a,b,z)
)
[
1
, Chapter 13]
,
[
7
, Section 13.2]
as follows

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} g(z)&=\tilde{c}_{1}\exp\left(-\frac{\mu_{0}z^{2}}{4\hbar}\right)z^{1+\frac{a}{2\hbar}}{}_{1}F_{1}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{4\lambda-a}{4\mu_{0}\hbar},1+\frac{a}{2\hbar},\frac{\mu_{0}z^{2}}{2\hbar}\right)+\\
&+\tilde{c}_{2}\exp\left(-\frac{\mu_{0}z^{2}}{4\hbar}\right)z^{1-\frac{a}{2\hbar}}{}_{1}F_{1}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{4\lambda+a}{4\mu_{0}\hbar},1-\frac{a}{2\hbar},\frac{\mu_{0}z^{2}}{2\hbar}\right),\end{aligned}

(2.202)

where
a=\sqrt{\hbar^{2}+8c}.

(For
c=0
reduces to harmonic oscillator with
\omega=\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}
.)

The solution of eq. (
2.197
) with the second potential in eq. (
2.178
) is again the harmonic oscillator, this time without shift, whose solutions are

g_{n}(z)=K_{n}H_{n}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\mu_{0}}{\hbar}}z\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\mu_{0}}{2\hbar}z^{2}\right),

(2.203)

where
K_{n}
is the normalization constant, so for the second potential in eq. (
2.178
) the spectrum of the Hamiltonian is

E=\hbar\mu_{0}(2n+1).

(2.204)

It is nevertheless continuous, because
\psi(\vec{x})
from eq. (
2.196
) is not normalizable.

Let us see if the systems separate in the cylindrical coordinates as well.

\begin{split}\hat{H}={}&-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2}\left(\frac{1}{r}\partial_{r}(r\partial_{r})+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\partial_{\phi\phi}+\partial_{ZZ}\right)-\\
&-\mathrm{i}\hbar\rho_{1}r\cos(\phi)\left(\sin(\phi)\partial_{r}+\frac{\cos(\phi)}{r}\partial_{\phi}\right)+\frac{\rho_{1}}{2}r^{2}(\cos(\phi))^{2}+W(Z).\end{split}

(2.205)

It is clear that we can separate the
Z
coordinate from the
r
and
\phi
, the equation for the
z
coordinate is (
2.197
). The remaining part of the separation is the same as in the case with constant magnetic field, see eq. (
2.48
), where we have shown that it is impossible to separate
r
and
\phi
in this gauge. However, it can be separated in another choice of gauge, see the text under eq. (
2.49
).

Stationary Hamilton-Jacobi equation in the Cartesian coordinates

\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial x}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial y}+\mu_{0}x\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial z}\right)^{2}\right]+W_{3}(z)=E

(2.206)

separates because the coordinate
y
is cyclic. After the
z
-dependence is solved,
\partial_{z}U=v^{\prime}(z)=\sqrt{2(\lambda_{3}-W_{3}(z))}
, the
x
-dependence is a quadrature of the form (
2.60
), and thus the result is

\begin{split}u(x)={}&\frac{E-\lambda_{3}}{\mu_{0}}\arctan\left(\frac{\mu_{0}x+p_{y}}{\sqrt{-\mu_{0}^{2}x^{2}-2\mu_{0}p_{y}x-p_{y}^{2}+2E-2\lambda_{3}}}\right)+\\
&+\frac{(\mu_{0}x+p_{y})}{2\mu_{0}}\sqrt{-\mu_{0}^{2}x^{2}-2\mu_{0}p_{y}x-p_{y}^{2}+2E-2\lambda_{3}}+C_{1}\end{split}

(2.207)

The solution for the first potential in (
2.178
) is of the type in eq. (
2.64
) and reads (assuming positive argument in the square root)

\begin{split}v(z)={}&-\frac{\sqrt{-\mu_{0}z^{4}+8\lambda_{3}z^{2}-8c}}{4}-\frac{\sqrt{2c}}{2}\arctan\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}\lambda_{3}+\sqrt{8}c}{\sqrt{c}\sqrt{-\mu_{0}z^{4}+8\lambda_{3}z^{2}-8c}}\right)-\\
&-\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\sqrt{-\mu_{0}}}\ln\left(\frac{4\lambda_{3}-\mu_{0}z^{2}}{\sqrt{-\mu_{0}}}+\sqrt{-\mu_{0}z^{4}+8\lambda_{3}z^{2}-8c}\right).\end{split}

(2.208)

For the second potential in (
2.178
) it reads (with the same assumption)

\begin{split}v(z)&=\frac{z\sqrt{-\mu_{0}z^{2}+2\lambda_{3}}}{2}+\frac{\lambda_{3}}{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}}\arctan\left(\frac{\sqrt{\mu_{0}}z}{\sqrt{-\mu_{0}z^{2}+2\lambda_{3}}}\right).\end{split}

(2.209)

If
\mu_{0}<0
, we can write the second term in terms of argtanh and
|c|
.

We try the same thing in the cylindrical coordinates as well. We use the other gauge (
2.49
) with
\mu_{0}=-\rho_{1}
, so the equation reads

\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial r}\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial\phi}+\frac{\mu_{0}r^{2}}{2}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\partial U}{\partial Z}\right)^{2}\right]+W_{3}(Z)=E.

(2.210)

Because
z\equiv Z
, we can separate it as in the Cartesian coordinates with the same results. Our choice of gauge ensures
\tilde{X}_{1}=p_{\phi}
(
\phi
is cyclic), so the
\phi
-dependent part of
U
reads
p_{\phi}\phi
. The
r
dependence is determined form

\frac{w^{\prime}(r)^{2}}{2}+\frac{(\mu_{0}r^{2}+2p_{\phi})^{2}}{8r^{2}}+\lambda_{3}=E.

(2.211)

The solution is of the same type as in (
2.208
) with different constants.

III.

k_{6}=0
,
\tau(\phi)\neq 0
. Assuming that
k_{1},

k_{2}
do not both vanish, we obtain

\tau(\phi)=\frac{\tau_{1}}{(k_{2}\cos(\phi)-k_{1}\sin(\phi))^{2}}.

(2.212)

We will use the form of the scalar potential from
[
11
]

\displaystyle W(r,\phi,Z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4r^{2}}T^{\prime\prime}(\phi)M(Z)-\frac{1}{4}T(\phi)M^{\prime\prime}(Z)-\frac{r^{2}}{8}M^{\prime}(Z)^{2}-\frac{1}{8r^{4}}T^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}+

\displaystyle+W_{1}(r)-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\left[\frac{C_{1}}{8}\left(T(\phi)\right)^{2}-w_{0}T(\phi)\right]-\frac{C_{2}}{8}\left(M(Z)\right)^{2}+w_{0}M(Z),

(2.213)

where
M^{\prime}(Z)=\mu(Z)
and
T^{\prime}(\phi)=\tau(\phi)
, which in our case means (assuming
k_{1}\neq 0
)

M(Z)=\mu_{0}Z+m_{0},\quad T(\phi)=-\frac{\tau_{1}}{k_{1}(k_{1}\tan(\phi)-k_{2})}+t_{0}.

(2.214)

Inserting the scalar potential (
III.
) into the constraint (
1.29
)

\frac{1}{r}[(k_{1}\cos(\phi)+k_{2}\sin(\phi))r\partial_{r}W-(k_{2}\cos(\phi)-k_{1}\sin(\phi)-k_{6}r)\partial_{\phi}W+k_{3}r\partial_{Z}W]=0.

(2.215)

and differentiating with respect to
Z
, we obtain

\begin{split}\mu_{0}[C_{2}k_{3}r^{3}((k_{2}^{3}-3k_{1}^{2}k_{2})\cos(\phi)^{3}+k_{1}(k_{1}^{2}-3k_{2}^{2})\cos(\phi)^{2}\sin(\phi)+\\
+3k_{2}k_{1}^{2}\cos(\phi)-k_{1}^{3}\sin(\phi))+2\tau_{1}(k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2})]=0\end{split}

(2.216)

To satisfy the equation for all
\phi
, each term must vanish on its own. However, that is not the case for the last term: the assumptions of our case are
\tau_{1}\neq 0
and
\mu_{0}\neq 0
, and
k_{1},

k_{2}
must be real, for otherwise we would have complex magnetic field

B^{\phi}(r,\phi,Z)=\frac{\tau(\phi)}{r^{3}}=\frac{\tau_{1}}{r^{3}(k_{2}\cos(\phi)-k_{1}\sin(\phi))^{2}}.

(2.217)

If
k_{1}=0
, the function
T(\phi)
changes to

T(\phi)=\tau_{1}\frac{\tan(\phi)}{k_{2}^{2}}+t_{0}

(2.218)

and the differentiated eq. (
2.215
) reads

\mu_{0}(C_{2}k_{2}k_{3}\mu_{0}r^{3}\cos(\phi)^{3}+2\tau_{1})=0,

(2.219)

which has the same consequences. Because the potential from eq. (
III.
) cannot be (under our assumptions) constant, see the first and the last term on the first line, we conclude that the considered subcase
k_{6}=0
,
\tau(\phi)\neq 0
does not have consistent scalar potential
W
.

2.1.6
Case
\mathrm{rank}(M)=1
subcase 3a 1b)

The assumptions of this case are

\mu(Z)\neq 0,\quad\rho(r)\neq 0,\quad\psi(\phi)=0,\quad\sigma(r)=0,\quad\tau(\phi)=0,

(2.220)

which means

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} W&=W_{1}(r)-\frac{r^{2}}{8}\mu(Z)^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\rho(r)\mu(Z)+W_{3}(Z),\\
B^{r}&=-\frac{r^{2}}{2}\mu^{\prime}(Z),\quad B^{\phi}=0,\quad B^{Z}=r\mu(Z)-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{\prime}(r).\end{aligned}

(2.221)

Recalling eq. (
2.24
), equation (
2.4
) implies
\mu(Z)=\mu_{0}
and subsequently from eq. (
2.3
) follows
\rho(r)=\rho_{1}r^{2}+\rho_{0}
with
\rho_{1}\neq\mu_{0}
(otherwise the magnetic field vanishes). Due to the last inequality and the form of
B^{Z}
in eq. (
2.221
) we can set
\rho_{1}=0
by redefinition of
\mu_{0}
, so we obtain the following two superintegrable systems with constant magnetic field:

The magnetic field is the same in both cases, namely

B^{r}(r,\phi,Z)=0,\quad B^{\phi}(r,\phi,Z)=0,\quad B^{Z}(r,\phi,Z)=r\mu_{0},

(2.222)

which in the Cartesian coordinates reads

B^{x}(x,y,z)=0,\quad B^{y}(x,y,z)=0,\quad B^{z}(x,y,z)=\mu_{0}.

(2.223)

The systems are distinguished by their scalar potentials, which are determined from equation

(k_{2}\sin(\phi)+k_{1}\cos(\phi))\partial_{r}W+k_{3}\partial_{Z}W=0.

(2.224)

Due to the form of the potential eq. (
2.221
) we have 2 possibilities with additional integrals of motion: the constant scalar potential
W=W_{0}
with 4 first order integrals from eq. (
2.34
) (with
\rho_{1}=-\mu_{0}
) and

W=W_{3}(Z)
with
k_{3}=0
, i.e. with the integrals
Y_{1},Y_{2},\tilde{X}_{1}
from eq. (
2.34
) and the not-reduced integral

X_{2}=\left(p_{Z}^{A}\right)^{2}+2W_{3}(Z).

(2.225)

(The other solutions of eq. (
2.224
)
W=W(r)
and
W=W(r,Z)
admit no additional integrals, only one or both cylindrical integrals reduce to the first order integrals.)

The system with constant potential is well known, see e.g.
[
15
]
,
[
20
]
and Subsection
2.1.2
. It is in fact maximally superintegrable with the integral from eq. (
2.36
) (with
\rho_{1}=-\mu_{0}
). The second system was considered in
[
17
]
. For more details see the text around eq. (
2.178
), where the same system was considered (with
\rho_{1}=-\mu_{0}
).

2.1.7
Case
\mathrm{rank}(M)=1
subcase 3a 2)

The assumptions of this system are

\tau(\phi)\neq 0,\quad\psi(\phi)=0,\quad\mu(Z)=0,\quad\rho(r)=0

(2.226)

and lead to the following magnetic field
B
and scalar potential
W

\displaystyle\begin{split}W&=W_{1}(r)-\frac{1}{8r^{4}}\tau(\phi)^{2}+\frac{1}{4r^{2}}\tau(\phi)\sigma(r)+\frac{1}{r^{2}}W_{2}(\phi),\\
B^{r}&=\frac{1}{2r^{2}}\tau^{\prime}(\phi),\quad B^{\phi}=\frac{1}{r^{3}}\tau(\phi)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{\prime}(r),\quad B^{Z}=0.\end{split}

(2.227)

First, we recall eq. (
2.24
), i.e.
k_{4}=k_{5}=0
. We also assume that
k_{1}
,
k_{2}
do not both vanish, because otherwise we would have the two cylindrical integrals
X_{1},X_{2}
only. Equations (
2.4
) and (
2.5
) differentiated twice with respect to
r
give us the form of
\sigma(r)
:

\sigma(r)=\frac{\sigma_{1}r+\sigma_{2}}{r^{2}}+\sigma_{0}.

(2.228)

Inserting this result, eq. (
2.4
) once differentiated with respect to
r

\sigma_{1}(k_{2}\sin(\phi)+k_{1}\cos(\phi))+k_{6}\tau^{\prime}(\phi)=0

(2.229)

leads to splitting of our considerations into 2 cases,
k_{6}=0
and
k_{6}\neq 0
.

I.

k_{6}=0
: In this case eq. (
2.229
) implies
\sigma_{1}=0
. Subsequent solving of eq. (
2.4
) and eq. (
2.5
) for
\tau(\phi)
yields

\tau(\phi)=\sigma_{2}-\frac{\tau_{1}}{(k_{1}\sin(\phi)-k_{2}\cos(\phi))^{2}}.

(2.230)

The corresponding magnetic field reads

\ B^{r}=\frac{\tau_{1}(k_{1}\cos(\phi)+k_{2}\sin(\phi))}{r^{2}(k_{1}\sin(\phi)-k_{2}\cos(\phi))^{3}},\quad B^{\phi}=\frac{-\tau_{1}}{r^{3}(k_{1}\sin(\phi)-k_{2}\cos(\phi))^{2}},\quad B^{Z}=0.

(2.231)

Without loss on generality, we simplify the following analysis using the formula
k_{1}\sin(\phi)-k_{2}\cos(\phi)=\sqrt{k_{1}^{2}+k_{2}^{2}}\cos(\phi+\phi_{0})
and choosing the coordinates (rotating the system) so that
\phi_{0}=\frac{3}{2}\pi
, i.e.
k_{1}=0
, and redefine
\tau_{1}
so that
k_{2}=1
.

Writing the simplified magnetic field in the Cartesian coordinates, we get

B^{x}=0,\quad B^{y}=-\frac{\tau_{1}}{x^{3}},\quad B^{z}=0.

(2.232)

The compatibility equations are solved, and we continue with eq. (
1.29
) to obtain the form of the scalar potential
W
. The solution in this case is

W(r,\phi)=W(r\cos(\phi)).

(2.233)

Comparing this result with the form of the scalar potential in eq. (
2.227
), the final form of
W
in the cylindrical coordinates is

W=\frac{W_{0}+\sigma_{0}\tau_{1}}{4r^{2}(\cos(\phi))^{2}}-\frac{\tau_{1}^{2}}{8r^{4}(\cos(\phi))^{4}}

(2.234)

and in the Cartesian coordinates reads

W=\frac{W_{0}+\sigma_{0}\tau_{1}}{4x^{2}}-\frac{\tau_{1}^{2}}{8x^{4}}.

(2.235)

The corresponding integrals of motion are (in the Cartesian coordinates)

\tilde{X}_{2}=p_{z}^{A}+\frac{\tau_{1}}{x^{2}},\quad Y_{1}=p_{y}^{A}

(2.236)

and the not-reduced cylindrical integral

X_{1}=\left(p_{\phi}^{A}\right)^{2}+\sigma_{2}-\frac{\tau_{1}}{(\cos(\phi))^{2}}=(L_{z}^{A})^{2}+\sigma_{2}-\frac{\tau_{1}}{x^{2}}.

(2.237)

This system was already analysed in Subsection
2.1.4
subcase
IV.
, where we had different definitions of constants and a rotated coordinate system.

II.

k_{6}\neq 0
. This time the eq. (
2.229
) can be solved for
\tau(\phi)
:

\tau(\phi)=-\frac{\sigma_{1}(k_{1}\sin(\phi)-k_{2}\cos(\phi))}{k_{6}}+\tau_{0}.

(2.238)

Inserting this into eq. (
2.4
), we get

18k_{1}k_{2}\sigma_{1}\cos(\phi)^{2}-[9\sigma_{1}(k_{1}^{2}-k_{2}^{2})\sin(\phi)-6k_{1}k_{6}(\tau_{0}-\sigma_{2})]\cos(\phi)+\\
+k_{2}[(6k_{6}(\tau_{0}-\sigma_{2})\sin(\phi))-9k_{1}\sigma_{1}]=0.

(2.239)

This must be satisfied for all
\phi
, which implies either
k_{1}=k_{2}=0
, i.e. no additional integrals, or
\tau_{0}=\sigma_{2}
and
\sigma_{1}=0
, which means zero magnetic field. This subcase, therefore, does not contain anything interesting for us.

2.1.8
Case
\mathrm{rank}(M)=1
subcase 3a 3)

The assumptions of the system

\rho(r)\neq 0,\quad\psi(\phi)=0,\quad\mu(Z)=0,\quad\tau(\phi)=0,\quad\sigma(r)=0

(2.240)

lead to the following magnetic field
B
and scalar potential
W

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=W_{1}(r)+W_{3}(Z),\quad B^{r}=0,\quad B^{\phi}=0,\quad B^{Z}=-\frac{1}{2}\rho^{\prime}(r).

(2.241)

Taking eq. (
2.24
) into account, the last remaining equation (
2.3
), namely

r^{2}(\rho^{\prime}(r)-r\rho^{\prime\prime}(r))(k_{2}\sin(\phi)+k_{1}\cos(\phi))=0,

(2.242)

implies
\rho(r)=\rho_{1}r^{2}+\rho_{0}
. (We exclude
k_{1}=k_{2}=0
, which leads to no additional integral to the 2 cylindrical ones.)

We have, therefore, again the system with constant magnetic field

B^{r}=0,\quad B^{\phi}=0,\quad B^{Z}=-\rho_{1}r,

(2.243)

which in the Cartesian coordinates reads

B^{x}=0,\quad B^{y}=0,\quad B^{z}=-\rho_{1}.

(2.244)

We next determine the scalar potential
W
. The zeroth order equation (
1.29
) with the potential from eq. (
2.241
) reduces to

(k_{2}\sin(\phi)+k_{1}\cos(\phi))W_{1}^{\prime}(r)+k_{3}W_{3}^{\prime}(Z)=0.

(2.245)

Assumption of additional integrals, i.e. at least one of the constants
k_{1},

k_{2}
non-vanishing, implies
W_{1}(r)=w_{0}
, which we can absorb into
W_{3}(Z)
by redefinition.

Therefore, we have two possible subcases, both with the constant magnetic field eq. (
2.243
):
The first subcase has constant scalar potential,

W=W_{0},

(2.246)

with 4 first order integrals from eq. (
2.34
),
and the second subcase has the potential

W=W_{3}(Z),

(2.247)

the first order integrals
Y_{1},

Y_{2},

\tilde{X}_{1}
from eq. (
2.34
) and the not-reduced cylindrical integral

X_{2}=\left(p_{Z}^{A}\right)^{2}+2W_{3}(Z).

(2.248)

The first system was considered in
[
15
]
,
[
20
]
, see the text around eq. (
2.34
), and the second in
[
17
]
. For more details on the latter system see the text around eq. (
2.178
).

2.1.9
Case
\mathrm{rank}(M)=1
subcase 3a 4)

This system is defined by

\mu(Z)=0,\quad\tau(\phi)=0,\quad\rho(r)=0,\quad\sigma(r)\neq 0,

(2.249)

so the magnetic field
B
and scalar potential
W
read

\displaystyle\begin{split}W=W_{1}(r)+\frac{1}{r^{2}}W_{2}(\phi),\quad B^{r}=0,\quad B^{\phi}=\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{\prime}(r),\quad B^{Z}=0.\end{split}

(2.250)

In this case eq. (
2.5
) reads

(-k_{2}\cos(\phi)+k_{1}\sin(\phi))r^{3}\sigma^{\prime}(r)=0,

(2.251)

which is solved either by
\sigma(r)=\sigma_{0}
, which implies vanishing magnetic field
B
, or both
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
must vanish, i.e. no additional integral to the cylindrical ones.

2.1.10
Case
\mathrm{rank}(M)=1
subcase 3b)

This case assumes

\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\neq 0,\quad\mu(Z)=0,\quad\tau(\phi)=0,\quad\sigma(r)=0

(2.252)

leading to the magnetic field
B
and potential
W
of the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}W(r,\phi,Z)&=W_{12}(r,\phi)+W_{3}(Z),\\
B^{r}&=0,\quad B^{\phi}=0,\quad B^{Z}=-\frac{1}{2r^{2}}\left(\rho^{\prime}(r)r^{2}+\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)+\psi(\phi)\right)\end{split}

(2.253)

and additional equations constraining
W

\displaystyle r\psi^{\prime}(\phi)W_{r}+\left(r\rho(r)-\psi(\phi)\right)W_{\phi}+\frac{\hbar^{2}(\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)+\psi^{\prime}(\phi))}{4r^{3}}=0,

\displaystyle\psi^{\prime}(\phi)\left(r^{3}\rho^{\prime\prime}(r)-r^{2}\rho^{\prime}(r)+r\rho(r)-3\psi^{\prime\prime}(\phi)-4\psi(\phi)\right)+

(2.254)

\displaystyle+\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)\left(r\rho(r)-\psi(\phi)\right)-4r^{5}W_{r\phi}-8r^{4}W_{\phi}=0.

This is very similar to the considerations in Subsection
2.1.3
, the only important difference being the form of the potential
W,
which admits dependence on
Z
. Let us therefore continue in the same manner and focus on the differences.

Recalling eq. (
2.24
) and differentiating eq. (
2.3
) twice with respect to
r
, we get eq. (
2.67
) for
\rho(r)
with solution

\rho(r)=\rho_{4}r^{2}+\rho_{2}\ln(r)+\frac{\rho_{3}}{r}+\rho_{1}.

(2.255)

Inserting it into eq. (
2.3
) differentiated only once we get

k_{6}(\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}(\phi)+\psi^{\prime}(\phi))+2\rho_{2}(k_{4}\sin(\phi)+k_{5}\cos(\phi))=0,

(2.256)

which splits the considerations into 2 subcases:
k_{6}\neq 0
and
k_{6}=0
.

I.

k_{6}\neq 0
: Solving the compatibility equations in the same way as in Subsection
2.1.3
subcase
I.
, we get

\psi(\phi)=-\psi_{2}\cos(\phi)+\psi_{1}\sin(\phi)+\rho_{1},\quad\rho(r)=\rho_{2}r^{2}+\frac{\rho_{1}}{r}+\rho_{0}.

(2.257)

So we obtained the constant magnetic field

B^{r}=0,\quad B^{\phi}=0,\quad B^{Z}=-\rho_{2}r.

(2.258)

Translating this into the Cartesian coordinates using eq. (
1.2
), it reads

B^{x}=0,\quad B^{y}=0,\quad B^{z}=-\rho_{2}.

(2.259)

We note that
\psi(\phi)
from eq. (
2.257
) implies vanishing of the quantum correction, so the quantum version of the system is identical to the classical version.

Having solved the compatibility conditions, we solve the original equations (
1.2
) to obtain

\displaystyle m(r,\phi,Z)

\displaystyle=\rho_{2}\left(k_{2}r\cos(\phi)-k_{1}r\sin(\phi)-\frac{k_{6}r^{2}}{2}\right).

(2.260)

Equation (
1.29
) reads

(k_{1}\cos(\phi)+k_{2}\sin(\phi))r\partial_{r}W_{12}-(k_{2}\cos(\phi)-k_{1}\sin(\phi)+k_{6}r)\partial_{\phi}W_{12}+k_{3}r\partial_{Z}W_{3}=0.

(2.261)

As in the previous case, we can differentiate the equation to get
k_{3}W_{3}^{\prime\prime}(Z)=0
, so we further split the considerations.

I.a)

k_{3}=0
: In this subcase we have no constraint on
W_{3}(Z)
and the remaining equations determining
W_{12}
are the same as in Subsection
2.1.3
subcase
I.
, implying
W_{12}(r,\phi)=W_{0}
, therefore (absorbing the constant into
W_{3}(Z)
)

W(r,\phi,Z)=W_{3}(Z).

(2.262)

The corresponding integrals of motion are the 3 first order integrals

Y_{1}=p_{x}^{A}-\rho_{2}y,\quad Y_{2}=p_{y}^{A}+\rho_{2}x,\quad\tilde{X}_{1}=L_{z}^{A}+\frac{\rho_{2}(x^{2}+y^{2})}{2}

(2.263)

and the non-reduced cylindrical integral

X_{2}=\left(p_{Z}^{A}\right)^{2}+2W_{3}(Z).

(2.264)

The system was considered in
[
17
]
. For more details see the text around eq. (
2.178
), where the same system was considered (with
\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}
).

I.b)

k_{3}\neq 0
: Here we get

W_{3}(Z)=aZ+b

(2.265)

so eq. (
2.261
) reads

(k_{1}\cos(\phi)+k_{2}\sin(\phi))r\partial_{r}W_{12}-(k_{2}\cos(\phi)-k_{1}\sin(\phi)-k_{6}r)\partial_{\phi}W_{12}+k_{3}ar=0.

(2.266)

The other equation which we shall solve is the first from eq. (
2.1.10
).
We assume
a\neq 0
, because if
a=0
we are back in Subsection
2.1.3
subcase
I.
with constant scalar potential
W=W_{0}.

Let us analyse the system of equations from the point of view of linear algebra. The extended matrix of the system reads

\begin{pmatrix}r(\psi_{2}\sin(\phi)+\psi_{1}\cos(\phi))&r(\rho_{2}r^{2}+\rho_{0})+\psi_{2}\cos(\phi)-\psi_{1}\sin(\phi)&0\\
r(k_{1}\cos(\phi)+k_{2}\sin(\phi))&-(k_{2}\cos(\phi)-k_{1}\sin(\phi)-k_{6}r)&-k_{3}ar\\
\end{pmatrix}

(2.267)

The left-hand side
2\times 2
minor

\begin{split}-2(k_{1}\psi_{2}+k_{2}\psi_{1})(\cos(\phi))^{2}+k_{1}\psi_{2}+k_{2}\psi_{1}+2(k_{1}\psi_{1}-k_{2}\psi_{2})\sin(\phi)\cos(\phi)-\\
-r[k_{1}(\rho_{2}r^{2}+\rho_{0})-k_{6}\psi_{1}]\cos(\phi)-r[k_{2}(\rho_{2}r^{2}+\rho_{0})-k_{6}\psi_{2}]\sin(\phi)\eqqcolon\text{det}A\end{split}

(2.268)

does not vanish for all
r,

\phi
due to our assumptions
\rho_{2}\neq 0
(non-zero magnetic field),
k_{3}\neq 0
,
k_{6}\neq 0
and at least one of
k_{1},

k_{2}
non-zero. Therefore, the system has the following unique solution

\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{r}W_{12}\\
\partial_{\phi}W_{12}\end{pmatrix}=-\frac{k_{3}a}{\text{det}A}\begin{pmatrix}-[r(\rho_{2}r^{2}+\rho_{0})+\psi_{2}\cos(\phi)-\psi_{1}\sin(\phi)]\\
r(\psi_{2}\sin(\phi)+\psi_{1}\cos(\phi))\end{pmatrix}.

(2.269)

This solution, however, does not satisfy Clairaut compatibility condition
